# ELF-DOS

A FAT16 DOS-like OS for the CDP1802 (Elf/OS-compatible hardware). No local emulator or hardware access exists in the development environment — all hardware testing is done by the user reflashing an SD card and reporting console output back.

## Build

```
make            # kernel.bin / kernel-full.bin
make progs      # all progs/*.asm -> bin/<name> (bare, no extension --
                # mirrors the on-device /bin layout; auto-discovers new files)
make clean
```

Toolchain: `asm02 -L -C -I .. <file>.asm` produces a `.prg`; `link02 -b -be -r -o <out> <files.prg...>` links them (`-r` = short-branch relaxation, see below). Link order for the kernel matters and is fixed in the Makefile: `kernel bpb fat dir path rtc file loader`. Installed at `/opt/elfc/{asm02,link02}` (root-owned; `sudo cp` to update). Source for both lives at `~/projects/Elf/Asm-02` and `~/projects/Elf/Link-02` — **not** `~/projects/Asm-02` (no `Elf/`), which is a stale, older checkout that doesn't match the installed binaries (confirmed 2026-07-10: it's missing `-C`, the case-sensitive-labels flag this project's own build always passes) and should probably be deleted.

To verify a build's symbol table without a full relink: `link02 -b -be -o /tmp/x.bin -s <files.prg...> 2>&1 | grep <symbol>`.

## Architecture

- **Kernel API jump table** at fixed address `$0106` (`include/kernel_api.inc`, `K_*` constants). Each slot is a 3-byte `lbr`. Entries are **append-only** — never reorder or remove — so a program built against an older kernel keeps working after a kernel rebuild. **One deliberate pre-release exception, 2026-07-13**: the whole table was fully renumbered (not just appended to) as part of the shell-owned-search work below, since nothing external depended on the addresses yet; do **not** repeat a full renumber after v1.0 ships — see the jump-table-renumbering gotcha under "Toolchain gotchas" for what it costs when a deployed binary predates one. After touching `kernel/kernel.asm`'s table, verify every slot is still `0xC0` (`lbr`) pointing at the expected target by decoding the built `kernel.bin` directly (see any recent session for the verification script) — don't just trust that the assembler "did the right thing."
- **`kernel_api.inc` is deliberately decoupled from `kernel.inc`** (no `#include` between them). Programs get `K_*` call addresses, `PROG_BASE`/`LOADER_ARGS`, `DIRENT_*`/`ATTR_DIR`, and `KERNEL_HDR_VER` — restated, not shared — so program code never depends on internal kernel structures that could change across updates. Fixed, never-shifting addresses (`PROG_BASE`, `KERNEL_HDR_VER`, etc.) are read directly rather than through a kernel call; only real subroutines get a jump-table slot.
- **Shell has zero built-in commands.** Every command (`VER`, `DIR`, `CD`, `TYPE`, ...) is a standalone `.EXE` in `progs/`, loaded via the shell's own search (below) + the kernel's `prog_run`. New shell functionality should default to a new `.EXE`, not a shell built-in.
- **The shell itself is now `progs/shell.asm`, an ordinary loadable program (`/bin/shell` on-device) — not kernel-resident code. CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-10)**: the user exercised nearly every command through the new shell/loader hand-off (directory create/remove, file create/copy/rename/delete) followed by a clean `fsck`, with no regressions from the kernel-resident-shell era. Previously `kernel/shell.asm` held the whole prompt/read/dispatch loop and `prog_load` did a 6-attempt search (current directory then root, three name variants each: as-typed, `+.EXE`, `+.exe`) using two 132-byte scratch buffers — a meaningful chunk of the kernel's own tight budget. Moved out entirely as the foundation for three planned 1.0 features (batch file processing, I/O redirection, argument processing) that are all naturally shell-level concerns, cheaper to build once the shell is reloadable rather than permanently kernel-resident. Net effect: `Highest address` dropped from `$3647` to `$3464` (kernel shrank further even after this session's earlier diagnostic cleanup), margins now **2304 bytes before `KERN_LOAD`, 2972 before `PROG_BASE`** — the best they've been all session.
  - **Why the shell can't call the loader itself**: the shell runs *from* `PROG_BASE`, the same fixed address any command it wants to run also loads to. If the shell called `K_PROG_LOAD`/`K_PROG_EXEC` directly, the `call` instruction would push a return address pointing back into the shell's own code at `PROG_BASE` — but loading the child command overwrites exactly that memory before the child's own `rtn` could ever return there safely. Instead, the **kernel** stays the one that calls `prog_load`/`prog_exec`, in a small `run_loop` living in `kernel_init` (`kernel/kernel.asm`) that never leaves kernel memory: load+run `/bin/shell` (which resolves one command line and returns), then load+run whatever it resolved, repeat. This works because `prog_exec`'s own `call PROG_BASE+6` already pushes a return address in kernel memory, not at `PROG_BASE` — the same mechanism that's always made the kernel-to-program call/return dance safe, now reused for program-to-program (shell-to-command) hand-off too.
  - **Hand-off protocol**: two new fixed addresses, defined the same way `PROG_BASE`/`LOADER_ARGS` already were (restated in both `include/kernel.inc` and `include/kernel_api.inc`, not normal linked symbols, since `progs/shell.asm` is assembled completely separately from the kernel and needs an address stable across kernel rebuilds): `RUN_TAIL_PTR = PROG_BASE-6` (a pointer into `LINE_BUF`, which is now *also* restated in `kernel_api.inc` — it used to be kernel-only since only kernel code touched it, but the shell program now needs direct read/write access too) and `RUN_PATH = PROG_BASE-70` (a 64-byte buffer, `RUN_PATH_LEN`, for the resolved path). Each round, `shell.exe` prompts, reads a line via `K_INPUTL` into `LINE_BUF`, tokenizes the name and tail exactly as the old kernel-side loop did, resolves the name (a bare name → `"/bin/"` + name; a name containing `/` → used as-is, loaded directly per the user's own instruction — no `.EXE`/`.exe` guessing needed anymore, executables are bare-named on-device now), writes the result into `RUN_PATH`/`RUN_TAIL_PTR`, and returns. Both resolution copy loops are bounds-checked against `RUN_PATH_LEN` — an unbounded copy here could silently corrupt `RUN_TAIL_PTR` itself, since it sits directly above `RUN_PATH` in memory.
  - **`prog_load` (`kernel/loader.asm`) shrank drastically** now that resolution is the shell's job: a single `file_open` on the exact path it's given, no case variants, no cur_dir/root fallback — dropping `prog_name_ext`/`prog_name_ext_lc`/`prog_saved_dir`/`ext_suffix`/`ext_suffix_lc` and the whole 6-attempt scaffolding entirely (code size for this one routine roughly halved: 490 → 232 bytes generated). `K_PROG_LOAD`'s calling convention changed (bare name+search → exact resolved path) — confirmed safe via a repo-wide grep showing nothing besides the old kernel-side shell loop ever called it directly. **Opportunistically fixed a known bug while rewriting this routine**: the "available space" calculation (`mem_top - PROG_BASE`) had `PROG_BASE`'s high byte hardcoded as `$20`, a leftover from when `PROG_BASE` was `$2000`, never updated across its later moves to `$3000` then `$4000` — silently overestimating available program RAM by 8KB. Now uses `$40`, the real current value.
  - `"Bad command."` now prints from the kernel's `run_loop`, not from `shell.exe` — the load failure is only discoverable once the kernel actually attempts it, one step after the shell has already returned.
  - `cur_dir` needed no changes — it already lived in kernel memory (unaffected by anything happening at `PROG_BASE`), so directory persistence across commands works exactly as before.
  - Batch-execution state (deferred, not part of this change): the user confirmed flat/non-nested batch files are sufficient for 1.0, so when batch support is built it only needs ~3 kernel-resident bytes (an FCB index + resume position), same precedent as `cur_dir` — no new memory region needed.
  - **Deployment note (updated 2026-07-10)**: `make progs` now builds directly into `bin/` (gitignored), bare-named — `progs/dir.asm` produces `bin/dir`, not `progs/dir.exe` — so `bin/`'s contents mirror the on-device `/bin` layout exactly and the whole directory can be copied wholesale, e.g. `mcopy -i /dev/sdX@@1M bin/* ::BIN/`, rather than per-file with a renamed extension. `/bin` must exist on the card first; this includes the shell itself (`bin/shell` → on-device `/bin/shell`, the exact path the kernel's `run_loop` loads at boot). `link02`'s per-target `.lkb` build-number file is deleted right after each link in the `bin/%` rule so it never lands in `bin/` alongside the real binaries.
- **Shell-owned executable search + jump-table cleanup — CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-14)**, built on top of the shell-as-a-program work above. Two problems drove this: `run_loop`'s own shell reload used a bare `/bin/shell` path, so switching the active drive to one without a copy could strand the system unrecoverably; and every drive needed its own full `/bin` copy for ordinary commands to work while active. Design (refined from the user's own proposal during planning): **the shell does the searching**, not the kernel. `progs/shell.asm` now confirms a command exists via `K_STAT` (trying the active drive's `/bin/`, then falling back to `shell_drive`'s `/bin/` for a bare name) *before* ever handing a path to the kernel, printing its own "File not found." and re-prompting itself if nothing matched — the kernel's `prog_run` (see below) is only ever called with an already-confirmed-to-exist path, so its own failure case shrank to "exists but isn't a valid program."
  - **Shell location, cached as a fixed-size dirent reference, not a path**: `kernel/kernel.asm` gained `shell_drive`/`shell_elba`/`shell_eoff` (the shell's own directory entry's sector LBA + byte offset — the user's own suggestion, avoiding a full directory re-scan on every single command cycle), populated once at boot by new boot-only `K_SHELL_INIT` (calls `_find_dirent` on `"C:/bin/shell"`). `kernel/loader.asm`'s new `prog_run_shell` reads that cached sector directly, validates it (rejects a deleted entry or a directory), and populates the kernel's own permanent `prog_fcb` straight from the raw on-disk fields — falling back to an ordinary path-based `prog_run("C:/bin/shell")` if validation ever fails (e.g. the shell was moved since boot). A real bug was caught and hardware-confirmed-fixed here: `prog_run_shell`'s `fd_table` registration copy-pasted `file_open`'s `fopen_found` pattern (`lda`/`ldn` — *dereferencing* a pointer variable) instead of recognizing that `prog_fcb` **is** the FCB struct itself, not a pointer to one — it needed `ghi`/`glo` on the address, not a dereference. The bug manifested as an infinite `dir`-reload loop on first hardware test; fixed by using `ghi rd`/`glo rd` to take the struct's own address.
  - **`prog_load`+`prog_exec` collapsed into one internal routine, `prog_run`** (`kernel/loader.asm`) — confirmed via repo-wide grep that neither could ever safely be called by a *running* program anyway (loading a second program to `PROG_BASE` overwrites the caller's own code), so both `K_PROG_LOAD` and `K_PROG_EXEC` were dropped from the jump table entirely (along with the already-dead `K_BPB_INIT` stub); `K_SHELL_INIT` and a new `K_GETSHELLDRIVE` (lets the shell query `shell_drive` for its own fallback search) were added. Net table size actually shrank by one slot despite the two additions.
  - **A false alarm, worth remembering as its own lesson**: a second apparent hang ("switch to D:, `dir bin` hangs") survived three diagnostic rounds (numeric register dumps added to `_switch_drive` and `progs/dir.asm`) before the user's own manual isolation found the real cause in minutes — a *stale* `D:/bin/dir` left over from before this session's jump-table renumbering, calling `K_PATH_RESOLVE` at its old (now wrong) address. Not a kernel bug at all. See the jump-table-renumbering entry under "Toolchain gotchas" for the generalized lesson (any table renumbering invalidates deployed binaries on *every* drive, not just the boot drive).
- **Batch scripting — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED (2026-07-14), NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED** (no hardware access from this environment; built while the user was away). Flat/non-nested only, per the user's own earlier confirmation that's sufficient for 1.0. New `kernel/batch.asm`, two new jump-table slots (`K_BATCH_START`=$0170, `K_BATCH_READLINE`=$0173, both appended — not another full renumber, see the jump-table bullet above for why that exception was one-time-only).
  - **Why the state has to live in the kernel, not the shell**: `progs/shell.asm` is reloaded from disk on *every* command cycle (see the shell-as-a-program bullet above) — it has no memory of its own that survives from one batch line to the next. So "which file, how far in" has to live in permanently kernel-resident memory, the same reason `cur_dir`/`cur_drive` do. Design: a dedicated kernel-resident FCB+512-byte I/O buffer (`batch_fcb`/`batch_iobuf`, can't reuse `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` — a batch file stays open across many command cycles while *each* of those commands independently loads+runs its own program through `prog_fcb` at the same time). `FCB_FPOS` inside `batch_fcb` already tracks the resume position for free (it's a fixed, persistent address), and `FCB_FLAGS`' `FCB_F_OPEN` bit doubles as the "is a batch active" signal — no separate resume-position or active-flag fields needed, just the FCB+iobuf themselves plus a 1-byte `batch_handle` (the `fd_table` index) and a 1-byte read scratch.
  - **Contract, deliberately minimal**: `K_BATCH_START(RF=already-resolved, already-confirmed-to-exist path)` → `DF`=0/1 (1 = already active, i.e. nesting rejected, or the open failed). `K_BATCH_READLINE()` → `DF`=0 with the next line in `LINE_BUF` (CR/LF stripped, both bare-LF and CRLF handled with no extra state — CR is just silently skipped), or `DF`=1 (no batch active, or it just hit EOF — auto-closes the FCB and clears `FCB_F_OPEN` in that case, so the *next* call also returns `DF`=1 until a new `K_BATCH_START`). A final line with no trailing newline is still returned once (via an EOF-with-nonzero-count special case) before the following call reports true EOF.
  - **Shell-side integration** (`progs/shell.asm`): `start:` now calls `K_BATCH_READLINE` *first*, before ever prompting — `DF`=0 means echo `<prompt><line>` and use it in place of an interactive `K_INPUTL` read; `DF`=1 falls through to the normal console prompt. Every existing "loop back to `start`" exit (empty line, drive switch, "File not found.") therefore advances to the next batch line automatically, with zero special-casing anywhere else — this was the whole point of checking batch status at the single, shared entry point rather than threading a "batch mode" flag through the rest of the file.
  - **Invocation, deliberately explicit, no implicit search**: a resolved command path ending in `.bat` (case-insensitive, checked via a new `check_batch_ext` helper in `shell.asm`) is started as a batch directly from the `resolved:` label — intercepted *before* `RUN_PATH` would otherwise be handed to the kernel's `run_loop`/`prog_run` (which would just fail with "Invalid program file.", since a batch script isn't an EDF binary). No extension-guessing or DOS-style `.COM`/`.EXE`/`.BAT` search order — matches this project's own established departure from implicit extension resolution (executables are already bare-named on-device, no `.EXE` guessing). The user types the `.bat` name explicitly (bare, resolved the same `/bin/`-relative way any other command name is, or a full path if it contains `/`).
  - **Deliberately out of scope for v1** (matches the roadmap's own "batch scripting" vs. "argument processing" being separate, later items): no `%1`/argument substitution, no `REM`/comments (a blank line is silently skipped, same as interactive), no `ECHO OFF` (every line always echoes), no `GOTO`/labels, no abort/Ctrl-C handling. A bad command *within* a batch prints its error and advances to the next line rather than dropping to an interactive prompt (falls out naturally from the design above, not a special case).
  - **Kernel margin cost, worth watching**: `batch_fcb`+`batch_iobuf` alone are 544 bytes of new kernel-resident data (`FCB_LEN`=32 + `SECTOR_SIZE`=512), plus code for the two new routines. `Highest address` went `$35a8`→`$38b5`, margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` now **1355 bytes / 1280 bytes** (down from 2136/1792) — still healthy, but the two figures to re-check before the next meaningful kernel addition. This is expected/already-planned cost: the roadmap's "one more kernel size-reduction pass, to settle `PROG_BASE`" is explicitly sequenced *after* batch scripting + I/O redirection, not before.
  - **Verification performed**: clean `make`+`make progs` (19/19, 0 errors/warnings), all 36 jump-table slots (34 original + 2 new) byte-decoded from `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr` with sane targets, D-clobber sweep clean on `kernel/batch.asm`/`kernel/kernel.asm`/`progs/shell.asm`, proc/endp balanced (3/3 in `batch.asm`), `krnboot.bin` unchanged at exactly 1536 bytes. **Not hardware-tested** — needs a real round: create a small `.bat` file with a few known-good commands (e.g. `dir`, `cd cfg`, `type env.dat`) via an on-target editor or transferred via `MR`, run it, confirm each line echoes and executes in order and the shell returns to normal interactive prompting at EOF; also worth testing a bad command mid-batch (confirms "advance to next line, don't drop to prompt"), a `.bat` file naming another `.bat` file (confirms the "Nested batch not supported." rejection), and a final line with no trailing newline (confirms the EOF-with-partial-line handling).
  - **A real bug, hardware-found and fixed (2026-07-15): `batch_readline` kept its running `LINE_BUF` write-offset in `R9` across a `call file_read` inside its byte-at-a-time read loop — but `file_read`'s own header documents `R9` (and every register except `D`/`DF`) as clobbered, per the caller-allocated-FCBs rework that landed the same day (`kernel/file.asm`).** This is gotcha #10's exact pattern (a callee's *documented* clobber list being exactly what a distant caller relies on surviving) hitting for a fourth time this project. Symptom: invoking a `.bat` script via its resolved absolute path correctly entered batch mode (`K_BATCH_START` returned success, `file_read` — traced with temporary instrumentation — read the real file bytes correctly and in the correct order, `FCB_FPOS`/`FCB_FSIZE` both advanced sanely) but the shell's `LINE_BUF` ended up holding unrelated garbage (mostly stale leftover text from the user's *previous* interactive command, with one byte disturbed) instead of the batch file's real first line — the classic signature of this bug class: the *data* being read is correct, but the *bookkeeping register* silently isn't. Root-caused by isolating that `batch_readline`'s own diagnostic-free code never touches `R9` (confirmed via `grep -n "r9\|R9"`), which pointed at the one register-surviving-a-call assumption left: `R9` across `file_read`. Fixed by moving the running count out of `R9` entirely into a new kernel-resident byte, `brl_count` (`_batch_data` in `kernel/batch.asm`) — reloaded into a register fresh, immediately before use, at each of the three points that need a 16-bit `add16` (append-byte, null-terminate) rather than trusted to survive any call. **A second, self-inflicted bug was caught by the D-clobber sweep during this same fix, before ever reaching the build**: the very first draft of `brl_count`'s zero-init wrote `ldi 0` / `mov rf, brl_count` / `str rf` — the standard gotcha #4 shape (`mov` clobbers `D` between the `ldi` and the `str`, so the store would have written `brl_count`'s own address low byte instead of 0). Caught by the sweep script and fixed by reordering to `mov rf, brl_count` / `ldi 0` / `str rf`. **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-15)**: user re-ran `/test.bat` (7 lines: `cd`, `type`, drive switch, `cd`, cross-drive `copy`, `cd`, `type`, drive switch back) from two different starting directories — every line executed correctly and in order, echoed with the right prompt, the cross-drive `copy` succeeded, and the shell returned cleanly to an interactive prompt at EOF. This closes out the batch-scripting bug hunt.
- **Program binary format**: `'EDF'` magic + version byte + 2 reserved bytes, code at `PROG_BASE+6` (`PROG_BASE = $3E00`). `mem_base`/`mem_top` are passed via the fixed `LOADER_ARGS = PROG_BASE-4`, since a program may need to consult them at any point (e.g. a heap allocator), unlike the command line which is only needed once at entry (see the argv/argc bullet directly below for how that's passed).
- **Shell-owned argv/argc parsing — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-15, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Replaces the old raw-command-tail-pointer ABI (`RA` = pointer to everything after the program name, DOS-PSP style) with a real `(argc, argv)` pair, matching C's `main(argc, argv)` — `argv[0]` is the program's own invocation name. Phase 1 of a three-phase shell roadmap the user and I agreed on (Plan Mode, 2026-07-15): argv parsing first (this), then I/O redirection built on top of the same tokenizing pass (`>`/`>>`/`<` recognized and stripped from argv, out of scope for this phase), then command history last (needs a raw-character input replacement for `K_INPUTL`, deliberately deferred — needs its own hardware investigation into what this terminal actually sends for arrow keys before design starts). Full plan at `~/.claude/plans/glistening-marinating-moler.md`.
  - **New ABI: `RA` = pointer to the argv table, `RC` = argc (word).** argv is an array of `argc` 16-bit big-endian pointers, each to a null-terminated token living in `LINE_BUF`. **Deliberately register-passed, not a fixed `equ` address any program's own source refers to by name** — a correction made mid-plan at the user's own suggestion, after my first draft proposed a fixed `ARGV_TABLE` constant every program would read via `ARGV_TABLE+N*2`. That repeats exactly the mistake this project has already been burned by twice (`PROG_BASE` moves and the full kernel jump-table renumber both broke already-deployed program binaries that had baked in a fixed address) — `argc`/`argv`, like the old tail pointer, are only needed once at entry, so they belong in registers, not a named constant a program's own compiled code depends on. The argv table's actual backing storage is still a fixed address (`RUN_ARGV_TABLE`, `include/kernel.inc`/`include/kernel_api.inc`) since it has to survive the shell being overwritten at `PROG_BASE` by the child it's about to run (same reason `RUN_PATH` already exists) — but it's kernel/shell-internal plumbing only, exactly like `RUN_PATH` already is: no ordinary program references it by name, only via the `RA` value it's handed. `RUN_ARGC` (a new fixed word, reusing the old `RUN_TAIL_PTR`'s exact address/size) is the matching relay slot for the dynamic argc value. `ARGV_MAX_ARGS = 16`.
  - **Shell-side tokenizer** (`progs/shell.asm`, replaces the old `name_scan` single-space split): one pass over the whole line, splitting on whitespace, with quoting (`"..."`, embedded spaces preserved, quote chars themselves dropped) and backslash-escaping (`\X` → literal `X`, inside or outside quotes — one generic rule covers `\"`, `\\`, and `\<space>` with no per-target special-casing) — both explicitly requested by the user for this first pass, not deferred. Every escape collapses 2 source bytes into 1 output byte and quote characters aren't copied at all, so the result is always shorter-than-or-equal to the source — written back into `LINE_BUF` in place (read cursor `RF`, write cursor `RD`, write always `<=` read), the same in-place-mutation convention `copy.asm`/`ren.asm` already used for their own hand-rolled splitting. An unterminated quote at end-of-line is treated as closed at the NUL (falls out of the state machine for free — the NUL check runs before the in-quotes check, so it doesn't need special-casing). Trailing whitespace after the last token is now correctly excluded (closes a small pre-existing gap — the old convention never trimmed it). No kernel/BIOS calls happen anywhere in the tokenizing loop, so all state (`RF`/`RD`/`RB`=next argv-table slot/`R9.0`=argc/`R8.0`=in-quotes flag) is safely register-resident with no memory stashing needed, unlike most of the rest of this file.
  - **⚠️ PLANNED, NOT YET IMPLEMENTED (noted 2026-07-16): single-quote support, bash-style.** Only `"..."` opens a quoted argument today — a bare `'` is just an ordinary character, confirmed via `ARGS`/`ECHO` hardware tests where `'d e'` tokenized as two separate tokens (`'d` and `e'`) rather than one. The user wants `'` to work as a second, symmetric quote-opener alongside `"` — either character can open a quoted argument, and everything up to the matching close quote is included verbatim, matching bash. Real bash semantics treat the two differently once inside (double-quoted content still processes some escapes/expansions; single-quoted content is 100% literal, not even backslash-escapes work) — this project's current double-quote handling already does backslash-escaping *inside* quotes (see above), so whether single-quoted content should suppress that (true bash behavior) or share the same escape rule `"..."` uses is an open design question to resolve before implementing, not yet decided with the user.
  - **Program migration, 18 of 23 programs** (every program that read the command tail; `mon`/`pwd`/`reboot`/`ver`/`template` don't and were untouched): the 13 single-required-argument programs (`cd`, `del`, `md`, `rd`, `stat`, `type`, `atest`, `hexdump`, `more`, `wbtest`, `wtest`, `sys`, `edlin`) each replace their old `ldn ra`/`lbnz` check with an `argc < 2` check plus one register-relative dereference (`mov rb, ra` / `add16 rb, 2` / `lda`/`phi`/`ldn`/`plo` → `argv[1]`); `dir` is the one optional-argument case (no path argument still means "list the current directory", same as before). `copy`/`ren` are the real payoff: their entire hand-rolled space-scanning/NUL-in-place splitters (45 and 30 lines) collapse to two register-relative reads (`argv[1]`, `argv[2]`), and the old "second argument's trailing whitespace was never trimmed" gap disappears for free since the shell's own tokenizer never included it in the first place. `mr`/`ms`'s hand-rolled `-u`/`-b` flag-prefix scanner simplifies similarly — since the shell's tokenizer already split on whitespace, checking `argv[1]` for an exact `"-u"`/`"-b"` token (2 chars + NUL) no longer needs the old per-character space-boundary check, just a direct comparison; filename is `argv[2]` if a flag was found, else `argv[1]`.
  - **A real bug caught mid-migration, before ever reaching a build**: the first draft of `mr.asm`'s (and, copy-pasted, `ms.asm`'s) migration placed a new `usage:` error-message label directly after the `not_flag:` fallthrough case with no branch between them — meaning the no-flag/default path would fall straight through the mode-setting code into the usage-error message and `rtn` instead of continuing to open the file. Caught by re-reading the control flow immediately after writing it (not by the assembler, which has no way to know this branch was missing), fixed by adding an explicit `lbr have_name_ptr` at the end of the `not_flag:` block.
  - **`dir.asm`'s own entry order matters and was preserved deliberately**: it calls `K_GETCURDIR` as its very first instruction, *before* reading argc/argv — this is safe specifically because `kernel_getcurdir`'s own hardware-confirmed fix (see the `K_GETCURDIR` cross-drive bug entry elsewhere in this file) already push/pop-protects `RA`/`RB`/`RC`/`R7` around its internal `_switch_drive` call, precisely because this program was already known to depend on `RA` surviving that call. Since `RA`/`RC` now carry the argv pointer/argc instead of the old tail pointer, that same existing protection continues to cover the new convention with no changes needed — but it was a real, deliberate check during migration, not an assumption.
  - **Verification**: full clean `make` + `make progs` (23/23 programs, 0 errors/warnings across every one), all 36 jump-table slots decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr` (unchanged — this phase adds no kernel jump-table slots), D-clobber sweep clean on all 21 touched files (`kernel/kernel.asm`, `kernel/loader.asm`, `progs/shell.asm`, and all 18 migrated programs), margins healthy (`Highest address` `$3934`, 1228 bytes before `PROG_BASE`, 768 bytes of sector-rounded boot headroom before `KERN_LOAD`), `krnboot.bin` unchanged at exactly 1536 bytes. The tokenizer's quote/escape state machine — the one genuinely new, non-mechanical piece of this phase — was independently re-implemented as a Python simulation (`shell_tokenizer_sim.py` in scratch) mirroring the exact same character-by-character dispatch order, and run against 11+ worked cases: plain tokens, a quoted token with an embedded space, `\"`/`\\`/`\<space>` escapes both inside and outside quotes, an unterminated trailing quote, a bare `"` producing a legitimate empty-string token, a trailing lone backslash at end-of-line, multiple/extra inter-token spaces, trailing whitespace after the last token (confirming the old gap is closed), and a line with more than `ARGV_MAX_ARGS` tokens (confirming the 16th is the last one recorded, the rest silently dropped rather than overflowing the table) — all passed.
  - **Two real bugs found via hardware testing (2026-07-15) after this was first thought done, both now fixed and CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE** (user tested `args`/`echo` with plain multi-word args, quoting, and escapes — "all functioned as expected"). Both were introduced in `progs/shell.asm`'s tokenizer, not in the ABI design itself, and both were only found by adding two purpose-built utility programs (`args`, `echo` — see below) specifically to make the tokenizer's output directly observable, exactly the kind of bug a silent/indirect symptom would have hidden for a long time otherwise.
    1. **Read/write cursor collision.** For a plain token with no quote/escape shrinkage (e.g. `args`), the write cursor (`RD`) and read cursor (`RF`) stay exactly equal all the way through — nothing ever gets stripped, so they never diverge. When the token ended by hitting a space, the code wrote the NUL terminator via `RD` *before* checking `*RF` to decide whether more input followed — but since `RD == RF` at that exact point, the terminator write silently overwrote the space itself, and the very next read saw its own just-written NUL and concluded the line was over. Every multi-argument command was capped at `argv[0]` (confirmed via a `TOK argc=` diagnostic reading exactly `1` regardless of how many words were typed). Fixed by consuming the space (`inc rf`) *before* falling into the shared terminator-writing code, so the write and read cursors can never coincide again for the rest of that token's cleanup.
    2. **Missing branch, introduced while writing fix #1.** The restructuring for fix #1 inserted the new "consume the space" block directly between the "is this character a space?" check and the pre-existing `tok_special` (quote/backslash/ordinary-character dispatch) — but without an explicit branch routing the "not a space" case to `tok_special`. The result: when a character *wasn't* a space, execution fell straight through into the new block instead, silently skipping the character (never copying it) and immediately treating it as ending the token. This is the exact same fallthrough-after-restructuring mistake caught once already this session in `mr.asm`/`ms.asm` (see the argv/argc migration bullet above) — caught here only via a hex-dump-of-`LINE_BUF` diagnostic showing real content replaced by runs of zero bytes, after a from-scratch instruction-level emulator (see below) failed to reproduce the bug and forced a return to the actual assembled `.lst` output. Fixed with an explicit `lbr tok_special`.
    - **Methodology note, worth remembering**: two successive Python re-implementations of the tokenizer's algorithm both *failed* to catch these bugs, for two different reasons — the first modeled read and write as logically separate operations, never actually mutating a shared buffer in place (missing bug #1's whole precondition); the second was a more careful "instruction-level emulator" that still silently transcribed the *intended* control flow from memory rather than the literal current file, so it modeled a `lbr tok_special` that didn't actually exist in the source yet (missing bug #2 entirely). Neither failure was caught until re-deriving directly from the real, current `.lst`/source content byte by byte. **Lesson for next time a simulation is used to verify assembly logic**: a simulation is only as trustworthy as its fidelity to the *actual current file*, not to a mental model of what the file is supposed to do — for anything beyond the simplest algorithmic check, prefer decoding the real assembled bytes (as this project's `.lst`-verification practice already does elsewhere) over an independent reimplementation, or at minimum re-derive the simulation directly from a fresh `Read` of the file immediately before trusting it.
  - **`ARGS`/`ECHO` utility programs, added specifically to make this bug findable**: `ARGS [args...]` prints every element of argv (including `argv[0]`) one per line; `ECHO [-n] [args...]` prints `argv[1..]` space-separated with a trailing newline (`-n`, exact match only, suppresses the newline and is itself omitted). Both pure userland, no kernel changes. `progs/template.asm`'s header comment (the canonical example `kernel_api.inc` points to) was also updated in the same pass — it still described the old raw-tail-pointer ABI.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-15)**: every command, plain and multi-argument, quoting and backslash-escaping, both new utility programs. Full regression closed out.
- **`PROG_BASE` must stay comfortably above the kernel's own "Highest address."** The kernel silently grew past the old `PROG_BASE=$2000` partway through adding FAT write support (reached `$2073`), and every subsequent `prog_load` clobbered the tail of the running kernel with the loaded program's bytes — the actual cause of a "DIR partially works, then Kernel init failed" crash that looked at first like a filesystem/write bug. After any kernel change that adds a meaningful amount of code, check the build's "Highest address" against `PROG_BASE` (both `include/kernel.inc` and `include/kernel_api.inc` define it — keep them in sync) and move it up with real headroom (not just a few dozen bytes) well before they get close.
- **`boot/krnboot.asm`'s own load address (`KERN_LOAD` in `boot/mbr.asm`) used to be a SEPARATE, much tighter boundary from `PROG_BASE` — as of 2026-07-10 they are the SAME address by design (see below), so this is now one boundary to check, not two.** `krnboot` (the 512-byte sector-1 bootstrap) loads the kernel proper into RAM starting at `$0100` (`KERN_BASE`), sector-by-sector, for `ceil(kernel.bin_size / 512)` sectors (computed by `sys/sys.c`, patched into the bootstrap's own header at install time) — and if that load range reaches as far as `KERN_LOAD` (where `krnboot` itself resides, loaded there by the MBR one step earlier), the load loop overwrites its own currently-executing code mid-flight. Unlike a `PROG_BASE` overrun (which fails loudly once a program is actually loaded), this failure mode is **totally silent** — it happens before `kernel_init`'s first print, so the symptom is literally nothing on the console, indistinguishable at first glance from a bad flash or dead hardware. Hit for real on 2026-07-09: `KERN_LOAD` was `$3000`, and the kernel's on-disk size (sector-rounded) had grown to reach `$3100` — a 256-byte overlap large enough to corrupt `krnboot`'s own load loop before it ever reached `KERN_ENTRY`. Moved `KERN_LOAD`/`KERN_ENTRY` to `$3800`/`$3806`, giving ~1.8KB of margin. That margin was gone again by the time MD/RD/REN all landed in one session (down to ~256 bytes) — moved a second time, to `$3E00`/`$3E06`, this time proactively, before it actually failed on hardware. Both moves keep `boot/mbr.asm` and `boot/krnboot.asm` in sync — same "real headroom, not a few dozen bytes" philosophy as `PROG_BASE`'s own moves. **Check this margin — `KERN_LOAD - $0100` vs. the kernel's actual sector-rounded on-disk size (`ceil(kernel.bin's byte size / 512) * 512`, NOT just the linker's "Highest address") — after any kernel change that adds a meaningful amount of code.** Moving `KERN_LOAD` requires a full `make install` (MBR rewrite) to take effect, not just `make update`.
- **`$0080-$00FF` is reusable scratch RAM once boot finishes.** The ROM's own small boot-time stack lives there (see `boot/mbr.asm`), but it's dead once the kernel sets up its own stack at himem, so kernel code can claim it as fixed-address storage instead of spending kernel-image bytes on a `ds`. Used so far for `LINE_BUF` (`include/kernel.inc`, the shell's 128-byte command line buffer) — saved 128 bytes off the kernel's "Highest address" versus reserving it in `kernel.asm`'s data area. Only safe for kernel-internal, boot-after-only scratch (not for anything a program might need, and not before the kernel's stack switch completes).
- **File write support (extend/overwrite an existing file) is confirmed working on hardware** (multi-cluster growth, cluster-chain extension, directory-entry size rewrite at close). `file_write` extends/overwrites already-existing files (allocating new clusters via `fat_alloc` as needed) and `file_close` rewrites the directory entry's size field if the file grew.
- **File creation (`file_open` mode 1/2 on a not-found path) and append mode (mode 2) are implemented and confirmed working on hardware** (multiple create/append rounds, sizes accumulating correctly across repeated `ATEST` runs, clean readback each time — no known open bugs as of 2026-07-08). `kernel/file.asm`'s `fopen_notfound` calls `_file_create`, which reuses `file_open`'s own just-completed directory scan (`dir_eptr`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_clust`/`dir_sect`, left pointing at the `$00` terminator) as the insertion point — no second directory scan. It generates a full LFN entry set (via `_gen_short_name`/`_classify_char`/`_lfn_fill_segment`) whenever the name isn't already a clean uppercase 8.3 name (in practice: almost always, since this card's existing files use lowercase names) and a fallback 8.3 short name with **no collision/uniqueness check** — deliberately skipped since this system never looks up a file by its raw short name, only by `dir_read`'s LFN-preferred name (see `_file_create`'s own header comment for the full reasoning). New entries always land at/after the terminator, extending the directory's cluster chain if needed (fails cleanly on a full root, which can't grow). **If the entry doesn't fit in the terminator's own sector and a later sector is used instead, the remainder of the OLD sector (from the old terminator onward) must be overwritten with `$E5` (deleted-entry markers), not left as-is** — a `$00` terminator means "end of directory" wherever `dir_read` meets it, in *any* sector, so leaving the old one in place made every entry written into a later sector permanently invisible to every future scan (`DIR`, `TYPE`, `file_open`) even though the write itself succeeded. `file_write` gained a matching "`FCB_CCLUST == 0`" first-write branch that allocates a file's very first cluster lazily; `_fclose_rewrite_size` now patches `DE_CLUSTER` alongside `DE_SIZE` to record it. Mode 2 (append) computes the end-of-file position via `sector_index >> spc_shift`/`& (spc-1)` shift-and-mask arithmetic (cluster size is always a power of two) and walks `fat_get` that many hops from `FCB_SCLUST`. Test via `progs/wtest.asm` (mode 1, create-or-overwrite) and `progs/atest.asm` (mode 2, create-or-append; run it repeatedly against the same file — size should accumulate and `TYPE` should read back clean every time).
- **`PROG_BASE` is `$3E00`, and — as of 2026-07-10 — is now DELIBERATELY EQUAL to `KERN_LOAD` (`boot/mbr.asm`), not just numerically close.** `krnboot`'s own header comment already established that its 512-byte sector is dead once `kernel_init` is reached (`load_done: lbr KERN_ENTRY` is the last thing that ever touches it) — nothing in the kernel or any program jumps back into it, and confirmed by grep. So there was never a reason to reserve a separate 512-byte gap between `KERN_LOAD` and `PROG_BASE`; the first program load of any boot session (`/bin/shell`, from `kernel_init`'s `run_loop`) simply overwrites krnboot's dead bytes with real program code, for free. This was one of the user's original size-reduction ideas ("is there boot-only code we could reclaim") in its smallest, lowest-risk form — just merging the two addresses, not (yet) moving any actual kernel init code into `krnboot`'s slack space (that bigger, riskier version is still a deferred stretch goal). **Two hardcoded literals in `kernel/loader.asm`'s `prog_load` had to move with it** (`PROG_BASE`'s high byte, used in the `mem_top - PROG_BASE` available-space subtraction and the `mem_base` rounding addition — the exact same class of bug fixed once already this session when `PROG_BASE` moved `$3000`→`$4000`, see `prog_load`'s own comment): both changed from `$40` to `$3E`. Verified via repo-wide grep that no other file hardcodes `PROG_BASE`'s high byte (`prog_exec`'s `call PROG_BASE+6` and every `org PROG_BASE` in `progs/*.asm` are symbolic and needed no change). `include/kernel.inc` and `include/kernel_api.inc` both updated in lockstep as always. Net effect: `RUN_PATH`/`RUN_TAIL_PTR` (which sit just below `PROG_BASE`) now land inside what used to be pure kernel-growth headroom instead of krnboot's dead sector, leaving **2460 bytes before the single unified `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` boundary, 2390 above `RUN_PATH`** — essentially unchanged from before the merge (the 512 reclaimed bytes went to programs' usable RAM above `PROG_BASE`, not to this margin). Build-verified only (28/28 jump-table slots intact, D-clobber sweep clean, all 14 `progs/*.asm` link with 0 errors/warnings) — not yet hardware-tested. Re-check this single boundary before adding the next meaningful chunk of kernel code. REN and the shell-as-a-program foundation are both hardware-confirmed (see its own bullet above) — but that hardware round used the build from *before* this `PROG_BASE=$3E00` move (the user started testing before this change was made), so the krnboot-reclaim itself and `REBOOT` are still awaiting their own hardware round. Batch/redirection/argv processing are the planned next layer on top of the now-confirmed shell foundation, alongside the still-pending caller-allocated-FCBs pass.
- **⚠️ krnboot slack-space reclaim (2b — moving actual kernel init code into krnboot's own sector, not just merging the boundary) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-11, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED (done while the user was away; needs a hardware round before being trusted).** `Highest address` `$33af`→`$3362` (77 bytes), margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` now ~2718 bytes (up from ~2641). `krnboot.bin` itself is still exactly 512 bytes (only ~182 of them used now, ~330 still spare for future additions).
  - **What moved**: krnboot's own load loop used only ~98 of its 512 bytes, leaving 414 bytes of pure, wasted zero padding after `load_done` — dead space by design (per the file's own pre-existing header comment: nothing ever jumps back into this sector once `KERN_ENTRY` is reached). The one-time-only parts of `kernel_init`'s old prologue — baud rate config (`f_setbd`), both startup banners ("ELF-DOS v0.1" / "Type a command."), and the `bpb_init`/`fat_init`/`file_init` calls (plus `bpb_init`'s own error check/handler, formerly `kern_err`) — moved from `kernel/kernel.asm` into a new `boot_init2` routine physically living in that spare space in `boot/krnboot.asm`. `load_done` now jumps to `boot_init2` instead of straight to `KERN_ENTRY`; `boot_init2` falls through to `KERN_ENTRY` once it's done.
  - **What deliberately did NOT move**: `kernel_init`'s `mem_top`/`mem_base`/`cur_dir` writes, and `run_loop` itself (plus its own `kern_shell_err` handler). `run_loop` runs forever, re-entered after every command — it structurally cannot live in the krnboot region, since that memory gets overwritten by the very first program load (`/bin/shell`). The `mem_top`/`mem_base`/`cur_dir` writes target kernel-resident, relocatable-address data; `boot/krnboot.asm` is linked completely separately from `kernel.bin` (its own `link02` invocation, `boot/krnboot.prg` only) and has no way to reach a relocatable symbol directly — only fixed, absolute addresses, which relocatable data labels aren't. (Fixed-address kernel data *is* possible in principle — `$0080-$00FF` is already established reusable boot-scratch RAM, see the `LINE_BUF` gotcha below — but it's already fully consumed by `LINE_BUF`, so moving these three data writes would need a new fixed-address home found elsewhere first; left as a possible future increment, not attempted here.)
  - **Mechanism for the calls that DID move**: three new K_\* jump-table slots — `K_BPB_INIT` ($015A), `K_FAT_INIT` ($015D), `K_FILE_INIT` ($0160), next free $0163 — added to both `kernel/kernel.asm`'s table and `include/kernel_api.inc`, exactly the same append-only mechanism every other cross-link-boundary call in this project already uses (`boot/krnboot.asm` now `#include`s `include/kernel_api.inc`, the same file programs use, since architecturally krnboot is in exactly the same position as any program: linked separately, reaching the kernel only through this fixed table). Marked "boot-only, do not call from ordinary programs" in both places, matching `K_INIT`'s own existing precedent, since re-running them mid-session would reset live BPB/FAT-cache/FCB state out from under whatever's currently using it. Net jump-table cost: 9 bytes (3 new slots) against the ~95 bytes of prologue code removed from the permanently-resident image — the other ~9-byte difference in the observed 77-byte total comes from `-r` relaxation finding 5 more branches now shrinkable given the changed layout, not from this change directly.
  - **Verification** (same standard as the link02 work — direct output-byte inspection, not just internal cross-checks, given a boot-code mistake is the worst kind of bug: silent, zero-output failure): all 31 jump-table slots (28 original + 3 new) decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`, including hand-verifying `K_BPB_INIT`/`K_FAT_INIT`/`K_FILE_INIT`'s targets exactly match `bpb_init`/`fat_init`/`file_init`'s real addresses; every new/changed instruction in `krnboot.bin` (the `load_done`→`boot_init2` jump, every `call`/`lbdf` in `boot_init2`, the `boot_kern_halt` self-loop, all three banner/error strings) decoded byte-for-byte against source; `krnboot.bin` confirmed still exactly 512 bytes; `kernel_init` confirmed to start directly with the `mem_top` setup (no leftover prologue bytes) and correctly fall through into `run_loop`; `kern_shell_err` confirmed to fall through cleanly into the (now-shared, still-present) `kern_halt` self-loop. Full `-r` relaxation re-verified on top of this changed kernel exactly as thoroughly as the original link02 work: 186 of 325 branches now shrink (up from 181, since the smaller/reshuffled layout let 5 more fit), all branches/`^`/`v` pairs/external H-L references/internal labels/remaining plain-code bytes cross-checked directly against the built binary — zero mismatches. `progs/*.asm` confirmed unaffected (the `kernel_api.inc` change is purely additive). Two clean rebuilds reproduced byte-identical output. D-clobber sweep clean on both touched files.
  - **Fallback preserved**: the previous, already-hardware-confirmed kernel (pre-2b, `-r` on, 181/325 shrunk, `$33af`) is saved at `~/kernel_pre2b_confirmed_fallback.bin` in case this round needs a quick revert.
- **Link/02 short-branch relaxation (`-r` flag) — REDESIGNED 2026-07-11 on an iterative rewrite-and-relink architecture (the user's own proposal), exhaustively byte-verified, and CONFIRMED WORKING ON REAL HARDWARE the same day.** Kernel result: `Highest address` `$3464`→`$33af`, 181 of 325 local long branches shortened, converges in 4 rounds. User ran nearly every command including the new `REBOOT`, all working. Two prior in-place-patching designs (an `addresses[]`/`values[]` staleness fix, then a `^`/`v` local-fixup deferred-resolution fix) were tried and abandoned mid-session after the first was deployed and **hung real hardware** despite passing its own extensive verification — see the design pivot below for why. Do **not** re-attempt the old `compactProcBranches()`/in-place-patching approach; this section describes what replaced it.
  - **A red herring during hardware bring-up, worth remembering**: the first hardware round of the redesigned `-r` kernel also failed, every command printing `"Bad command."` — this had NOTHING to do with branch relaxation itself (two independent single-branch bisection builds, one conditional one unconditional, both provably byte-correct via direct verification against Asm/02's own unmodified short-branch path, reproduced the identical failure). Root cause: `/bin`'s on-card contents (including `shell` itself) were stale from *before* this same session's earlier `PROG_BASE` move (`$4000`→`$3E00`) — `progs/*.asm` is assembled with `org PROG_BASE`, baking the value into every absolute address in the program at build time, so a program built against the old `PROG_BASE` and loaded by a kernel that now loads everything to the new `PROG_BASE` silently misresolves things like `RUN_PATH` (a `PROG_BASE`-relative constant) even though the program's own kernel-API calls (fixed, `PROG_BASE`-independent addresses) keep working — explaining why `shell` itself loaded and ran (banner/prompt fine) while every shell-resolved command failed to open. Fixed by `make progs` + a full fresh `/bin` redeploy. **Lesson**: after moving `PROG_BASE` (or anything else `progs/*.asm` bakes in via an `include/kernel_api.inc` constant), stale on-card programs are a real, silent failure mode indistinguishable from a kernel bug — redeploy `bin/*` whenever `PROG_BASE` changes, not just when a program's own source changes.
  - **Hit again, in a different disguise, during the shell-owned-search/jump-table-renumbering work (2026-07-14)**: after that session fully renumbered the kernel API table (`K_PATH_RESOLVE`, `K_DIR_OPEN`, etc. all moved to new fixed addresses), an old `D:/bin/dir` — leftover from *before* the renumbering, on a secondary drive the user hadn't redeployed to yet — called those jump-table slots at their *old* addresses, landing on whatever routine now happened to live there instead. Symptom looked exactly like a fresh kernel bug: a full multi-round hardware bisection (adding numeric-value diagnostics to `_switch_drive` and `progs/dir.asm`) chased it as a register-corruption issue for two full sessions before the user's own manual test (delete the stale `D:/bin`, recreate it from a fresh `C:/bin/dir` copy, confirm it works) revealed the real cause in minutes. **Generalized lesson**: *any* change to the kernel API jump table's addresses — not just a `PROG_BASE` move — invalidates every already-deployed program binary on *every* drive, not just the boot drive. When debugging a hardware failure that only reproduces on one specific drive/command combination right after a kernel rebuild, check whether that drive's copy of the binary actually postdates the last jump-table or `PROG_BASE` change before spending a diagnostic round assuming it's a new kernel bug.
  - **Architecture**: instead of shifting bytes in place and trying to catch every place a pre-computed address might go stale when a *later* branch also shrinks (the failure mode behind both earlier bugs), Link/02 now (1) parses each object file's `.prg` text into raw passthrough lines plus fully-parsed procs (byte buffer + ordered fixup list); (2) for a given "must-stay-long" exclusion set, **regenerates each proc's text completely from scratch** — shrunk branches get their opcode adjusted (`-0x90`) and their two operand bytes collapsed to one, and *every* offset appearing anywhere in the proc (byte positions, every fixup's own patch offset, and for local fixup types their stored target) is renumbered against the proc's new, smaller size; (3) writes the regenerated text to temp files and runs it through the **completely unmodified** `loadFile()`/`doLink()` pipeline, exactly as if it were the original input — cross-proc placement and external-symbol resolution need no special-casing at all, since every proc's internal layout is already final before linking starts; (4) if any shrunk branch comes back "Short branch out of page" (the pre-existing `<`-type check, unmodified), that exact branch is added to the exclusion set and the whole thing reruns from a clean link-state reset. Iterates until a round produces zero failures. All new code lives in `~/projects/Elf/Link-02/relax.c` (~600 lines), wired into `main()` behind the existing `-r` flag; `main.c`'s own load/link block is untouched when `-r` is absent (confirmed **byte-identical** flag-off kernel build).
  - **Asm/02 side is unchanged from the original design and still correct**: `OT_LBR` (the long-branch family — `lbr`/`lbq`/`lbz`/`lbdf`/`lbnq`/`lbnz`/`lbnf`) tags its fixups with `!SYMBOL ADDR` (external target) or `#OFFSET` (local target) instead of the generic `?`/`+` markers, so the `.prg` format can distinguish "branch operand, opcode precedes it" from "plain data." Confirmed byte-identical codegen either way. **Only `#` (local, intra-proc) branches are ever shrunk** — `!` (external-target long branches) always stay long, since a branch to an external symbol can't have its range validated until `doLink()` resolves that symbol, long after this proc's own layout would need to be final. In this kernel `!` is a small, isolated case (14 of 339 candidates, all of them `kernel.asm`'s own dispatch table, which sits outside any `proc`/`endp` block entirely and so isn't touched by relaxation at all). Because Asm/02 now *always* emits `#`/`!` for long branches (never the old bare `?`/`+`), `loadFile()` itself needed a small but real fix beyond the relaxation feature: `#`/`!` are now resolved unconditionally (regardless of `-r`) identically to `+`/`?` respectively — without this, a flag-off build would silently leave every long branch unresolved. Caught immediately by the flag-off regression test (678 bytes differing from the known-good baseline) before it went anywhere near hardware.
  - **Two real, load-bearing bugs found via synthetic testing before the real kernel was ever attempted** (small hand-built `proc`/`endp` test files with a mix of near/far/external branches, decoded by hand against ground truth):
    1. **Emission ordering.** Asm/02 itself only ever flushes `+`/`^`/`v`/`#` (the LOCAL fixup types, resolved by `loadFile()` via a direct `memory[]` read at load time) at `OT_ENDP` — i.e. strictly *after* every `:` content line for that proc, confirmed by inspecting real kernel `.prg` output. The first version of `rlxEmitProc()` emitted fixup marker lines *before* the byte content, which happened to produce a plausible-looking (but wrong) result for a target of exactly zero and a false "collision" warning otherwise — a kept-long branch's target was being resolved against `memory[]` before its own placeholder byte had even been written there. Fixed by buffering fixup-line text and emitting it after the `:` lines, matching Asm/02's own convention exactly.
    2. **Lossy single-byte target for the pre-existing `<` mechanism.** `<`'s original design (`value = memory[addr+offset] + offset`) can only ever validate a target whose own proc-relative offset is under 256 — the stored byte is masked to one byte at rewrite time, so for any proc ≥256 bytes (this kernel's largest is 1662) a shrinking branch whose target's local offset is ≥256 gets silently, incorrectly validated as "same page" regardless of where it truly lands, because the high bits were thrown away before the check ever ran. Never triggered previously since this project has zero genuine hand-written short branches. Fixed by having relax.c emit the *full, unmasked* proc-relative target as an explicit field on generated `<` lines, and having `loadFile()`'s `<` handler use it (when present) instead of reconstructing a value from the lossy stored byte — the plain, non-relax `<` path is untouched, so a genuine hand-written short branch (if one is ever added) still works exactly as before.
  - **Two more bugs found on the first real multi-file (kernel) run, after all synthetic tests passed clean**: (3) `RlxSegment` embedded a full `RlxProc` struct (~300KB, dominated by an 8192-byte content buffer and a 2048-entry fixup table) in *every* segment slot, including the hundreds of plain passthrough lines per file — `malloc()`'d ~19GB across the kernel's 8 object files and failed silently, caught as a NULL-pointer segfault rather than a checked error. Fixed by heap-allocating `RlxProc` only for segments that are actually procs (a handful per file, not hundreds). (4) The outer round loop set `rlxCurOrigFile` (used to attribute an out-of-page failure back to its original file/proc/offset for exclusion) once per file during the *emit* step, long before `loadFile()` actually processed each temp file — so every failure got attributed to whichever object file happened to be emitted last, exclusions from one round never matched the real branch on the next round's retry, and the loop pegged its round cap without ever converging. Fixed by setting it inside `rlxLinkOnce()`'s own per-file load loop, at the point each file is actually loaded.
  - **Verification, matching this project's standing "check actual output bytes, not just internal cross-checks" practice** (see the toolchain-gotchas section's own lesson from the prior design's failure): flag-off kernel build confirmed byte-identical to the last hardware-confirmed baseline; `progs/*.asm` (flat, no `proc`/`endp`) confirmed a genuine no-op (0 of 0 candidates, every program). For the flag-on kernel build: all 28 kernel API jump-table slots decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr` with sensible targets; **all 325 local branches** (181 shrunk, 144 kept long) and **all 14 `^`/`v` local-fixup pairs** cross-checked by reading the final round's actual rewritten `.prg` text plus the real symbol table, computing the expected resolved bytes independently in Python, and comparing directly against the real bytes in the built `kernel.bin` at their true, final addresses — zero mismatches. Two full rebuilds from a clean `make clean` reproduced byte-identical output (deterministic). Backed up to `~/toolchain_backup_2026-07-11/link02-rewrite-relink-verified` (and a matching `asm02-matches-installed`, unaffected/no action needed there). **Installed to `/opt/elfc/link02` and CONFIRMED WORKING ON REAL HARDWARE (2026-07-11)** — see the red-herring note above for the one real bug hit along the way (unrelated to link02 itself). Source version strings bumped (Link/02 v1.6→v1.7, Asm/02 v1.4→v1.5) but **not yet committed/pushed/tagged** — the user plans to do that and cut a release once ready; ELFC (the C compiler, which also uses Asm/02 and Link/02 internally and exclusively emits long branches) is expected to benefit once wired up, per the user's own note.
  - **Scope, unchanged from the original design**: only benefits `proc`/`endp`-wrapped code (`kernel/*.asm`); `progs/*.asm` is flat and sees zero effect, harmlessly. **User's own note, still open**: the ELFC C compiler also uses Asm/02 and Link/02 internally and exclusively emits long branches, so `-r` should help it too once wired into its own build — not yet explored.
- **`REBOOT` (new, `progs/reboot.asm`, not yet hardware-tested)**: warm-reboots via the existing `K_BOOT` passthrough (`f_boot`, BIOS+00h) rather than jumping back into `krnboot`'s own code — which no longer works reliably now that `PROG_BASE` has reclaimed krnboot's sector (its bytes may already be overwritten by whatever program last ran). `f_boot`'s exact contract (whether it ever returns) isn't independently confirmed on this hardware, so the call is followed by a defensive halt loop. Trivial by design — the whole point of `K_BOOT` already existing as a bare BIOS passthrough (added earlier, for a different reason) is that no new kernel primitive was needed here at all.
- **File timestamps**: `kernel/rtc.asm` owns a shared `cur_time` buffer (6 bytes, `f_gettod`'s own format: month/day/year(0=1972)/hour/minute/second, plain binary) and two entry points: `rtc_refresh` (checks `f_getdev`'s device-flags result — bit 4 of the low byte — for RTC presence, and only calls `f_gettod` when it's set, working around a known BIOS bug that clobbers `RC`'s high byte by push/pop-ing `RC` around the call unconditionally) and `_pack_fat_datetime` (packs `cur_time` into FAT's on-disk bit-packed date/time format via flat, non-looping `shl16`/`or` sequences — deliberately not a counted loop, to sidestep gotcha #12 below). Without an RTC, `cur_time` just keeps a fixed boot-time default (midnight, January 1 2000) for the whole session; `TIME`/`DATE` (see their own bullet below) can override it once hardware-confirmed. Only **last-write** time/date is tracked (`DE_WRTTIME`/`DE_WRTDATE`, populated at `_file_create` and repatched by `_fclose_rewrite_size` alongside `DE_SIZE`/`DE_CLUSTER`) — creation time and last-access-date were deliberately scoped out (the latter would mean rewriting the directory entry on every open, even read-only). `K_DIR_READ`'s result buffer (`DIRENT_LEN`) grew to carry `DIRENT_WRTTIME`/`DIRENT_WRTDATE` (same LE-on-disk → BE-in-buffer convention as `DIRENT_CLUST`/`DIRENT_SIZE`), and `DIR` unpacks/prints them as an `MM/DD/YYYY HH:MM` column. **Confirmed working on hardware** (believable, correctly-varying dates on both creation and append).
- **`COPY`/`DEL`/`CD`/`DIR` all confirmed working on hardware** (2026-07-09, after a multi-round bug hunt — see the toolchain gotcha #13 list entry for the full methodology). The `file_open` FCB_FLAGS fix (below) exposed a SECOND, independent, previously-invisible bug in `kernel/loader.asm`'s `prog_load`: `pload_opened`'s `mov rf, prog_fcb` / `str rf` (meant to save the FCB index `file_open` just returned) is the same gotcha-#4 pattern — the `mov` clobbers `D` right before the `str`, so `prog_fcb` was always left holding a fixed garbage constant (`$92`, part of its own linked address) instead of the real index. Every later `file_close(prog_fcb)` call therefore hit the bounds check (`$92 >= FCB_COUNT`) and silently did nothing, **leaking one real FCB slot on every successful program load** — invisible before the `file_open` fix because every open always landed on slot 0 anyway (the old bug made every slot look free regardless of real state), so a failed close was never observable. A THIRD instance sat right next to it: `mov rf, PROG_BASE` between loading the index and `call file_read` also clobbers `D` (its own comment wrongly claimed "D unaffected") — `file_read` was therefore always effectively called with index `0` (`PROG_BASE`'s own low byte), regardless of which slot was actually opened; harmless only because slot 0 has always happened to be the one in use, but a latent trap for any future scenario where it isn't. Both fixed by stashing the real index in `R9` (free at both points) before the intervening `mov`, reloading right before use. Confirmed via hardware diagnostic (`file_open` prints `cur_dir` zero/nonzero + all 3 FCB slots' free/busy state) showing the exact predicted pattern: `000` → `X00` → `XX0` across three sequential program loads, one leaked slot per load. Two real bugs found and fixed en route, both in `kernel/file.asm`'s `file_open`, exposed specifically by `COPY` being the first program to hold two FCBs open simultaneously (single-FCB programs like `TYPE`/`WTEST`/`ATEST` never exercised the failure path): (1) `file_delete`'s LFN-cleanup fix (below) itself shipped with a `mov`-clobbers-`D` bug on its first attempt, later found and fixed; (2) the real `COPY` root cause — `file_open`'s "found an existing file" path computed the new FCB's `FCB_FLAGS` byte as `ldi FCB_F_OPEN` followed by `mov rf, fo_mode` (which clobbers `D`, gotcha #4) then `ldn rf` (overwriting `D` with the raw *mode value*), so a **mode-0 (read) open of an already-existing file left `FCB_FLAGS = 0`** instead of `FCB_F_OPEN` — the slot looked instantly free again while still legitimately in use. Mode 1 masked this by coincidence (mode value `1` happens to equal `FCB_F_OPEN`'s own bit, so ORing `FCB_F_WRITE` onto it landed on the right answer anyway) — mode 2 has the same latent miscomputation (stores `2` instead of `3`, missing the `FCB_F_OPEN` bit) but hasn't caused a visible failure since nothing has depended on that exact bit yet. `COPY`'s source open (mode 0) triggered the bug, so its destination open (mode 1) then "found" the same slot free and reused it — both files silently aliased onto one FCB, so the read loop saw the freshly-created empty destination instead of the real source, returning 0 bytes immediately and leaving a permanent 0-byte destination file despite a false "File copied." success. Fixed by computing the final `FCB_FLAGS` value via a fresh `ldi` in each branch (mode-0 vs mode-1/2), with no `mov` in between to clobber it. Confirmed via a multi-round hardware bug hunt using `; TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC` markers in both `kernel/file.asm` and `progs/copy.asm` (all since removed, verified via `grep`) — including one round where the diagnostic output itself was misleading at first because `prog_load` also calls `file_open` internally while searching candidate filenames, producing several expected-but-easily-misread "chose idx=0" lines before the two calls that actually mattered. `progs/copy.asm` is a pure program (`COPY <source> <dst>`, single-file only, no wildcards) composed entirely from existing `K_FILE_OPEN`/`K_FILE_READ`/`K_FILE_WRITE`/`K_FILE_CLOSE` — no new kernel primitive needed. `DEL <filename>` (`progs/del.asm`) calls the new **`K_FILE_DELETE`** (`$014B`, next free slot now `$014E`), which forwards to `kernel/file.asm`'s new `file_delete`: resolves the path via `path_resolve`, rejects directories (`ATTR_DIR` check), then — deliberately in this order for crash-safety, directly informed by the fsck-caught corruption class earlier in the project — marks the directory entry `$E5` and writes that sector to disk via `f_idewrite` **before** walking the cluster chain (`fat_get`) and freeing each cluster (`fat_set`), finishing with an explicit `fat_flush()` (closes a latent gap: `fat_set` is write-back-only and nothing else forces a flush if `DEL` happens to be the last filesystem op before power-off). The captured first-cluster number is kept in memory (`fdel_next_clust`), not carried in a register, across both the `f_idewrite` call (whose preservation of anything beyond `RA`/`RC`/`RD` is unconfirmed — gotcha #10) and the `fat_get`/`fat_set` calls inside the free loop (same reasoning, `fat_set`'s clobber footprint isn't trustworthy either). This register-across-untrusted-call issue was caught and fixed during static review, before any hardware test — worth calling out since it's exactly the class of bug that otherwise only surfaces as an intermittent hardware failure. (User has since confirmed, from source, that `f_idewrite` does *not* touch `R9` — see gotcha #8 — so the `fdel_next_clust` memory stash around that one call is technically no longer needed. Left in place deliberately for now; revisit when doing the caller-allocated-FCBs/kernel-size-reduction pass, item 5 on the roadmap below.)
- **`MD <path>` (create a subdirectory) confirmed working on hardware** (2026-07-09: created several directories in different locations, copied files into them, `PWD` and `fsck` both clean). `progs/md.asm` calls the new **`K_DIR_CREATE`** (`$014E`, next free slot now `$0151`), which forwards to `kernel/file.asm`'s new `dir_create`: resolves the path via `path_resolve`, rejects an empty final component and a final component of literally `.` or `..` (the root directory has no such entries to catch this via the collision scan below, since root is a fixed region rather than a normal cluster-chain directory), then scans the parent for a name collision using the same `dir_open`/`dir_read`/`f_strcmp` loop `file_open` itself uses. On no collision, allocates a fresh cluster (`fat_alloc`), flushes the FAT immediately (`fat_flush`, same crash-safety-before-referencing-it reasoning as `file_delete`'s own explicit flush), zeros the entire cluster, writes `.` (self) and `..` (parent, or `0` for root — the same root-is-cluster-0 sentinel this project already uses everywhere, so no special-casing needed) into the first sector with real write-time stamps, writes any remaining sectors (`bpb_spc > 1`) as zero, then re-reads the parent's terminator sector back into `dir_buf` (borrowed as scratch for all of the above — restoring it is one extra `f_ideread`, cheap, and necessary since `_file_create` below expects `dir_buf`/`dir_eptr`/`dir_cur_lba` to still describe the parent's insertion point) and calls **`_file_create`** to insert the new directory's own entry into the parent. `_file_create` itself was generalized for this: it used to hardcode `ATTR_ARCHIVE` and leave `DE_CLUSTER` at its zero-filled default (right for a lazily-allocated file); it now reads both from new scratch fields `fc_new_attr`/`fc_new_cluster`, which `file_open`'s own call site sets to `ATTR_ARCHIVE`/`0` to preserve its exact prior behavior, and which `dir_create` sets to `ATTR_DIR`/the real allocated cluster. **Static review caught three real bugs before any hardware test** (a useful reminder that new kernel code in this project reliably needs this level of scrutiny even after the mov-clobbers-D sweeps became routine): (1) the `.`/`..` short-name padding was off by one in both entries (used 9/8 trailing spaces instead of the correct 10/9 needed to fill the 11-byte `DE_NAME` field, leaving the last byte as a stray `$00` instead of `$20` — a genuine on-disk format violation, not just cosmetic); (2) the freshly-allocated cluster's LBA was stored in the wrong register-to-memory byte order not once but twice (both the store right after `_cluster_to_lba` and the reload for the sector-zeroing loop's LBA-increment step used `R7.hi` where the established convention — confirmed by rereading `_cluster_to_lba`'s own accumulator setup in `dir.asm` — is `R8.lo` first), which would have written every zeroed sector to a completely wrong disk location; (3) the "." / ".." name guard's own `f_strcmp` calls clobber `RD`, and an early version of the fix computed `dcr_parent` (needed later for the `..` entry and for `dir_open`) from `RD` *after* those calls had already stomped it — reordered to save both of `path_resolve`'s return values (`RD`=parent cluster, `RF`=final component) into memory immediately, via a third register as the destination pointer so neither gets destroyed as a side effect of the save itself. All three found via full re-reads immediately after first writing the code, before ever attempting to build — worth noting since none of them would have been caught by the mov-clobbers-D line-adjacency sweeps (gotcha #13); they're a different failure shape (arithmetic miscounts, wrong-byte-order conventions, wrong-order-of-operations) that only a careful trace catches.
- **`RD <path>` (remove an EMPTY subdirectory) confirmed working on hardware** (2026-07-09: tested a number of scenarios, final `fsck` clean). `progs/rd.asm` calls the new **`K_DIR_REMOVE`** (`$0151`, next free slot now `$0154`), which forwards to `kernel/file.asm`'s new `dir_remove`. Refuses `.`/`..`, the root, non-directories, and non-empty directories (no recursive delete). Deliberately reuses rather than duplicates `file_delete`'s (DEL's) own mark-`$E5`/LFN-cleanup/cluster-free logic: that tail was factored out of `file_delete` verbatim into a new shared proc **`_delete_located_entry`** (Args: `R9` = target's first cluster; `dir_last_off`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_buf` = the located entry's parent-sector state — same precondition `_file_create` already relies on), with `file_delete` itself changed only to `call` it instead of falling through — zero logic changes to the already-hardware-confirmed DEL code path, just an extraction (labels inside the extracted tail renamed `fdel_*`→`dle_*` to avoid a duplicate `fdel_err` between the two procs). `dir_remove`'s own novel part is the empty-check: after locating the target's entry in its parent (mirroring `file_delete`'s own scan loop, but requiring `ATTR_DIR` instead of rejecting it), it does a SECOND `dir_open`/`dir_read` pass over the *target's own* cluster, accepting only entries that match `.` or `..` and rejecting anything else as "not empty." That second scan clobbers dir.asm's live scan state (`dir_last_off`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_buf`), which `_delete_located_entry` depends on to still describe the *parent's* location — so `dir_remove` saves that state into dedicated scratch fields (`drm_saved_clust`/`drm_saved_off`/`drm_saved_lba`) *before* the empty-check scan and restores it (`dir_buf` via a fresh `f_ideread`, not just copying bytes) immediately after, right before calling `_delete_located_entry` — the same "save real state, do risky work, restore before the shared tail runs" shape as `dir_create`'s own cluster-zeroing borrow of `dir_buf`. The `.`/`..` guard and the parent-cluster stash follow `dir_create`'s own already-proven fix exactly (save `path_resolve`'s both return values into memory immediately via a third register, before the guard's `f_strcmp` calls — confirmed clobbering `RD` — can destroy either one); an early draft of this got that ordering wrong (parent cluster read from `RD` *after* the `f_strcmp` guard had already stomped it, and one dead leftover instruction from an earlier attempt) and was corrected during a full re-read before ever building, the same static-review discipline `dir_create` needed. Builds clean, 26/26 jump-table slots verified.
- **`REN <path> <newname>` (rename a file or directory) — CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-10)**, including the fix for the multi-round "duplicate directory entry" bug (see gotcha #10's `_fat_load_sector`/`fat_flush` `R9`-clobber entry for the full story). User ran extended `DIR`/`DEL`/`PWD`/`MD`/`RD`/`COPY` combinations post-fix with no errors and a clean `fsck`. `progs/ren.asm` splits the two space-separated arguments (same pattern as `COPY`) and calls the new **`K_FILE_RENAME`** (`$0154`, next free slot now `$0157`) → `kernel/file.asm`'s new `file_rename`. Same-directory rename only (no cross-directory move); works on either a file or a directory (no `ATTR_DIR` check either way, unlike DEL/RD). Deliberately always does insert-new-then-remove-old rather than an in-place short-name/LFN edit — reuses `_file_create` (now further parameterized with a THIRD field, `fc_new_size`, alongside `fc_new_attr`/`fc_new_cluster`, so the new entry carries over the target's existing size exactly — `file_open`'s and `dir_create`'s own call sites both set it to 0 to preserve their exact prior behavior) to insert a new entry with the SAME attribute/cluster-chain/size as the old one, under the new name, **before** removing the old entry — collision-checked first, so if anything fails partway the worst case is a harmless duplicate entry, never a lost one (same "safe step before the destructive one" ordering as DEL/MD). Removing the old entry reuses `_mark_entry_deleted` (see below) but deliberately **not** `_delete_located_entry` — the cluster chain must survive, since the new entry now points at it. `_delete_located_entry` itself (DEL/RD's shared tail) was split into two procs for this: `_mark_entry_deleted` (mark `$E5` + LFN-cleanup + write sector, no cluster free) and `_delete_located_entry` (now just `call`s `_mark_entry_deleted` then frees the chain) — zero logic changes to either already-hardware-confirmed path, purely an extraction. Renaming a directory doesn't require touching its own `.`/`..` entries or its children's `..` entries at all, since those reference the cluster number (unchanged by a rename), not the name. Caught and fixed one real bug during the same static re-review this project now does routinely for new kernel code: the attribute-byte capture (`file_dirent`'s `DIRENT_ATTR` → `fc_new_attr`) computed the byte via `ldn rf` then immediately did `mov r9, fc_new_attr` before the `str` — the exact `mov`-clobbers-`D` gotcha (#4) this project has hit repeatedly, caught by manual re-trace since the cluster/size captures right next to it happen to order the `mov` *before* their own `lda`/`ldn` (correct) rather than after (wrong), making the one wrong instance easy to miss by pattern-matching against its neighbors. **Also moved `KERN_LOAD` proactively this same session, `$3800`→`$3E00`** (see its own note below) — MD+RD+REN together brought boot margin down to ~256 bytes, and this time it was caught and fixed before a hardware failure forced the issue, not after.
- **`COPY` gained two enhancements: copying into an existing directory (source's own basename is used), and an overwrite-confirmation prompt when the destination already exists. CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-10)** — both features specifically exercised (a directory-destination copy, and an overwrite accepted via the Y/N prompt), alongside the rest of the shell-as-a-program hardware round. The directory case reuses `CD`'s own `K_PATH_RESOLVE`/`K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ`/`ATTR_DIR` pattern verbatim (no new kernel primitive) to check whether the destination argument names an existing directory; if so, `progs/copy.asm` builds `<dst>/<basename of src>` into a local scratch buffer (`dst_final`) and uses that as the real destination for the rest of the program, falling back to the destination argument unchanged otherwise. The overwrite prompt needed one new kernel primitive, since nothing exposed a way to read a single keystroke to programs before now: **`K_READ`** (`$0157`, next free slot now `$015A`), a straight passthrough to the BIOS's `f_read` (blocking single-character console read) — same trivial one-line pattern as the existing `K_TTY`/`f_tty` passthrough. `f_read`'s exact register contract (D = char in, no other side effects) is the standard Elf/OS BIOS convention, and is now confirmed correct on this hardware (the overwrite prompt read back `Y`/`y` correctly during the 2026-07-10 hardware round).
