Only `Y`/`y` (case-folded via `ani $DF`) confirms; anything else, including bare Enter, cancels — the user's explicit choice over defaulting Enter to yes, on grounds of not wanting to blow away a file by reflex. **Three real bugs caught during static review before ever building**, all instructive: (1) `K_PATH_RESOLVE`'s own header documents `RC` as one of its clobbered registers, so an initial draft that stashed the destination string pointer in `RC` across the `K_PATH_RESOLVE` call (to reuse it later when building `dst_final`) was silently reading garbage after the call returned — fixed by re-reading the pointer fresh from its own memory variable (`dst_ptr`) at the point of use instead of trusting a register to survive an audited-clobber call; (2) the basename-scan helper used `RF` itself as scratch for its own string-pointer indirection, silently destroying `dst_final`'s in-progress write position (which also lived in `RF`) — the exact "which register does this block actually need to survive across it" class of bug gotcha #10 already warns about, just self-inflicted this time; caught by re-tracing which registers each block actually needed preserved, not by pattern-matching against the surrounding code. (3) `file_close`'s own header (confirmed by reading `kernel/file.asm`'s `file_close` directly) takes the FCB index in `D`, not `RF` — an initial draft of the "does the destination already exist" check passed it in `RF` instead, copying a `mov rf, rd` idiom from elsewhere in the file that was solving a *different* problem (passing a **name pointer** to `K_FILE_OPEN`, which does take it in `RF`) without checking whether the same convention applied to the very next call. Also deliberately stashes the single character read from `K_READ` in memory rather than a register across the two calls that follow it (`K_TTY` to echo, `K_INMSG` for the newline) — this project has only confirmed `R9`'s survival across `f_msg`/`f_inmsg` (gotcha #8), and neither of those two calls has been separately audited for any other register.
  **A genuine, previously-latent kernel bug surfaced by this feature's first hardware test (2026-07-10)**: overwriting an existing file with SHORTER new content left the directory entry reporting the OLD (larger) size forever, with the file's real bytes beyond the new content silently left as stale leftover data from whatever was there before — confirmed with `WTEST` creating a 2200-byte file, then `COPY`'s new overwrite path writing 52 bytes over it: `DIR` kept showing 2200 bytes, `TYPE` showed the correct 52 new bytes followed by ~2148 bytes of the old test pattern. Root cause: `file_write`'s size-tracking (`kernel/file.asm`) only ever updates `FCB_FSIZE`/sets `FCB_F_SIZECHG` when `FCB_FPOS` grows *past* the size the file had when it was opened (see its own header comment: "if it grows FCB_FSIZE past the size recorded at open, FCB_F_SIZECHG is set") — a deliberate scope limit from when `file_write` was first built (`WTEST`/`ATEST` only ever grow or hold steady, never shrink), never revisited once `COPY` started exercising "overwrite an existing file with different-length content" for the first time. Fixed at `file_open`'s mode-1 (write) FCB population, not in `file_write` itself: when mode 1 finds an *existing* file, `FCB_FSIZE` is now truncated to 0 immediately at open (matching "overwritten from position 0" semantics — classic DOS open-for-write behavior) and `FCB_F_SIZECHG` is set right there, unconditionally, rather than waiting for `file_write` to notice growth. `file_write`'s own existing grow-detection then naturally tracks the true final size from 0 upward as bytes are actually written, with zero changes needed to `file_write` itself — and a mode-1 open that's closed without writing anything now correctly truncates the file to empty, matching DOS semantics for that case too, which wasn't previously exercised by anything either. Mode 0 (read) and mode 2 (append) are both unaffected (verified by tracing: the new mode-1 check falls through to the untouched mode-2 check for any other mode value, reaching the exact same `fopen_no_append` exit as before). Not yet hardware-tested — this is the fix itself awaiting confirmation, alongside the two `COPY` features that exposed it.
- **`K_GETCURDIR` now activates `cur_drive`'s own BPB/FAT cache before returning (fix, 2026-07-15), found via a real hardware bug on a multi-partition drive.** Symptom: `cd`'d into a subdirectory on `D:` while `C:` was the boot drive — the shell's own prompt fell back to its plain `"D:> "` form (never showing the subdirectory name) and `PWD` printed `"Error reading directory structure."` immediately after. Root cause: `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` (`kernel/dir.asm`) never call `_switch_drive` themselves — only `path_resolve` does — so a cluster number handed back by `K_GETCURDIR` is only meaningful if the active BPB *already* happens to match `cur_drive`, which nothing guaranteed. In particular `prog_run_shell`'s own fast-path reload (`kernel/loader.asm`) unconditionally reactivates `shell_drive` (hardcoded C) on *every* shell reload regardless of `cur_drive`, and a program can itself load from a drive other than `cur_drive` via the shell's own `shell_drive` fallback search — so by the time control reached `print_prompt` (`progs/shell.asm`) or `PWD` (`progs/pwd.asm`), both of which call `K_GETCURDIR` then `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` directly with no switch of their own, the active drive was silently C while the cluster they'd just been handed was only valid on D. `DIR`'s own bare (no-argument) listing has the identical vulnerable shape but happened not to misfire in testing so far, purely because loading `dir.exe` itself from the active drive's own `/bin` usually leaves that same drive active as a side effect — an accident of timing, not a guarantee. Fixed once, centrally, in `kernel_getcurdir` (`kernel/kernel.asm`) — it now calls `_switch_drive(cur_drive)` before computing its return value, a documented no-op when already correct so the fix is free in the already-working case. This transparently fixes every caller (`print_prompt`, `PWD`, `DIR`'s bare path, and any future one) with no changes needed on their end. **The first attempt at this fix was itself a regression, caught on the very next hardware round (2026-07-14/15):** calling `_switch_drive` from inside `kernel_getcurdir` with no register protection broke two real callers that depended on the routine's old, narrow (if never formally documented) clobber footprint — `progs/dir.asm` calls `K_GETCURDIR` as its very first instruction, *before* reading its own command tail out of `RA`, and with `RA` now clobbered by `_switch_drive`, a bare `DIR` (no argument at all) treated garbage as a path and printed its own `"Directory not found."`; `progs/shell.asm`'s `shell_drive`-fallback comparison similarly keeps a value alive in `RB` across the same call. Fixed by saving/restoring `RA`/`RB`/`RC`/`R7` around the internal `_switch_drive` call inside `kernel_getcurdir` (the registers it overwrites anyway as part of its own normal logic — `R8`/`R9`/`RD`/`RF` — didn't need protecting). This restores the exact external behavior every existing caller was already relying on, while still getting the beneficial drive-activation side effect. **Lesson for next time a kernel routine gains an internal call to something with a broad "Modifies" list**: check every existing caller's *actual* register usage around the call site, not just the routine's own formally documented contract — undocumented-but-real narrow-clobber assumptions are exactly as load-bearing as documented ones, and this project has now hit that specific trap in both directions (a callee assuming a narrow footprint that wasn't real, gotcha #10; and here, a caller assuming a narrow footprint that was real but unwritten). **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-14/15)** — a diagnostic added to `progs/dir.asm` (`ARG:<tail> DRV:<digit>`, right after `K_GETCURDIR`) showed an empty tail and the correct drive digit on C:, D:, and E: across many `dir` invocations, and `cd`/`pwd`/the prompt all tracked the active drive correctly through repeated `cd`/drive-switch sequences. Diagnostic removed once confirmed (grep-verified clean). Final margins: `Highest address` `$38ed`, 1299 bytes before `PROG_BASE`, 1280 before `KERN_LOAD`, `krnboot.bin` unchanged.
- **Kernel size-reduction pass, 2026-07-15: `mov reg,symbol+CONST` folding — ATTEMPTED, CAUSED A BOOT FAILURE ON HARDWARE, REVERTED, THEN RE-APPLIED SUCCESSFULLY AFTER AN UPSTREAM ASM/02 FIX. BUILD-VERIFIED AND BYTE-LEVEL-VERIFIED, AWAITING A HARDWARE ROUND.** Full incident, in order:
  - **What was attempted**: `mov reg, symbol` + `add16 reg, CONST` folded into a single `mov reg, symbol+CONST` across 29 kernel-internal instances (`rtc.asm` ×4, `path.asm` ×2, `kernel.asm` ×1, `loader.asm` ×1, `file.asm` ×21). Build-verified clean (assembly, D-clobber sweep, jump table, margins, `make progs`) and reported as done — but the verification never decoded the actual resolved bytes of a *folded* site, only the jump table's own `lbr` entries (a different fixup mechanism). The user flashed it and got `"No shell on C:."` at boot — the exact failure signature previously caused by the large-partition `_cluster_to_lba`/RC-clobber bug (already fixed, unrelated) — which is what made this look at first like a recurrence of something old rather than a brand new bug.
  - **Real root cause, found by decoding the actual built `kernel.bin` at `kernel_shell_init`**: `mov rf, file_dirent+DIRENT_ATTR` resolved to `RF = 0x0080` — not `file_dirent`'s real linked address plus 128. The relocatable symbol's contribution was silently dropped, leaving only the bare constant. `0x0080` lands in kernel scratch RAM (`LINE_BUF` territory), so the "is this a directory?" check read garbage — which happened to look like `ATTR_DIR` was set, misclassifying `/bin/shell`'s own entry as a directory and failing `K_SHELL_INIT`. Root cause was in Asm/02 itself (`asm.c`'s expression evaluator, not this project's code) — see toolchain gotcha #17 below for the full mechanism. The retracted gotcha #2 entry only ever tested numeric-literal offsets (`real_block+300`), which don't hit this path at all — a materially different case from `symbol+EQU_CONSTANT_NAME`, which is what nearly every one of these 29 folds actually used (`DIRENT_ATTR`, `FCB_ELBA`, `DE_SIZE`, etc. are all named `equ` labels, not raw numbers).
  - **First response: reverted all 29 folds** back to the original two/three-instruction `mov reg,symbol` + `add16 reg,CONST[+CONST2]` form (`kernel/rtc.asm`/`path.asm`/`kernel.asm`/`loader.asm` via a clean `git checkout`, `kernel/file.asm`'s 21 sites reverted individually via script while keeping its separate, unrelated `dirent_patch_buf` elimination changes intact). Re-verified by decoding `kernel_shell_init` again post-revert: `RF` now correctly resolved to `file_dirent`'s real address via a proper carry-safe `GLO`/`ADI`/`PLO`/`GHI`/`ADCI`/`PHI` sequence. **User confirmed on hardware (2026-07-15): the reverted kernel boots, `dir`/`cd`/`wtest`/`atest` all work, `fsck` clean.**
  - **Asm/02 itself then fixed upstream (Asm/02 v1.6, `Elf/Asm-02` commit `ae1139a`, pushed).** The bug: parsing an expression's *second* operand token, when that token is itself a named `equ` constant (not a bare numeric literal), unconditionally overwrote the assembler's internal `usedReference` tracking based on whether *that* token is external/local — silently clobbering the relocation reference recorded from the *first* operand (the relocatable base symbol). Fixed by only updating that tracking when the current token is itself a genuine reference. Verified via isolated tests (plain symbol, numeral offset, the exact broken named-constant case, chained named offsets, a byte-boundary-crossing carry case, `high`/`low` with a named offset — all correct) and a zero-regression check (ELF-DOS's full kernel + all 25 `progs/*.asm`, rebuilt with the patched compiler, byte-identical to the previous compiler's output, since the reverted codebase has no instances of the broken pattern). `equ alias: equ symbol+CONST` (gotcha #15) is a separate, still-open bug, confirmed unaffected by this fix. Full write-up lives in `Asm-02`'s own `readme.md` and commit message, not repeated here.
  - **Re-applied all 29 folds a second time** (same 29 sites, script-identified and re-verified against the original list to confirm an exact match: 4+2+1+1+21). This time verification included **direct byte-level decode of the previously-broken site and a 3-term chained site**, not just the jump table: `kernel_shell_init`'s `file_dirent+DIRENT_ATTR` now resolves to `RF = 0x2e2f`, exactly matching `file_dirent`'s real linked address (`0x2daf`) plus `DIRENT_ATTR` (`0x80`); `dir_create`'s `dir_buf+DIR_ENT_SIZE+DE_ATTR` resolves to `RF = 0x0554`, exactly matching `dir_buf` (`0x0529`) + `DIR_ENT_SIZE` (`0x20`) + `DE_ATTR` (`0x0b`). Full standard checklist also re-run clean: D-clobber sweep, all 36 jump-table slots + both data-pointer slots, `K_STAT`/`K_BATCH_START`/`K_BATCH_READLINE` symbol cross-checks, `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), `make progs` 25/25. Margins: `Highest address` `$3649`, **1975 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 1792 bytes of sector-rounded boot headroom** — identical to the very first (buggy-compiler) attempt's numbers, confirming the fold pass itself was always mechanically sound; only the compiler's handling of it was broken. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a fresh round before being trusted, same as any kernel change, despite the byte-level verification this time.
  - **Lesson for next time a `mov reg,symbol+CONST`-style fold (or anything like it) is attempted**: verifying the jump table's `lbr` entries is NOT sufficient evidence that a `mov`-operand compound expression resolves correctly — `lbr`'s fixups use a distinct marker (`!`/`#`) from a plain `mov`'s NW-immediate fixups (the generic `?`/`W`/`+` markers), and this bug was specific to the latter. Any future claim that this kind of folding is safe needs a *direct* byte-level decode of an actual folded site (base symbol's real linked address, plus the constant, compared against the resolved instruction bytes) — exactly the check that would have caught this before it ever reached hardware the first time, and the check that was actually performed the second time around.
- **`_fclose_rewrite_size`'s dedicated 512-byte `dirent_patch_buf` scratch buffer eliminated (2026-07-15), reusing the closing FCB's own `FCB_IOBUF`-pointed buffer instead — BUILD-VERIFIED, awaiting the same fresh hardware round as the fold pass above (independent of it — this change doesn't use the `mov reg,symbol+CONST` folding pattern anywhere, so it was never affected by the Asm/02 bug above).** Safe because: `dirent_patch_buf` had no other caller anywhere in the tree (confirmed by grep); this project's `file_write` always writes through to disk immediately (no deferred-dirty-buffer design), so by the time `file_close` reaches this routine the FCB's iobuf can only be holding a stale *read* cache, never unflushed data; and `file_close` zeroes the entire `FCB_FLAGS` byte (including `FCB_F_IOVALID`) unconditionally right after this routine returns regardless of which path was taken, so no new cache-invalidation logic was needed. The resolved iobuf address is stashed once, at entry, into a new 2-byte `fcrw_iobuf` field (replacing the old 512-byte `dirent_patch_buf` declaration) and re-read fresh from memory at each of the routine's 5 use sites via a *plain* `mov reg,fcrw_iobuf` (no compound offset) — a register can't be trusted to carry it between sites, since none of the several BIOS/kernel calls this routine makes (`f_ideread`, `rtc_refresh`, `_pack_fat_datetime`, `f_idewrite`) are proven to preserve any given register (gotcha #8/#10). Register-liveness was hand-traced at all 5 sites to pick a scratch register genuinely free at that exact point (R9, R8, R9, RB, R9 respectively) without disturbing whatever the surrounding code already had live in R7/R8/R9/RC/RD at that point. Saved 197 bytes off `Highest address`. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round exercising anything that closes a file after writing (`WTEST`/`ATEST`/`COPY` overwrite, `MD`/`RD`/`REN`).
- **I/O redirection (`>`, `>>`, `<`) — Phase 2 of the shell command-line roadmap, IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-16, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Builds directly on Phase 1's argv/argc tokenizer (`progs/shell.asm`): the same per-token loop now recognizes a leading `>`/`>>`/`<` and, instead of writing an ordinary `argv[]` entry, writes the following token's pointer (optional whitespace allowed between the operator and the filename, e.g. both `>file` and `> file` work) into one of two new fixed relay slots — `RUN_REDIR_OUT`/`RUN_REDIR_OUT_APPEND`/`RUN_REDIR_IN` (`include/kernel.inc`/`kernel_api.inc`, same "kernel/shell-internal plumbing, pointer into `LINE_BUF`, never referenced by ordinary programs" convention as `RUN_PATH`) — without incrementing argc, using a new per-token flag (`R8`'s high byte, alongside the existing quote-state low byte) that `tok_end_token` checks to skip the argc++ for a redirect target. At most one output and one input redirect per line; a repeated operator overwrites the slot (last one wins); operators can appear anywhere on the line. Because this lives in the shared tokenizer, redirection works inside `.bat` scripts for free.
  - **Made transparent to every existing and future program with zero calling-convention changes**: `K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`/`K_READ`/`K_INPUTL`'s jump-table entries (`kernel/kernel.asm`) changed from bare `lbr f_*` BIOS passthroughs to a plain `lbr` into a new dispatcher in a new `kernel/redir.asm` (matching the `kernel/batch.asm` precedent — a self-contained feature file, added to the Makefile's `KOBJ`) — each checks a `redir_*_active` flag and falls straight through to the original BIOS call with zero added overhead when redirection isn't active, so no jump-table address or program-facing contract changed at all. `K_INMSG` — Elf/OS's inline-message SCRT idiom, `call K_INMSG` immediately followed by `db "text",0` — is fully supported, not deferred, once the user supplied the real BIOS source (`lda r6`/`bnz`-loop/`sep sret`, using `R6` as the "resume past the inline text" pointer set up by the *outer* call's own SCRT mechanism): the new `_redir_inmsg` scans the whole message first with a pure `lda r6`/`lbnz` loop (no calls during the scan, so nothing can clobber `R6` mid-scan), stashes `R6`'s start/end values to memory, dispatches once (one `file_write` if redirected, or the original byte-at-a-time `f_type` loop if not, matching today's console timing exactly), then explicitly reloads `R6` from the stashed value before returning rather than assuming it survives the dispatch call (gotcha #8/#10's standing discipline). Reached via a plain `lbr`, never a nested `call`, from `k_inmsg`'s own jump-table slot — critical, since a nested call would reset `R6` via the outer SCRT mechanism before this routine ever got a chance to read it.
  - **Memory cost kept to the rare case only, per the user's own proposal (2026-07-16), instead of a permanent dedicated allocation**: output-only or input-only redirection — the overwhelming majority of real use — costs **zero** new permanent kernel-resident bytes, by reusing `kernel/loader.asm`'s `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf`. Confirmed safe by reading `prog_run`: `_prog_finish_load` closes `prog_fcb`'s handle *before* `_prog_exec_now` ever jumps to the child's entry point, so `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` are provably idle for the child's entire run — exactly the window redirection needs. Only the rare case of a single command redirecting **both** output and input at once needs a second, simultaneously-open FCB+iobuf pair. Rather than a second permanent 544-byte (`FCB_LEN`+`SECTOR_SIZE`) allocation, that pair is carved dynamically out of the top of RAM for the duration of that one command: `_redir_reserve` relocates the hardware stack (`R2`) and `mem_top` down by `REDIR_RESERVE_LEN` (544) bytes — computing `stack_used = mem_top - R2` fresh (never assumed), copying that many bytes from their current locations to 544-bytes-lower locations with a plain register-only byte-copy loop (no calls of its own, so nothing can grow the stack out from under the byte count computed at the top of the routine), then adjusting `R2` and `mem_top` to match; a sanity check rejects the reservation (failing the whole command, per the user's own preference over "half working") if the resulting `mem_top` would drop below `mem_base`. `_redir_release` (called from `_redir_teardown`, always, after both FCBs are already closed — the reserved region *is* exactly the memory being handed back) reverses it by adding `REDIR_RESERVE_LEN` back to both. The dynamic pair's own address is never stashed separately — it's just `mem_top + 1` (already reduced), recomputed on demand. Two explicit assumptions documented in `_redir_reserve`'s own header, since neither is independently verifiable from within the routine: the copy source/destination ranges don't overlap (true by a wide margin this shallow into the call chain — `run_loop` → `_redir_setup` → `_redir_reserve`), and nothing else can push onto the stack asynchronously mid-copy (no interrupt-driven activity during normal kernel/program execution on this hardware).
  - **`_redir_setup`/`_redir_teardown`** (`kernel/redir.asm`) run around `prog_run` in `run_loop` (`kernel/kernel.asm`), replacing the plain `call prog_run` there. `_redir_setup` opens output first (if requested, always via `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf`), then input (if requested) — using `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` if output wasn't *also* opened, or the dynamically-reserved pair if it was. Any failure (bad path, disk full, the input file missing, or — extremely rare — insufficient RAM headroom for the dynamic reservation) unwinds whatever had already succeeded (closes an opened output, undoes a completed reservation) before reporting `DF=1`, so a failed redirect never leaves anything half-open and the whole command is skipped rather than run with partial redirection. `_redir_setup` must run *before* `run_loop` loads `RA`/`RC` from `RUN_ARGV_TABLE`/`RUN_ARGC` (its own "Modifies" list includes both). `_redir_teardown` runs on *both* of `run_loop`'s post-`prog_run` paths (success and `run_bad_program`) — deliberately checked and called on each path separately rather than once after a shared `lbdf`, since `_redir_teardown`'s own internal work would otherwise clobber `prog_run`'s `DF` result before `run_bad_program`'s own check ever saw it.
  - **Verification**: full clean `make`+`make progs` (25/25, 0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep clean on every touched file, all 36 jump-table slots decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`, `K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`/`K_READ`/`K_INPUTL`'s targets specifically cross-checked against `_redir_type`/`_redir_msg`/`_redir_inmsg`/`_redir_read`/`_redir_inputl`'s real linked addresses via `link02 -s` (all matched), `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes). Every routine in `kernel/redir.asm` (`_redir_reserve`/`_redir_release`'s stack arithmetic especially) was hand-traced end-to-end against a fresh read of the file, checking every branch/fallthrough and every register-liveness assumption — the same discipline the argv/argc tokenizer's own two hardware-found bugs (a cursor collision, a missing branch) already taught this project is necessary beyond what a clean assemble or the D-clobber sweep script catches. `Highest address` `$3ae3`, margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` now **797 bytes**, sector-rounded boot headroom **768 bytes** — noticeably tighter than prior margins (this is explicitly expected to be the last kernel-resident-growing feature before the project shifts to userland command work, per the user's own framing). **NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED — this is the highest-risk kernel change in the project's history** (the first-ever runtime relocation of the real hardware stack pointer) and needs a careful, dedicated round before being trusted: plain `dir > out.txt` + `type out.txt` (basic output, doesn't exercise `K_INMSG`); `echo one >> out.txt` twice (append); `type nonexistent.txt > out.txt` (exercises `K_INMSG` redirection specifically — "File not found." should land in the file, not the console); an ordinary unredirected command immediately after, to catch a `redir_*_active` flag left set by teardown; a `K_READ`-driven test via `COPY`'s overwrite prompt fed through `< file`; a genuine **dual-redirect** command (`cmd < in.txt > out.txt`) to exercise the stack-relocation path specifically, ideally followed by several more ordinary commands to confirm the system remains stable after `R2`/`mem_top` are restored; redirection inside a `.bat` script.
  - **First hardware round (2026-07-16) immediately hit a real bug, unrelated to the stack-relocation mechanism**: a plain `dir`, no redirection typed at all, failed with `"Cannot redirect."`. Root cause: `RUN_REDIR_OUT`/`RUN_REDIR_IN` are fixed RAM addresses below `PROG_BASE` (same category as `RUN_PATH`/`RUN_ARGC`), not part of the kernel's own zeroed data section — and the tokenizer only ever *wrote* them when it actually saw a `>`/`<` operator, never reset them otherwise. So they held uninitialized RAM on first boot (and, after any command that ever redirected, stale leftover values from that command) — `_redir_setup` read that garbage as a real redirect request on every subsequent command. Fixed by explicitly zeroing both relay slots at the top of `progs/shell.asm`'s tokenizer, every pass, before any `>`/`<` detection runs — same "always reset shared state up front" shape as `RUN_ARGC`/`RB` already being freshly reset each time. Build-verified; kernel itself unchanged (userland-only fix).
  - **Second hardware round (2026-07-16), same day, a much more serious bug — a register-preservation contract violation across all five dispatchers.** Symptom: `TYPE` (no redirection involved at all) printed only 1-2 garbage characters per file and no trailing newline, with the shell's next prompt gluing onto the same line — plus, on the very first boot, a mysterious 0-byte file with a garbled/mojibake name appeared in the root directory (a leftover artifact from the *first* bug above, on a run where the uninitialized `RUN_REDIR_OUT` garbage happened to look like a creatable filename instead of failing cleanly — `fsck` reported it as a structurally valid, if meaningless, entry). Root cause: every one of the five new dispatchers (`_redir_type`/`_redir_msg`/`_redir_inmsg`/`_redir_read`/`_redir_inputl`) used `RF`/`RC`/`RA`/etc. as ordinary internal scratch and returned with them clobbered — but the old bare `lbr f_*` passthroughs never touched those registers at all, and real callers throughout this codebase were written relying on specific, previously-undocumented-but-real survival guarantees: `progs/hexdump.asm`'s own comment already establishes "RF/RC across K_TYPE is the one exception, proven by `progs/type.asm`'s own hot loop" (its `read_loop`/`print_loop` depends on `RF` — the read cursor into `type_buf`, advanced via `lda rf` immediately before each `call K_TYPE` — surviving the call); gotcha #8 already establishes `R9` survives `f_msg`/`f_inmsg`, which `_redir_inmsg` itself was internally using as scratch (`kim_start`'s value) without preserving it; `f_read`'s own confirmed contract is "D = char in, no other side effects" (from the `COPY` overwrite-prompt work) — the strongest of the five. **Fix**: every dispatcher now `push`es everything it uses as scratch immediately after stashing whatever incoming argument lives in `D` (gotcha #4 ordering — a `push` itself clobbers `D`, so the stash must happen first), and `pop`s it all back, in correct LIFO order, before *every* exit point (both the redirected-success return and the console-fallback branch) — so from a caller's perspective each dispatcher is now indistinguishable from calling the bare BIOS routine directly, whether or not redirection is actually active. `_redir_type`/`_redir_msg`/`_redir_read` preserve `RF`/`RC`/`RA`; `_redir_inmsg` also preserves `R9` (the one it was internally reusing); `_redir_inputl` additionally preserves `R8`/`RB` (its own internal chain-walk scratch). One unprotected register remains fair game in each (`R7`, used to stash the incoming argument across the pushes) — matching the project's default "assume clobbered unless proven" stance, since nothing establishes `R7` specifically as safe for any of these five calls. Build-verified: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, all 36 jump-table slots decoded and confirmed `0xC0`, all 5 dispatcher targets cross-checked against their real linked addresses, `krnboot.bin` unchanged, `make progs` 25/25. **Margins dropped further from all the new push/pop code**: `Highest address` `$3be9`, margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` now **535 bytes**, sector-rounded boot headroom now **only 256 bytes** — genuinely tight, worth watching closely; this project has hit a silent boot failure before (the original `KERN_LOAD` overwrite bug) when this exact margin got too thin.
  - **Third hardware round (2026-07-16), same day, a real design flaw (not a small bug): `dir >dir1.txt` created the file but left it empty (0 bytes), `TYPE` on it just returned immediately.** Root cause: `_redir_setup` was called from `run_loop` *before* `prog_run`, and opened the output target via `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` — but `prog_run` *itself* also uses `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` moments later, to load the resolved command's own binary (`dir.exe`) into memory. `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf` are only genuinely idle *after* that load completes (confirmed: `_prog_finish_load` closes the load's own handle before `_prog_exec_now` ever jumps to the child's entry point) — not from the moment `_redir_setup` ran. So `prog_run`'s own internal `file_open` for `dir.exe` silently overwrote the `dir1.txt` FCB state `_redir_setup` had just written, before `DIR` ever got a chance to write a single byte through it. This invalidated the "single-direction redirect costs nothing, just reuse `prog_fcb`" design for *both* directions, not just output -- input-only redirect had the identical bug, just not yet exercised by testing.
    - **Fix**: moved the `_redir_setup` call from `run_loop` (kernel/kernel.asm) into `prog_run` itself (kernel/loader.asm), positioned right after `_prog_finish_load` succeeds and right before `_prog_exec_now` runs — the genuinely idle window. A failure there now shares `prog_run`'s existing `prun_err` exit (so `_prog_exec_now` never runs and the command is skipped, matching "fail the whole command"), at the minor cost of `run_loop` printing "Invalid program file." for a redirect failure too, rather than a distinct message — accepted rather than adding a second memory flag just to tell the two apart.
    - **A second, related issue caught during the same fix**: `_prog_finish_load` publishes `mem_top` to `LOADER_ARGS` (for the child's own heap use) *before* `_redir_setup` now runs — so if a dual-redirect's `_redir_reserve` shrinks `mem_top` afterward, the child would still see the stale, un-shrunk ceiling and could wander into the space just reserved for the second FCB. Fixed by having `_redir_reserve` also republish the reduced value at `LOADER_ARGS+2` whenever it succeeds, not just update the kernel's own `mem_top` variable.
    - **A third issue caught while reasoning through the fix**: `prog_run_shell`'s own rare fallback path (used when its cached `shell_elba`/`eoff` sector read fails validation) calls `prog_run` directly to reload `/bin/shell` itself by path — and since `prog_run` now always calls `_redir_setup` internally, that reload could spuriously "redirect" *itself* using stale `RUN_REDIR_OUT`/`RUN_REDIR_IN` values left over from the *previous* command (the shell hasn't tokenized the new line yet at that point, so the tokenizer's own top-of-pass reset — the second-round fix above — hasn't run either). Fixed by having `run_loop` also clear both relay slots immediately before calling `prog_run_shell`, closing the one window the tokenizer's own fix can't reach. The normal (non-fallback) shell reload path never touches `prog_run` at all, so it was never at risk.
    - Build-verified: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean on every touched file, all 36 jump-table slots + all 5 dispatcher targets cross-checked against real linked addresses, `krnboot.bin` unchanged, `make progs` 25/25. Margins: `Highest address` `$3bf4`, **524 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** — essentially unchanged from the previous round (this fix moved code between files rather than adding much net new). Awaiting a fresh hardware round.
- **`DATE`/`TIME` utilities — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-16, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Zero kernel changes** (chosen deliberately while kernel margins are tight post-redirect at 524/256 bytes — both commands are pure userland, built entirely on the already-existing `K_GETTOD`/`K_SETTOD`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG` jump-table passthroughs, no new slots). Named `DATE`/`TIME` rather than `SETDATE`/`SETTIME` (the user's own preference, since each command both displays and sets, combining both roles rather than being set-only) — `progs/date.asm`/`progs/time.asm`, `DATE [MM/DD/YYYY]` and `TIME [HH:MM[:SS]]` (seconds optional — omitting them leaves the current second field untouched); no argument just displays the current value in the same format `DIR` already uses for file timestamps. Both follow the user's own explicit requirement to minimize the K_GETTOD-to-K_SETTOD gap: every argument is parsed and range-validated (`DATE`: month 1-12, day 1-31, year 1972-2227 — the last so `year-1972` still fits `cur_time`'s single-byte on-disk encoding; `TIME`: hour 0-23, minute 0-59, second 0-59) *before* `K_GETTOD` is ever called, so the read-modify-write that follows is just a handful of memory-to-memory byte copies with no parsing/branching in between. Parsed fields are kept in **memory** scratch (`sd_month`/`sd_day`/`sd_year0`, `st_hour`/`st_min`/`st_sec`/`st_has_sec`), not registers, across the `K_GETTOD` call — matching `kernel/rtc.asm`'s own established practice for exactly the same known BIOS bug (`f_gettod` clobbers `RC`'s high byte despite being documented to preserve every register but `RF`). Both use a hand-rolled decimal parser (`parse_uint`, shift-based ×10 via `RD*8+RD*2`, no dependency on `f_mul16`) and the same `print2digit` helper `DIR` already established — deliberately not `f_astodt`/`f_dttoas`/`f_atoi`, none of which have ever been exercised anywhere in this codebase, so their exact register contracts are unconfirmed (same reasoning as avoiding `f_tty` in a loop, gotcha #14). **Verification**: both assemble clean (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep clean on both files, full clean `make`+`make progs` (25/25, 0 errors/warnings, kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$3bf4` since neither file touches the kernel, `krnboot.bin` unchanged at 1536 bytes). **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: bare `DATE`/`TIME` display correctly; a valid `DATE 07/16/2026` / `TIME 14:30:00` round-trip (set then bare-display readback matches); `TIME 14:30` (no seconds) leaves the real second field progressing normally rather than resetting to 0; each of the range-rejection cases (month 13, day 32, hour 24, minute 60, second 60, malformed separator) prints the usage message and leaves the clock unchanged.
- **`EDLIN` v1.1 enhancements — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-16, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Zero kernel changes** (edlin is fully userland, `progs/edlin.asm`, own private in-RAM buffer/line-table — none of these additions touch the kernel). Prompted by the user's own request for "edlin enhancements — search, etc.," with a FreeDOS edlin command reference supplied for ideas. Scoped to three additions this pass, chosen to be low-risk and to reuse existing primitives rather than duplicate buffer/line-table-shifting logic; `R`(eplace)/`C`(opy)/`M`(ove)/`T`(ransfer file)/the `?` confirm-prompt flag remain deliberately deferred, same as before:
  - **`L` now takes an optional `[n]`/`[n1,n2]` range** (bare `[n]` lists just that one line, matching `D`'s own single-number convention; no range still lists the whole file — there's no paging UI on this hardware yet, so that's more useful than DOS's classic 23-line default window). `ed_list_loop`'s termination check now compares against a new `ed_list_last` instead of the hardcoded `ed_line_count`.
  - **Bare line number `<n>` now actually implements real edlin's own core behavior — display the line, then prompt for a replacement** (previously it only displayed; there was no way to edit a line's content at all). Enter alone leaves the line unchanged; any real text replaces it. Implemented deliberately as **insert-then-delete, not delete-then-insert**: the new text is inserted before the old line first, and only if that succeeds is the old line (now shifted one position down) deleted — so a "buffer full" failure leaves the original line completely intact instead of losing it.
  - **New `S[text]` command**: case-sensitive literal substring search over an optional `[n]`/`[n1,n2]` range (default: whole file; a bare `[n]` means "from n to the end," unlike `L`/the bare-number edit, where a lone number means "just that line" — for a search, "start from here" reads more naturally). Stops at and displays the first matching line, sets `cur_line` to it; "Not found." if nothing matches in range. New leaf routine `ed_line_contains` (naive outer/inner substring scan, no calls internally, everything reloaded from memory before use matching this file's own established defensive style) plus `f_ltrim` reused to allow both `"S text"` and `"Stext"`.
  - **`D`'s own delete mechanics factored out into a callable `ed_delete_range`** (Args: `ed_d_first`/`ed_d_last`, pre-validated by the caller) so the new single-line-edit path can reuse the exact same buffer/line-table shifting logic instead of duplicating it — `D`'s own command handler is otherwise byte-for-byte unchanged, just restructured from an inline fallthrough into a call+return (the old `ed_d_do` label's body is now `ed_delete_range`, ending in `rtn` instead of printing "Deleted." and jumping back to the prompt; `ed_cmd_d` now does that print itself, after the call).
  - **Verification**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep clean, full clean `make progs` (25/25, kernel unaffected). The two genuinely new, non-mechanical pieces (`ed_line_contains`'s substring scan, and the `S`/`L` range-defaulting logic) were independently re-implemented in Python and cross-checked against 14 substring test cases (including empty haystack, needle-longer-than-haystack, overlapping-prefix patterns) and 8+6 range-defaulting cases for `S`/`L` respectively (no-range, single-number, two-number, each error case) — all passed, matching this project's own established practice (from the argv/argc tokenizer's two hardware-found bugs) of independently verifying new algorithmic logic rather than trusting a clean assemble alone. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: ranged `L` (single-line and two-number forms, plus the out-of-range error case); bare-number edit (both the Enter-leaves-unchanged case and an actual replacement, ideally including one that grows the line significantly); `S` with and without a range, a found and a not-found case, and specifically a search text containing a leading space (`S foo` vs `Sfoo`) to confirm the `f_ltrim` handling.
- **⚠️ ONGOING BUG HUNT (2026-07-16, redir2.txt): `ECHO`'s redirected APPEND output repeats its first argument's text instead of printing each argv token — TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC ADDED, awaiting a hardware round.** `echo "This is a test" > test3.txt` (truncate, single arg) worked correctly. `echo "This is a test" a b c 'd e' >> test3.txt` (append, 5 args) appended `"This is a test"` repeated 6 times, joined by single real spaces, instead of `"This is a test a b c d e"` — confirmed by counting bytes precisely (the pasted transcript's mid-line wrap is just terminal width, not real file content: 6×14-byte copies + 5 one-byte separators = 89 bytes exactly). The prior hardware round (`dir /cfg >test.txt` then `dir /cfg >>test.txt`, both truncate and append) looked "reasonable" — meaningful because `DIR` also interleaves `K_MSG`/`K_INMSG` heavily (confirmed via grep), so simple multi-write append itself isn't the whole story; the one thing genuinely new to `ECHO`'s test is printing content that comes directly from **argv** (pointers into `LINE_BUF`) rather than freshly-read disk data.
  - **Extensive static tracing found no bug**: `file_write`'s append/growth logic (`kernel/file.asm`), `file_open`'s mode-2 end-of-file repositioning, `_redir_msg`/`_redir_type`/`_redir_inmsg`/`_redir_setup` (`kernel/redir.asm`, including their push/pop register protection), the shell tokenizer's `>`/`>>` operator parsing and its in-place write-cursor (`RD`) mechanics (`progs/shell.asm`), and `echo.asm`'s own loop (fully memory-resident state, no register-survival assumptions, provably correct on a fresh read) all traced clean. Also checked for an address collision between `LINE_BUF`/`RUN_ARGV_TABLE` and `prog_fcb`/`prog_iobuf`/`redir_*`/`kim_*`/`kir_*`/`path_buf` by decoding real linked symbol addresses — none found (`prog_fcb=$311c`, `prog_iobuf=$313c`, `redir_out_handle=$3be5`, `kim_start=$3bea`, `path_buf=$0f45`, all well clear of `LINE_BUF=$0080-$00ff` and `RUN_ARGV_TABLE=$3dda`).
  - **Diagnostic added** (`kernel/redir.asm`, `_redir_msg`'s `rmsg_scandone`, marked `; TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC`): prints `"<len> C=<first char>\r\n"` to the **real console** via direct `f_uintout`/`f_msg` calls (deliberately NOT `K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`, which are the redirect-aware dispatchers under investigation — using them here would land the diagnostic text in the very file being debugged, or recurse). `RC` (byte count) and `R7` (string start, needed by the real `file_write` call right after) are stashed to new `dbg_saved_rc`/`dbg_saved_r7` and reloaded afterward, since `f_uintout`/`f_msg`'s own clobber footprint on them isn't confirmed (gotcha #8). New data fields `dbg_saved_rc`/`dbg_saved_r7`/`dbg_first_char`/`dbg_buf` (own `extrn`/`public` pair, gotcha #6, same-file cross-proc). This will show, for each of `ECHO`'s 5 `_redir_msg` calls, exactly what length/first-character it's being asked to write — directly bisecting "upstream of `_redir_msg`" (would show correct/varying values) from "downstream, in `file_write`/disk" (would show the same call arguments 5 times despite correct-looking console output).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, `K_MSG`'s jump-table entry decoded and confirmed still resolves to `_redir_msg`'s real address (`$3914`), `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), `make progs` clean. Margins genuinely tight now: `Highest address` `$3c66` (+114 bytes over the prior redirect-only build), **410 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** (unchanged — the extra bytes didn't push into a new sector).
  - **Diagnostic result (2026-07-16, redir4.txt)**: `echo "This is a test" a b c 'd e' >test1.txt` printed `14 C=T` six times — every `_redir_msg` call got the SAME (wrong) length/first-char, confirming argv content really is duplicated, not a `file_write`/disk issue. **Critical new fact from the same round (time1.txt): `date 07/16/2026` (a single-argument command with NO redirect operator at all) also fails** — `"Usage: DATE [MM/DD/YYYY]"` for a correctly-formatted date. This rules out the redirect dispatchers as the root cause entirely (nothing about `_redir_*` is even reachable in that code path) and points at the shell's core argv-building/tokenizer instead.
  - **Exhaustive re-verification found the source, as written, should work**: re-read `progs/shell.asm`'s whole tokenizer fresh, decoded the actual assembled `.lst` bytes for the entire `tok_check_end`→`tok_char`→`tok_special`/`tok_ordinary`→`tok_end_token` loop instruction-by-instruction (every opcode matches expectations, no missing branch, no D-clobber), and independently re-implemented the whole state machine in Python from that fresh read — all three (source, real bytes, simulation) agree the algorithm should produce correct, distinct argv entries for both failing commands (simulation predicts exactly the observed 6-call count for the echo case, with correct varying content, and `['date', '07/16/2026']` for the date case). Also re-confirmed `progs/date.asm`'s own parse/validate logic against a Python simulation of "07/16/2026" — passes cleanly, matching the identical, already-hardware-confirmed argv[1]-extraction pattern used in `cd.asm`.
  - **Stale-binary hypothesis RULED OUT (2026-07-16, redir5.txt) — user did a full clean `bin/` redeploy and re-tested.** `args a b c`/`args` with complex quoting both correct; unredirected `echo` with the same args also correct; the SAME `echo ... >file` redirected still broken (repeats the first arg) — since `echo.asm`'s code is identical either way, this narrowed the bug to something in the redirect path specifically, not the tokenizer or `echo.asm` itself.
  - **Bug A (ECHO) ROOT-CAUSED AND FIXED (2026-07-16, redir6.txt).** `args a b c >file` + `type file` came back **completely correct** — proving the corruption is NOT generic to "any redirected program," ruling out `_redir_setup` and confirming it's something specific to `echo.asm`'s own call pattern vs. `args.asm`'s. Comparing the two side by side: `args.asm` calls `K_MSG` then `K_INMSG` every iteration (content always printed before the separator); `echo.asm` calls `K_INMSG` (separator) THEN `K_MSG` (content) for every argument after the first, and its `echo_get_arg` label computes `&argv[echo_i]` via `shl16 rd` immediately after that `K_INMSG` call, relying on `RD` (holding `echo_i`) surviving it. **Found the bug**: `_redir_inmsg` (`kernel/redir.asm`) protected `RF`/`RC`/`R9`/`RA` across itself but NOT `RD` — and its redirected path calls `file_write`, whose own header explicitly documents `RD` as scratch ("R7/R8/R9/RD are scratch, recomputed fresh each iteration"). The first argument always printed correctly (no separator/no `K_INMSG` call precedes it, so `RD` was never at risk); every argument after that got corrupted once `RD` came back clobbered from `file_write`'s own internal use of it — and since `file_write`'s final `RD` value is apparently consistent across calls, every corrupted argument resolved to the SAME wrong address rather than a different one each time. Silent when not redirected, since `_redir_inmsg`'s console (`kim_console`) path uses a byte-at-a-time `f_type` loop that never touches `RD` at all — explaining why this survived every earlier round of testing until real hardware exercised a multi-argument `ECHO` through actual redirection. **Fixed** by adding `push rd`/`pop rd` to `_redir_inmsg`, in the same position/LIFO order as its other four protected registers — matches the exact pattern already used to fix this same class of bug in the other four dispatchers during the second hardware round. The temporary `_redir_msg` console diagnostic (added while chasing this) has been fully removed now that the real root cause turned out to be in `_redir_inmsg`, not `_redir_msg`. **Verified**: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, all 5 redirect-aware jump-table slots (`K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`/`K_INPUTL`/`K_READ`) decoded from the real `kernel.bin` and confirmed pointing at their correct dispatcher addresses, `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), `make progs` clean. Margins after removing the diagnostic and adding the one-register fix: `Highest address` `$3bfa`, **518 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** (net cost of the real fix alone is ~6 bytes vs. the pre-diagnostic baseline). **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-16/17, redir7.txt)** — user confirmed "output redirection and appending are working now." This closes out the whole I/O redirection bug hunt: all three hardware rounds (uninitialized relay slots, all-5-dispatchers register preservation, `prog_fcb` reuse timing, and now this `RD`-across-`_redir_inmsg` gap) are resolved.
  - **Input redirection (`<`) also exercised for the first time this round, via `edlin test2.txt < create_file.in`, and worked** — created a real multi-line file via edlin's insert mode fed entirely from a redirected input file (`i` / 4 lines of content / `.` / `e`), confirmed via `dir`/`type` afterward. One design question raised by the user from this test: `K_INPUTL`'s redirected path does NOT echo the characters it reads to the console, so the on-screen transcript only shows prompts with nothing typed after them. **Decided (2026-07-17): leave it as-is** — the user's own call, since not echoing matches both Linux and MS-DOS convention (echo is a terminal-driver property applied to live keystrokes, not something that happens for file-sourced input).
  - **`NUL` device support — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-17, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Prompted directly by the `K_INPUTL`-echo discussion above: the user's real goal (feeding a scripted sequence into `edlin` and discarding/no-oping the parts that don't matter) is better served by a real null device than by echoing input. `>NUL` discards whatever a program writes; `<NUL` returns EOF immediately, matching MS-DOS's own convention for both. Deliberately scoped to the *current* redirect architecture rather than the bigger Unix-style fd-0/1/2 redesign the user also raised in the same conversation (a `K_READ`/`K_INPUTL`/`K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`-as-file-I/O unification, with special FCB "kinds" for console/null/file) — agreed with the user to defer that: it's motivated by future C-library support that doesn't exist yet, and the current design only just finished four full rounds of hardware bug-fixing this same session, so replacing it right as it stabilizes would be premature. `NUL` needed zero shell/tokenizer changes — it's just an ordinary redirect-target token, indistinguishable from a real filename until `_redir_setup` inspects it.
    - **New shared helper `_is_nul_device`** (`kernel/redir.asm`): does the string at `RF` spell `"NUL"` case-insensitive, with nothing following (rejects a prefix match like `"NULfoo"`), via the same `ani $DF` uppercase-fold idiom the shell's own drive-letter check already uses (verified safe the same way: only `'N'`/`'n'` alias to `'N'` under that mask).
    - **`_redir_setup`** calls it for both `RUN_REDIR_OUT` and `RUN_REDIR_IN` before ever calling `file_open`. A `NUL` target skips the real open entirely and sets a new one-byte flag (`redir_out_null`/`redir_in_null`) alongside the existing `redir_*_active` flag, instead of opening `prog_fcb`/a dynamically-reserved pair. **A real edge case caught and fixed during design, before ever assembling**: the dual-vs-single decision for input redirect (`rs_in_open`, "does output redirect ALSO need `prog_fcb`, forcing input into the dynamically-reserved pair instead?") originally checked only `redir_out_active` — but an output redirect to `NUL` sets that flag too, despite never touching `prog_fcb`. Left unfixed, `cmd <realfile >NUL` would have wastefully (and pointlessly, for zero benefit) triggered the expensive dynamic stack-relocation reservation. Fixed by also checking `redir_out_null`: only a real, non-`NUL` output redirect counts as "using `prog_fcb`" for this decision.
    - **The 3 output dispatchers** (`_redir_type`/`_redir_msg`/`_redir_inmsg`) each gained one check, right after confirming redirection is active: if `redir_out_null` is set, skip the real `file_write` entirely and report success — discarding the byte/string/message with no new failure mode. **The 2 input dispatchers** (`_redir_read`/`_redir_inputl`) each gained the same shape of check, but branch straight to their own *already-existing* EOF-handling code instead of needing anything new: `_redir_read` jumps to `rrd_eof` (matching `f_read`'s own contract, `D`=0), `_redir_inputl` sets `kir_count`=0 and jumps to `kir_term`, which was already known to correctly handle "an empty line" — matching MS-DOS's own "`NUL` as input returns EOF immediately" behavior for free, with zero new EOF logic required.
    - **`_redir_teardown` and `_redir_setup`'s own error-unwind path (`rs_err_maybe_close_out`) both needed a matching fix**: since a `NUL` redirect never opens a real FCB, calling `file_close` on `redir_out_handle`/`redir_in_handle` for it would operate on a stale/bogus `fd_table` index. Both now check the null flag first and skip `file_close` when it's set — but still unconditionally clear both the active and null flags either way, so neither can leak stale into the next command's own `_redir_setup` call.
    - **Verified**: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, all 5 redirect-aware jump-table slots re-decoded from the real `kernel.bin` and confirmed pointing at their correct (now-shifted) dispatcher addresses, `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), `make progs` clean. Traced all 6 combinations of {output: none/real/NUL} × {input: none/real/NUL} by hand against the actual control flow to confirm each one takes the right path (in particular the dual-vs-single fix above, and that `NUL`+`NUL` together never touches any FCB at all). **Margins, worth watching closely**: `Highest address` `$3cf9`, **263 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** — this feature cost ~255 bytes, notably more than the original ~50-150 byte estimate (the dual-vs-single edge-case fix and the teardown/error-unwind changes both added real code beyond the minimal per-dispatcher checks). The `PROG_BASE` margin in particular is now the tightest it's been all session — worth a size-reduction pass before adding anything else non-trivial to the kernel. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: `dir >NUL` produces no output and no error; `type nonexistent.txt >NUL` (redirects the "File not found." message, via `K_INMSG`, to the null device) still reports success/no visible output; `sometool <NUL` gets an immediate empty/EOF result (e.g. via `edlin somefile <NUL` immediately hitting `K_INPUTL` EOF at the very first prompt); a plain unredirected command immediately after each, to confirm the null flags don't leak; and ideally a genuine `cmd <realfile >NUL` to exercise the dual-vs-single fix specifically.
  - **`K_INPUTL` gained a real, defined EOF signal via `DF` — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-17, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Found by the user testing `edlin file <NUL`: input redirection from an exhausted source (`<NUL`, or a real file already fully read) has NO WAY to tell the caller "there's nothing left" — `_redir_inputl` always wrote an empty line and returned normally, indistinguishable from a genuine blank line in the file. `edlin`'s own command loop treats an empty line as "re-prompt," so this was a REAL infinite loop (`<NUL` specifically hits it on the very first read), and `edlin`'s insert-mode loop treats a blank line as real content to insert, so it would have silently inserted blank lines until the buffer filled rather than looping forever, but still clearly wrong. Before this fix, `DF`'s value after `K_INPUTL` was undefined/unused everywhere in this codebase ("every existing caller ignores `DF`"), so redefining it is safe for existing behavior.
    - **New contract**: `DF=0` means `RF`'s buffer holds a real line (possibly empty — a blank Enter at a live console, or a genuinely blank line in a redirected file); `DF=1` means the redirected input source is exhausted. Console (non-redirected) input always reports `DF=0` — forced explicitly via a real `call f_inputl` (changed from the previous tail `lbr f_inputl`, since a tail-jump can't let the caller override whatever `DF` the BIOS itself leaves, and `f_inputl`'s own `DF` behavior isn't confirmed anywhere in this codebase). `f_inputl` has no `R6`-based inline-message mechanism like `f_inmsg` does, so switching from a tail `lbr` to a real `call`+explicit `rtn` is safe.
    - **The tricky part, and the reason a genuine blank line can't just be inferred from an empty result**: `_redir_inputl`'s internal byte-accumulation counter (`kir_count`) reaching 0 happens for THREE different reasons — a real empty line (two consecutive newlines), a partial final line with no accumulated bytes before hitting real EOF, and `<NUL`'s own immediate EOF — only the last two of which are true "EOF." Solved with a separate explicit flag (reusing `R7`, free scratch from that point in the routine onward) set at each of the four paths into the shared `kir_term` exit: the "buffer full" and "real LF found" paths set it to "not EOF" (real content, even if empty); `kir_null_eof` and `kir_eof`-with-zero-accumulated-bytes set it to "EOF"; `kir_eof`-with-nonzero-accumulated-bytes (a partial final line) is redirected to the SAME "not EOF" path as a real line, matching the pre-existing "return a final line without a trailing newline" behavior this routine already had. `kir_term` itself sets `DF` from that flag right before returning.
    - **Verified two ways before touching any caller**: build (clean assemble, D-clobber sweep) and an independent Python simulation of the whole state machine covering 5 scenarios (`<NUL`, a real 2-line file with no trailing newline, a file with a genuine blank line in the middle, a fully empty file, and a line that exceeds the buffer length) — all 5 produced exactly the expected `(line, is_eof)` pairs, including the critical case (a real blank line correctly reported as `DF=0`, not confused with EOF).
    - **`edlin`'s three `K_INPUTL` call sites updated** (`progs/edlin.asm`, pure userland, zero kernel-adjacent risk beyond the `redir.asm` change itself): the main command prompt (`ed_cmdloop`) now checks `DF` and jumps to a new `ed_eof_quit` (prints a closing newline, exits with code 0 — same "abandon unsaved edits" semantics as a confirmed `Q`, since there's no live user left to ask for a Y/N confirmation); the insert-mode prompt (`ed_i_loop`) jumps to the existing `ed_i_done` on EOF, treating it the same as the `.` terminator rather than inserting blank lines; the bare-number single-line-edit prompt was deliberately left unchanged — an EOF there already falls through to "leave the line unchanged," which loops back to `ed_cmdloop`, whose own check then catches the exhausted input on its very next read (one extra bounce, not a second copy of the same logic).
    - **`kernel_api.inc`'s own `K_INPUTL` documentation updated** to describe the new contract, since that's the file programs actually reference.
    - **Verified**: clean assemble on both files, D-clobber sweep clean, `make progs` clean. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: `edlin file <NUL` now exits cleanly instead of looping (the original bug report); a real `<file` with a genuine blank line in it is still handled correctly by both `edlin`'s insert mode and its command loop; a real file that runs out mid-script still works as before (this project's own established `test.bat`-equivalent scenario for `edlin`, from the very first successful `<file` round).
  - **Kernel size-reduction pass (search-loop consolidation, completing what the 2026-07-12 pass deferred) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-17, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Fixes a genuine boot-blocking regression.** After the `NUL` device + `K_INPUTL` EOF-signaling work above, `kernel.bin` grew to 31 sectors — 256 bytes PAST what `KERN_LOAD`/`PROG_BASE` (merged at `$3E00`) has room for before `krnboot`'s own load loop would silently overwrite itself mid-boot. Caught by this project's own standing margin-check discipline before ever being flashed. Rather than move `KERN_LOAD` (a boot-code change, the highest-stakes category in this project, needing a full `bin/` redeploy), the user chose to finally do the search-loop consolidation identified and deliberately deferred back in the 2026-07-12 pass — `_find_dirent` at the time only covered 4 of 8 "must find" call sites (`file_delete`, `dir_remove`'s locate-target, `file_rename`'s locate-old-name, `file_stat`), explicitly leaving `file_open`'s own search, `dir_create`'s collision check, and `file_rename`'s new-name collision check undone because those are "must NOT find, but still need the resolved parent cluster on EITHER outcome" callers, which `_find_dirent`'s simple `DF=0/1` contract couldn't serve without a real design pass.
    - **New shared routine `_scan_dir_for_name(RD=already-resolved parent cluster, RF=name to search for)`**: the `dir_open`+`dir_read`-loop+`f_strcmp` search core, factored out of `_find_dirent`'s own original body. Returns `DF=0` (found — `file_dirent` populated, `RD`=parent reloaded for convenience) or `DF=1` (not found — `dir_eptr`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_clust`/`dir_sect` left describing the `$00` terminator, ready for `_file_create` to insert directly). Takes the search name as an explicit `RF` argument (rather than assuming it's already in the shared `fo_name` field, as the original `_find_dirent` did) specifically so `file_rename`'s new-name check — which searches for `ren_new_name`, not `fo_name` — can reuse it too; internally it stashes whatever's passed into `fo_name` regardless, so existing callers that already keep their search name there see zero behavior change, and `file_rename`'s own downstream `ren_insert` step (which redundantly re-derives `fo_name = ren_new_name` right after) still works, just now redundantly — left alone deliberately, to minimize touched surface in a proc this critical.
    - **`_find_dirent` itself shrank to a thin wrapper**: `path_resolve` + its own error handling, then one `call _scan_dir_for_name` + a plain `rtn` (its `DF` result already matches `_find_dirent`'s own "must find" contract with zero translation needed). Zero behavior change for its 4 existing callers — confirmed by construction, not just testing, since the extracted scan-loop body is byte-for-byte the same code that used to live inline.
    - **`file_open`, `dir_create`, and `file_rename`'s new-name check** each replaced their own hand-rolled `dir_open`+scan-loop+`f_strcmp` block with `call _scan_dir_for_name` + a single `lbdf`. Each site's own "found" and "not found" *handling* code (the parts that actually do something with the result) was left completely untouched — only the search mechanics were consolidated, minimizing risk in code this central (`file_open` runs on every single file access in the system).
    - **Verification, held to a higher bar given the stakes**: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, proc/endp balanced (22/22). Given the risk of trusting a `.lst` file's own per-proc byte offsets after `-r` relaxation shrinks branches (which renumbers everything, exactly the mistake that produced 3 false mismatches on the first attempt here) — verification instead searched the real, final `kernel.bin` directly for the literal 3-byte `call $<_scan_dir_for_name's real address>` encoding, found **exactly 4 occurrences** (matching the 4 source-level call sites, no more, no less), each falling within its expected proc's real address range. Also hand-traced every one of the 4 call sites plus both new/refactored routines fresh against the current file, confirming no register clobber crosses the new call boundaries incorrectly (in particular, that `RD`/`RF` genuinely survive from `path_resolve` into `_scan_dir_for_name` unclobbered at each site, and that each site's own "not found" handler reads everything it needs fresh from memory rather than depending on any register `_scan_dir_for_name` leaves behind). `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), `make progs` clean (25/25).
    - **Result: 83 bytes saved, `Highest address` `$3cc6`.** Smaller than the original ~250-350 byte estimate for this class of consolidation, because that estimate covered all 8 call sites — 4 were already consolidated in the 2026-07-12 pass, and the new shared routine itself has real code cost that eats into the remaining 4 sites' savings. **Margins restored to positive: 314 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** (back from the -256-byte overflow this pass exists to fix) — the kernel is boot-safe again, though margins remain worth watching; there's likely more available via an `equ`-based scratch-coalescing idea (sidestepping Asm/02's broken relocatable-label-aliasing bug, gotcha #15, by sharing one real field directly across mutually-exclusive call sites instead of trying to alias a second name onto it) discussed but not yet attempted at the time this note was written. **STATUS UPDATE (2026-07-26): this WAS subsequently attempted and shipped — see `_shared_scratch` in `kernel/file.asm` (a 9-byte block, `#define`-aliased across `fa_*`/`fdel_*`/`dcr_*`/`drm_*`/`ren_*`, 5 of file.asm's own mutually-exclusive per-routine scratch groups) and gotcha #15's own updated writeup for the full mechanism. One group was NOT folded in and remains genuinely open: `file_seek`'s own `fsk_*` scratch (16 bytes, `fsk_whence`/`fsk_off_hi`/`fsk_off_lo`/`fsk_fcb`/`fsk_target`/`fsk_boff`/`fsk_cluster_idx`/`fsk_sector_in_clust`) is still separately, permanently allocated — confirmed mutually exclusive with the other 5 (file_seek only ever calls the same leaf helpers, `_switch_drive`/`fat_get`, never `file_open`/`file_delete`/`dir_create`/`dir_remove`/`file_rename`) but never folded into `_shared_scratch`, likely simply not revisited once the block's own size need (9 bytes, set by `ren_*`) was already met. Folding it in would grow the shared block to 16 bytes (the new max across 6 groups) for a net saving of roughly 9 bytes — real, low-risk, using an already-proven mechanism, but a much smaller opportunity than this note originally implied remained available; don't assume there's a large undiscovered pool here. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming `file_open`'s own core behavior is unaffected (open/read/write/append/create across all the existing hardware-confirmed test programs — `TYPE`/`WTEST`/`ATEST`/`COPY`), plus `MD`/`REN` specifically (the two other refactored call sites) — this is the highest-stakes verification target of anything this session, since `file_open` runs on every file access in the system.
  - **Bug B (DATE/TIME) — ROOT-CAUSED AND FIXED (2026-07-17).** The diagnostic added in the prior round (redir7.txt) was completely decisive: `date 07/16/2026` printed `M=7 SEP=47` (correct — `47` is `/`'s ASCII value) then `D=18 Y=3486` (both wrong — should be `16`/`2026`); `time 12:00:01` printed `H=14 M=0 S=1 HS=1` (hour wrong, should be `12`). **Real bug in `parse_uint`'s own multiply-by-10 logic**, present identically in both `progs/date.asm` and `progs/time.asm` (hand-copied code): the ×10 accumulation is built as `RD*8 + RD*2`, computed via `mov r8,rd` + three `shl16 r8` (`R8=RD*8`, correct) followed by what was supposed to be `RD*2` via a single `shl16 rd` — but the code had **two** `shl16 rd` calls, computing `RD*4` instead of `RD*2`. The routine therefore silently computed `value*12+digit` instead of `value*10+digit` for every call. Invisible whenever the accumulator is still `0` going into the multiply (any number's first digit, or any digit following a leading `0`) since `0*12 == 0*10 == 0` — exactly why `"07"` (month) always parsed correctly while `"16"` (day, first real multiply with a nonzero accumulator) and `"2026"` did not. Confirmed by hand: `1*12+6=18` (matches `D=18`), `((((0*12+2)*12+0)*12+2)*12+6)=3486` (matches `Y=3486`), `1*12+2=14` (matches `H=14`) — all three diagnostic values reproduced exactly by the buggy formula. **Root cause of why two separate verification passes both missed this**: the earlier byte-level `.lst` decode confirmed the assembled bytes matched the *source* faithfully, but never questioned whether the source's own shift count was arithmetically correct; the earlier Python re-simulation modeled the *intended* `value*10+digit` formula directly (using real decimal multiplication) rather than translating the actual `shl16` sequence, so it could never have caught a shift-count bug in principle. **Fixed** by removing the extra `shl16 rd` in both files (one shift = ×2, matching the original intent). Independently re-verified this time by literally translating the real shift sequence into Python (`rd = (rd<<1)`, not `rd*2` as a formula) for both the buggy and fixed versions — the buggy sim reproduces all three observed hardware values exactly, the fixed sim produces correct results for every test case (`07`→7, `16`→16, `2026`→2026, `12`→12, `00`→0, `01`→1). Diagnostics fully removed from both files (confirmed via grep). **Verified**: both assemble clean, D-clobber sweep clean, full `make progs` clean (kernel untouched, this is pure userland), binaries shrank back down (`date`: 453 bytes, `time`: 472 bytes, both smaller than the diagnostic builds and smaller than the original pre-diagnostic builds too, since the fix itself removes one instruction). **Not yet hardware-confirmed** — needs a round re-running `date 07/16/2026`/`time 12:00:01` (and ideally a couple of other two-digit-starting-nonzero values) to confirm the real fix.
- **Path support**: `kernel/path.asm`'s `path_resolve` (exposed to programs as `K_PATH_RESOLVE`) resolves a `/`-separated path to (parent directory cluster, final component). A leading `/` resolves from the root; otherwise from a caller-supplied base cluster (`cur_dir` for `file_open`'s internal call, `K_GETCURDIR`'s result for `CD`). `.` and `..` need no special-casing — they're real FAT directory entries, same as any other name. The final component is deliberately NOT looked up by `path_resolve` itself — the caller decides what to do with it (`file_open` searches for a file and rejects directories; `CD` searches for a directory). An empty final component (path was `""`, `"/"`, or ended in `/`) means the resolved parent cluster itself IS the target: `file_open` treats this as an error (can't open a directory as a file), `CD` skips straight to `K_SETCURDIR`. The input path is copied into a kernel-owned scratch buffer (`path_buf`) before parsing so the caller's own string is never mutated; the returned final-component pointer points into that buffer — same "raw pointer into kernel scratch" convention as the RA command-tail pointer at program entry, valid until the next `K_PATH_RESOLVE` call. **Confirmed working on hardware** (`TYPE`/`CD`/`DIR` with nested paths, short and long names, multiple directories). `DIR` also gained an optional path argument on top of this (lists a directory without changing into it), and `PWD` does the reverse walk (cluster → full path, by finding "my own name" in the parent's listing at each level up to root) — both confirmed working too.
- **Heap manager libraries (`lib/`) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-17, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED (built while the user was away for a couple of days, no hardware access at all for this round).** Two general-purpose dynamic-memory libraries for userland programs, requested as "a change of pace" — pure userland, zero kernel involvement, so no margin impact whatsoever. `lib/` is a new directory: files here have **no `org PROG_BASE`, no EDF header, no entry point** — they're not standalone programs, they're assembled separately (`lib/heap_bump.prg`/`lib/heap_malloc.prg`) and linked alongside whichever program wants them, the exact same `proc`/`endp` + `extrn`/`public` multi-module mechanism the kernel's own `KOBJ` list already uses. A calling program just does `extrn heap_alloc` etc. and calls it like any other routine. Both read their bounds via an explicit `init(base, top)` call rather than reading `LOADER_ARGS` themselves — keeps the libraries generic (not hardwired to the program-loading convention), with the caller responsible for reading `LOADER_ARGS` (word0=`mem_base`, word1=`mem_top`, both **inclusive** bounds — `mem_top` is documented as "last usable RAM byte," not one-past-the-end) and passing it through. Neither library makes any kernel/BIOS call at all, so none of this project's "does a register survive a call" gotchas apply to a caller using them — ordinary straight-line register-clobber rules only.
  - **`lib/heap_bump.asm` — a minimal bump/arena allocator.** One pointer (`bump_next`) that only ever moves forward within `[base, top]`; `bump_alloc(RC=size)` returns the current pointer and advances it, full stop — no header, no bookkeeping, no way to free an individual block. Reclaiming space is scoped instead: `bump_mark()`/`bump_release(mark)` snapshot and later rewind the pointer, freeing everything allocated since in one shot (a classic stack-discipline pattern — mark, do temporary work, release, zero per-object cost) — or `bump_init` again, to reclaim the whole arena. Trades away general-purpose `free()` entirely for being about as small and fast as an allocator can be; the right tool for a program with simple, scoped, or never-freed allocation needs.
  - **`lib/heap_malloc.asm` — a general-purpose `malloc`/`free`, for when allocations need to be freed in arbitrary order.** Classic design: address-ordered singly-linked free list, first-fit search, splitting a too-large block on alloc (unless the leftover remainder is smaller than `HEAP_MIN_SPLIT`=4 bytes — a free block's own size+next header — in which case the whole block is handed over rather than leaving an unusably tiny sliver), and coalescing adjacent free blocks in **both directions** on free. Every block (free or allocated) has a 2-byte `SIZE` header; a **free** block additionally stores its `NEXT` pointer inside its own usable space (costs nothing extra, since that space isn't holding live data) — only allocated blocks pay the 2-byte header tax.
  - **Algorithm verified in Python *before* writing any 1802 assembly** — the free-list/split/coalesce logic was independently re-implemented and stress-tested: basic round-trips, FIFO-order and reverse-order alloc-then-free-everything, **20 randomized-order trials** (confirming full coalescing back to exactly one block every single time), exhaustion+recovery, exact-fit (must NOT split), and remainder-below-`HEAP_MIN_SPLIT` (must NOT split). All passed before any assembly was written — this validates the algorithm itself, independent of 1802 register-level concerns.
  - **The 1802 implementation was then hand-traced against that same Python model, register by register**, since `heap_malloc.asm`'s three routines make **zero subroutine calls internally** (pure straight-line/branching memory manipulation) — meaning the usual "does a register survive a call" question doesn't apply here at all; the only real risk is a register getting clobbered across a *branch* within the same routine, which the D-clobber sweep script doesn't check (it only catches immediately-adjacent instruction pairs). The trickiest part, matching what the Python model already established: `kir_count`/block-size reaching a particular value happens for multiple different reasons (a real empty/short line vs. true end-of-data), and the backward-coalesce step specifically **re-reads the freed block's SIZE/NEXT fields fresh from memory** rather than trusting a register that the forward-coalesce step (run moments earlier, in the same call) might already have invalidated — the same "don't trust a register across code that might have mutated the thing it describes" defensive pattern this project's own gotcha #10 already established the hard way, applied here by design rather than learned the hard way a second time.
  - **A real bug was caught and fixed during this same review, before ever assembling**: the first draft's `heap_free` call sites in the test programs (see below) used `mov rf, mt_pN` / `lda rf` / `phi rf` — using `RF` as *both* the staging pointer for reading the stored value from memory *and* the destination register for that value, simultaneously. `lda rf` advances `RF` to point at the *second* byte of the stored word before `phi rf` runs, so the write landed on a garbage combination of (real high byte) + (leftover pointer low byte) instead of the real value. Caught by a systematic script scan across every new file, matching this session's established practice for a bug class that recurs in *this project specifically* (gotcha #4's whole family) — not just eyeballing it. Fixed by using `RB` as the staging pointer and `RF` as the clean destination throughout, matching the pattern already used correctly elsewhere in the very same files.
  - **Two dedicated test programs**, since there's no debugger and no hardware access for this round: `progs/bumptest.asm` (4 checks: monotonic allocation addresses, mark/release reuse, clean exhaustion failure) and `progs/malloctest.asm` (11 checks: basic allocation, fill-and-verify data integrity across 3 adjacent blocks — proving no overlap — free+realloc slot reuse, neighbor-integrity re-checks after that churn, and — the check that actually *exercises* coalescing rather than just declaring it — freeing three adjacent blocks in an order specifically chosen to hit forward coalescing, then a combined backward+forward 3-way merge in one call, then proving the whole heap is one contiguous block again by successfully allocating its *full original size* back at the *original base address*). Both read the real `[mem_base..mem_top]` from `LOADER_ARGS`, print `PASS`/`FAIL` per check plus a final summary — deliberately using only already-hardware-confirmed primitives (`K_INMSG`, `K_MSG`, `f_uintout`) rather than `f_hexout4` for address output, since `f_hexout4` has **zero prior usage anywhere in this codebase** and its exact register contract is as unconfirmed as `K_SETTOD` was before this session exercised it for the first time — no reason to introduce a second untested BIOS routine into a test program meant to be trusted at face value while the user is away.
  - **Build system**: `lib/` is a new top-level directory with its own `Makefile` assembly rules (matching the kernel's own per-file pattern). `progs/bumptest.asm`/`progs/malloctest.asm` need a library `.prg` linked in, unlike every other single-file `progs/*.asm` — handled via two explicit `bin/bumptest`/`bin/malloctest` rules that override the generic `bin/%: progs/%.prg` pattern rule for just those two targets (confirmed working: GNU Make always prefers an explicit target-specific rule over a pattern rule that also matches, verified by the actual build log showing the correct multi-file link commands running).
  - **Verified**: both libraries and both test programs assemble clean, D-clobber sweep clean on all four files, full clean `make progs` (29/29 including the two new binaries), `bin/bumptest`/`bin/malloctest` both link with zero unresolved symbols, kernel completely untouched (`Highest address` identical before/after, `krnboot.bin` unchanged) — this whole feature has zero kernel-resident cost. **NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED AT ALL** — this is genuinely new, non-mechanical code (the coalescing logic especially) that has only been verified by simulation, hand-tracing, and successful assembly/linking, none of which is a substitute for real hardware given this project's own repeated experience that register-level 1802 code needs an actual run to be trusted. **Needs a dedicated round**: run `BUMPTEST` and `MALLOCTEST`, confirm all 4 and all 11 checks report `PASS`, and if anything fails, the check number pinpoints exactly which part of the design to re-examine first.
