- **`LS` utility (`progs/ls.asm`) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-17, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** `LS [-l] [path]`: default output is Linux-`ls`-style, sorted names only, printed in columns (column-major fill order — entries fill *down* each column before moving to the next, matching real BSD/GNU `ls`, not left-to-right); `-l` switches to one entry per line with a `d`/`-` type indicator, right-justified size, last-write date/time (same `MM/DD/YYYY HH:MM` format and FAT packed-date/time bit-unpacking sequence `DIR` already uses, copied rather than shared — this project's established `DIR`/`STAT` precedent for small helpers), then the name. Column width is `longest-name+2`; column count is `80 / column-width` (`LS_SCREEN_COLS=80` is a plain constant for now — the header comment notes `ROWS`/`COLUMNS` environment variables or ANSI terminal querying as future options once this project gets that far), clamped to `[1, LS_MAX_COLS=32]`.
  - **Deliberately self-contained, not built on `lib/heap_bump.asm`**: collected entries live in a fixed-size static table (`LS_MAX_ENTRIES=128`, names truncated at `LS_NAME_CAP=32` chars for display only) rather than the heap library, even though "collect everything, use it, exit" is exactly the bump allocator's designed use case — chosen because the heap libraries are themselves still not hardware-tested (see above), and taking on that dependency for a "quick" utility would mean two untested things instead of one. A future version can switch once the heap libraries are hardware-confirmed. Total static cost (~5.6KB: `ls_entries` + `ls_ptrs` + `ls_colbase`) is irrelevant to the kernel's own tight margin — it's ordinary program RAM above `PROG_BASE`, a completely different budget than the kernel-resident bytes this project is normally careful about.
  - **Sorting uses a hand-rolled `ls_namecmp`, not the BIOS's `f_strcmp`**: every existing call site of `f_strcmp` in this codebase (`file.asm`'s `_scan_dir_for_name`, `dir.asm`'s own `dir_find`) only ever checks zero/nonzero (equality) via `lbnz`, never ordering — so `f_strcmp`'s behavior for *unequal* strings (does it return a true signed difference like C's `strcmp`, or something else?) has never been exercised or confirmed in this codebase, the same "don't trust an unconfirmed BIOS contract" discipline already applied to `f_hexout4`/`f_div16`/`K_SETTOD` before their first real use. `ls_namecmp` is defined in `ls.asm` itself (byte-by-byte `sm`-based comparison, DF=1 if name1>name2) so its exact contract is fully known. Column-count/row-count division likewise avoids the BIOS's `f_div16` (also zero prior callers anywhere in this codebase) in favor of a hand-rolled `ls_div` via repeated subtraction — fine for this program's magnitudes (screen width, name lengths, entry counts, never more than a few hundred).
  - **Row/column layout avoids runtime multiplication entirely**: rather than computing `idx = col*num_rows+row` fresh (a multiply) for every cell, `ls_colbase[c] = c*num_rows` is precomputed once via `num_cols` additions (`ls_colbase_loop`), and the print loop only ever adds a row number to a table lookup.
  - **A real register-survival bug caught during a full manual re-trace after the D-clobber sweep and self-overwrite scans both came back clean** (matching this project's standing lesson, gotcha #13, that those two scripted sweeps only catch the *cheap, adjacent-instruction* form of a clobber bug, not one separated by a `call`): the columnar print loop originally kept the current entry's struct address in `R8`, then reused `R8` (as both the name pointer and, later, `col_width`) *after* `call K_MSG`/`call K_INMSG` — but this project has only ever confirmed `R9` (not `R8`) survives those calls (gotcha #8, and the I/O-redirection dispatchers' own explicit preservation list from the Phase 2 work). Fixed by stashing the name pointer into a new memory variable (`ls_curname`) immediately after computing it and reloading fresh after each call, and by re-reading `col_width` from its own memory location (`ls_colwidth`) every padding-loop iteration instead of holding it in `R8` — the same "don't trust a register across an unaudited call" pattern this project has hit repeatedly (gotcha #10) and now applies proactively. Caught the same manual-trace pass also fixed an unrelated off-by-one in the padding loop's own comparison (it was structured to pad to `col_width+1`, always printing one extra trailing space, harmless cosmetically but not what was intended).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep clean, a separate script scan for the `lda X`/`phi X` self-overwrite pattern (gotcha from the heap-library work above) clean, full clean `make`+`make progs` (29/29, 0 errors/warnings, kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$3cc6` — `progs/*.asm` never touches kernel margin), links with zero unresolved symbols. Every `call` site in the file was manually traced for register survival (the class of bug the scripted sweeps can't catch) — the one real bug found (above) was fixed; every other call site either only needs its own designed return value (`ls_div`, `ls_namecmp`, `K_GETCURDIR`'s documented `RA`/`RC` protection) or reloads everything fresh from memory afterward (`ls_curentry` in the `-l` path was already written that way from the start). **NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED** — needs a round covering: bare `LS` and `LS -l` in a directory with several files and subdirectories (columnar alignment, sort order, type indicators, date/time correctness); a directory with one very long name (column width/wrapping); a path argument (`LS somedir`, `LS -l somedir`); an empty directory; a directory with more than `LS_MAX_ENTRIES` (unlikely to occur in practice, low priority); a not-found path and a path naming a file instead of a directory (error messages).

- **⚠️ ONGOING BUG HUNT (2026-07-18): `edlin test.txt < NUL` still spins forever despite the K_INPUTL DF=0/1 EOF contract — hardware-confirmed regression, TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTICS ADDED, awaiting a hardware round.** User confirmed DATE/TIME, both heap-library test programs (all checks PASS), and `>NUL` output redirection all working on hardware (2026-07-17/18) — but `<NUL` input redirection into `edlin` still hangs, unchanged from before the DF-based EOF fix was supposedly applied. Exhaustive static re-trace of the entire path (shell tokenizer's `<`-operator/target-capture code, `run_loop`'s relay-slot clearing and `_redir_setup`/`_redir_teardown` sequencing, `_redir_setup`'s NUL-detection branch and dual-vs-single FCB decision, `_is_nul_device`'s exact-match logic, `_redir_inputl`'s `kir_null_eof` path, and `edlin.asm`'s own `ed_cmdloop` DF check + `ed_eof_quit`) found every piece individually correct — no bug located by re-reading alone. Two `; TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC` blocks added, both printing directly to the real console via plain `f_inmsg`/`K_INMSG` (not through anything redirect-aware) so they're unaffected by whatever is or isn't actually redirected: (1) `kernel/redir.asm`'s `rs_in_open`, printing `[RID:notnull]` if `_is_nul_device` says the input target ISN'T NUL, or `[RID:isnull]` (at `rs_in_isnull`) if it IS — pins down whether NUL detection itself is even firing for this case; (2) `progs/edlin.asm`'s `ed_cmdloop`, printing `[DF0]` or `[DF1]` immediately after `call K_INPUTL`, before acting on it — pins down whether the kernel-side EOF signal actually reaches edlin correctly. Verified: clean assemble both files, D-clobber sweep clean on both, `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes), all 5 redirect-aware jump-table slots (`K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`/`K_READ`/`K_INPUTL`) re-decoded from the real `kernel.bin` and confirmed pointing at their correct dispatcher addresses, full clean `make`+`make progs` (29/29). Margins: `Highest address` `$3cf4` (+46 bytes for the diagnostic), **268 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** — still positive, boot-safe. **Needs a hardware round**: run `edlin test.txt < NUL` again with this diagnostic build and report exactly what prints (or doesn't) before the hang — `[RID:...]` tells us whether NUL was recognized as the input target at all; `[DF0]`/`[DF1]` (and whether it repeats) tells us whether the EOF signal is reaching edlin. Remove both diagnostic blocks once root-caused (`grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC"` before calling it done, per this project's standing practice).

- **`MOVE` command — CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-23)**, `progs/move.asm` + a new `lib/move.asm` library (per the user's own request: factor the general-purpose "try a fast rename, else copy+delete" logic into a library, reusable later for a C standard library `rename()` implementation in the separate ELFC project). `MOVE <source> <destination>` / `MOVE <source> [source...] <destination-directory>` — same argv shape, destination-directory convenience, and multi-source-requires-existing-directory rule as COPY (its own header comment covers the shared rationale). For each source, tries a fast, data-free rename first, falling back to a full copy-then-delete only when that's not possible (moving a directory across directories isn't supported at all — reported as an error, since that needs a recursive copy, a future XCOPY's own job).
- **`XCOPY` command — FULLY CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-24/25), through three rounds of hardware bug-fixing.** `progs/xcopy.asm`, this project's first real recursive routine (`xc_walk` calls itself). `-s`/`-e`/`-y`/`-d`/`-c`/`-v` and cross-drive operation all confirmed on real hardware across the full test round; `-h` was never separately called out but is trivial and low-risk (a plain hidden-attribute filter, same as `DIR`/`LS`'s own already-proven `-a`/hidden handling). Zero kernel changes — pure userland, built on `lib/heap_bump.asm` (per-recursion-level state lives in a bump-allocated `XC_FRAME` struct, since `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` share one kernel-resident scan position and can't be interleaved with a nested call that also opens a directory — a directory's entries are fully collected into a bump-allocated array in pass 1, then pass 2 iterates that array, recursing into subdirectory entries, matching `progs/ls.asm`'s own established "collect everything, use it" pattern).
  - **First shipped version had no MS-DOS-style switches at all and always recursed unconditionally.** User's real-world repro (`md testx` then `xcopy /cfg testx`) hit an infinite loop: `/testx` filled with nested `cfg` directories inside `cfg` directories, forever, no files ever copied. Root cause: `xc_walk`'s own entry called `bump_mark` then `bump_alloc` — both of which clobber `RD` per their own documented `Modifies` lists (`bump_mark`: "Modifies: RD"; `bump_alloc`: "Modifies: R7, R8, R9, RB, RD") — **before** `call K_DIR_OPEN`, which still needed `RD` to hold the caller's own source cluster argument. A pre-existing comment right above the `K_DIR_OPEN` call had asserted "RD still holds the source cluster passed in — nothing above this line touches RD" without ever actually checking `bump_mark`/`bump_alloc`'s real contracts — exactly the class of mistake gotcha #10 already warns about, self-inflicted here. Since `bump_alloc`'s own internal `size-1` arithmetic always left `RD` holding the same fixed value (`XC_FRAME_LEN-1=7`) regardless of recursion depth, every single call to `xc_walk` opened the identical wrong cluster — explaining the "same directory name, forever, deeper and deeper" symptom exactly (a real, repeating directory-create, not a display glitch). Fixed by stashing `RD` into `RA` (confirmed untouched by both calls' documented clobber lists) at `xc_walk`'s very first instruction, restored immediately before `K_DIR_OPEN`.
  - **Redesigned around real MS-DOS XCOPY switches at the user's own request** ("redesign it with the switches... allow me to try sample commands that I find in xcopy tutorials"), deliberately **dash-prefixed** (`-s` not `/S`) rather than matching real XCOPY's slash syntax — this project's own paths are forward-slash-rooted (`/cfg`), so a slash-prefixed switch would be genuinely ambiguous with a path argument, not just a style mismatch. `XCOPY [-h] [-v] [-y] [-s] [-e] [-i] [-c] [-d] <source> <dest>`, clustered like `LS`'s own flags. Real semantics, matching actual MS-DOS behavior rather than the first version's own incorrect assumption: **`-s` (recurse into subdirectories) is now opt-in** — without it, a directory source copies only its own top-level files, no subdirectories touched at all (this alone fixes the reported bug: the original repro now copies `/cfg`'s files into `testx` with no recursion and no loop). `-e` keeps subdirectories that end up empty (only meaningful with `-s`) — without it, a subdirectory `xc_walk` itself freshly created via `K_DIR_CREATE` is removed again after the fact if nothing was ever copied anywhere inside it (tracked via a per-frame `XC_FRAME_FRESH` flag plus an `xc_copied_count` snapshot-and-compare around the recursive call; a subdirectory that already existed, i.e. `xcw_subdir_merge`, is never a cleanup candidate regardless of what ends up inside it). `-i` is accepted but inert (suppresses a file-vs-directory ambiguity prompt this implementation never has, since `K_STAT` already disambiguates unambiguously). `-c` continues past a per-file/per-directory error instead of aborting the whole run at the first one (real XCOPY's actual default is the opposite — abort on first error — implemented via a new `xc_report_error` helper and a plain global `xc_abort` flag, checked at the top of every level's own pass-2 loop so it propagates up through the recursion for free with no special per-return-point handling; an out-of-memory condition from the shared bump arena always aborts unconditionally regardless of `-c`, since continuing would almost certainly fail identically for every remaining entry). `-d` only copies a file if its source is strictly newer than an already-existing destination, with **no explicit cutoff-date argument** (`-d:date` is deliberately not implemented) — FAT's packed date/time fields compare correctly as plain unsigned 16-bit values, so a new `xc_src_newer` helper compares `DIRENT_WRTDATE` then `DIRENT_WRTTIME` (date primary, time as tiebreaker) via `K_STAT` on both source and destination, needing no unpacking of individual year/month/day/hour/min/sec components.
  - **Verification (of the redesign itself)**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), full clean `make`+`make progs` (38/38 steps, kernel untouched — `bin/xcopy` links cleanly against `lib/heap_bump.prg` with 0 unresolved symbols). D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite (`lda X`/`phi X` or `plo X` with no refresh) sweep, and gotcha #18 sweep (`str r2` staging immediately followed by a register-register `add16`/`sub16` before the intended consumer) all run via script across the whole file — zero hits. Every new/rewritten block (the `-s` collection-loop gate, the `-c`/`xc_abort` top-of-loop check, the rewritten subdirectory create/merge/recurse/cleanup path, `xc_report_error`, `xc_src_newer`, and the `-d` wiring in `xc_copy_one_file`) was additionally hand-traced for register liveness across every `K_STAT`/`K_DIR_CREATE`/`K_DIR_READ`/`xc_walk` call — matching this file's own established discipline (its header comment already documents why the frame pointer is kept in a global, `xcw_frame_ptr`, reloaded fresh before every single use rather than trusted in a register across any of these calls, with a `push`/`pop` of `R8` as the one exception, around the recursive call itself).
  - **First hardware round (2026-07-25) on the redesign: `-s`/`-e` CONFIRMED WORKING, `-c` not yet exercised (user wasn't sure how to set up a repro), `-d` CONFIRMED BROKEN.** The original infinite-loop repro (`md testx`/`xcopy /cfg testx`, no switches) no longer loops at all — copies only top-level files as intended. `-s` recursion into a real tree worked; `-s -e` empty-directory retention behaved as expected. `-d`, tested by xcopying a tree then immediately re-running the identical `xcopy -d` with nothing touched, prompted to overwrite every single file instead of silently skipping all of them (source not actually newer than the just-written destination).
  - **`-d` root cause: the `SM` opcode direction was backwards in `xc_src_newer`.** `SM` computes `D = D − M(R(X))`, NOT `M(R(X)) − D` — confirmed against Asm/02's own `SUB16` macro expansion (`opcodes.def`: stages the *subtrahend* via `str r2` first, then loads the *minuend* into `D`, then `SM`) and cross-checked against an already-proven real usage (`kernel/loader.asm:86-89`, `D = mem_top.lo − PROG_BASE.lo` via exactly that staging order). `xc_src_newer`'s first draft staged the source byte into `M(R2)` and loaded the dest byte into `D` right before `SM` — backwards — so it actually computed `dest − source`, and (since a file's destination copy always gets a *newer* write-time than its source, per `_fclose_rewrite_size` always stamping the current time, never the source's original time) it reported "source is newer" on essentially every re-run, falling through to the ordinary overwrite prompt instead of skipping. Fixed by swapping the staging order at all four byte-pair comparisons (WRTDATE hi/lo, WRTTIME hi/lo) — dest staged first, source loaded right before `SM`. Verified: clean rebuild (byte-identical size, pure operand reorder), full sweep suite clean. **Awaiting a hardware re-test.**
  - **A second, independent bug found via the same hardware round (not a `-d` symptom — reachable with no switches at all): cross-drive XCOPY created the destination directory but silently copied zero files, no error shown.** Root cause: `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` (`kernel/dir.asm`'s own `dir_open` header: "Args: RD = starting cluster... " — no drive parameter at all) operate against whichever drive is *currently active* in the kernel's BPB/FAT cache, not necessarily the drive the cluster number actually belongs to — only `path_resolve` (reached via `K_STAT`/`K_DIR_CREATE`/`K_FILE_OPEN`/etc, given a real path *string*) ever calls the kernel's own `_switch_drive`. `xc_source_is_dir`'s own top-level flow does `K_STAT`/`K_DIR_CREATE` on the *destination* path immediately before ever calling `xc_walk` — activating the destination's drive — so by the time `xc_walk`'s pass 1 called `K_DIR_OPEN` on the *source*'s cluster, it silently read sectors under the wrong drive's BPB (explaining "directory created, zero files copied, no error" exactly — a wrong-but-plausible-looking empty scan, not a crash). Same risk exists one level into any `-s` recursion, since `xcw_subdir_recurse`'s own dest-side `K_STAT`/`K_DIR_CREATE` runs immediately before each nested `xc_walk` call too. **Note there is no general cross-drive gap in this project** — `TYPE`/`K_FILE_OPEN`-based commands like `COPY` already work cross-drive fine (hardware-confirmed independently, both via the user's own `type c:/cfg/env.dat` test while on drive E:, and via this project's earlier `test.bat` cross-drive-copy round), precisely *because* they operate on full path strings through `path_resolve` every time, not a bare cluster number carried across calls the way `xc_walk`'s own pass 1 does.
    - **Fix**: at the top of `xc_walk`, right before pass 1's `K_DIR_OPEN`, added a `K_STAT(xc_src_path)` call purely for its side effect (reactivating the source's own drive via `path_resolve`'s internal `_switch_drive`); the returned `DIRENT` is discarded. `xc_src_path` already holds this level's own full source path at every call site (set from `xc_src_arg` at the top level, extended by `xc_append_name` during recursive descent), so this works identically whether `xc_walk` is entered at the top or several levels deep. **`K_SETDRIVE` was deliberately NOT used** — per its own `kernel_api.inc` doc, it permanently changes `cur_drive` (the *shell's* own working-drive state, reflected in the prompt), not just the BPB/FAT cache; that would have been a real, surprising side effect (the shell's active drive silently changing after running `xcopy`) that this fix must not have. The cluster argument itself is now stashed to a new 2-byte memory field (`xcw_src_clust`), not just a register (`RA`, used by the *first* hardware round's fix) — a third call (this new `K_STAT`) needed to run before `K_DIR_OPEN` with no proven-safe clobber list of its own, so trusting any single register across all three calls (`bump_mark`/`bump_alloc`/`K_STAT`) would have repeated the exact unverified-assumption mistake gotcha #10 already describes; the now-dead `RA` stash from the first round's fix was removed. Verified: clean rebuild, hand-traced that nothing between the new `K_STAT` call and the next real use of `R8` (much later, in `xcw_collect_keep`) depends on `R8`'s pre-call value, full sweep suite re-run clean across the whole file. **Not yet hardware-tested.**
  - **Third hardware round (2026-07-25) — CONFIRMED WORKING**: the fixed `-d` (re-running an unchanged tree correctly skips every file now), the fixed cross-drive case, and `-y` all tested and passed. `-v` (re-open-and-compare after each copy — no way to force a genuine mismatch without corrupting a file mid-copy, so the realistic test is just confirming a normal run completes clean with no false "verify failed") and `-c` (tested via a constructed conflict: a plain file pre-created at the destination where a source subdirectory needed to go, forcing a per-entry error mid-`-s`-tree — without `-c` the run stopped there, with `-c` it skipped that one entry and continued into the rest of the tree) both confirmed working too. This closes out XCOPY's hardware verification — no outstanding switches or bugs.
  - **First design (built same day, abandoned after a real hardware bug hunt): resolve BOTH paths via `K_PATH_RESOLVE` and compare (drive, parent cluster) to decide fast-vs-fallback.** This meant `move_rename`'s own "new name" argument, handed to `K_FILE_RENAME`, was sourced directly from `K_PATH_RESOLVE`'s own returned pointer — into the kernel's `path_buf` scratch. `K_PATH_RESOLVE`'s own contract says that pointer stays valid "until the next `K_PATH_RESOLVE` call" — but `K_FILE_RENAME`'s own implementation (`file_rename`, to locate the OLD entry) makes exactly such a call internally, AFTER already receiving the new-name argument but BEFORE ever dereferencing it — silently overwriting it with the OLD path's own resolved text first. Symptom: `move env.bak env-old.dat` (same directory) failed with "destination exists" — the new-name collision scan was searching for `env.bak` (what `path_buf` now held) instead of `env-old.dat`. **Nothing before this had ever exercised this exact pattern** — `REN` (`progs/ren.asm`) always sources its own new-name argument from `argv`/`LINE_BUF`, a completely different, stable buffer, so this was a genuinely new interaction, not a regression.
  - **First fix attempt: `file_rename` copies the new-name STRING CONTENT into a private, kernel-resident buffer at entry, instead of just saving the caller's pointer.** Fixed the "already exists" false-positive, but hit a SECOND bug on the very next hardware round (a "remaining capacity" counter read back as `0` where it should have been `127`, and the buffer came back empty) that took a properly-written 1802 disassembler to root-cause: `REN_NEW_NAME_MAX`, the buffer's own size constant, was declared inside `file_rename`'s own `proc` block and referenced in an expression there — an Asm/02 bug (fixed upstream 2026-07-24, see toolchain gotcha #19 for the full corrected mechanism) tagged that reference with a bogus relocation fixup, and under `-r` relaxation's multi-round rebuild the immediate-operand bytes depending on it were silently replaced with unrelated values in the FINAL linked kernel, even though the initial (pre-relaxation) assembly got it right. Moving the `equ` declaration entirely outside the `proc` fixed it at the time (declaration order turned out not to matter — only proc-scope did, per gotcha #19's own corrected writeup), confirmed via direct byte-decode of the real linked `kernel.bin`.
  - **Final design, after the user's own step-back ("this ought to be self-contained") — eliminated `K_PATH_RESOLVE` from `move_rename` entirely, and reverted ALL kernel changes.** `K_FILE_RENAME`'s own contract already says everything needed: its new-name argument must be a bare name (no `/`). So `move_rename` just scans the destination string for `/` or an `X:`-style drive prefix — if found, reports "needs copy" without ever calling into the kernel; otherwise calls `K_FILE_RENAME` directly with the caller's own untouched strings, never anywhere near `K_PATH_RESOLVE`. `kernel/file.asm` is back to its exact original, already-proven state (plain `git checkout`, since none of this session's kernel changes were ever committed; confirmed byte-identical `kernel.bin` to the pre-MOVE-work baseline) — **MOVE needed zero kernel changes in the end.** `lib/move.asm` shrank from 170 bytes of generated code to 51. One accepted tradeoff: `MOVE /cfg/foo.txt /cfg/bar.txt` (full paths on both sides, but the same directory) takes the copy+delete fallback instead of the instant rename, since the routine has no way to know the two paths resolve to the same place without doing the resolution it's specifically avoiding — acceptable since real usage types a bare name for a same-directory rename and a path/directory for an actual move.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-23)**: the original failing case (`move env.bak env-old.dat`, same directory) now succeeds, plus the user's own broader pass (cross-directory move, moving into a directory, overwrite prompt).

- **ERRORLEVEL prelude to batch `IF`/`GOTO` — IMPLEMENTED AND CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE 2026-07-25.** Scoped deliberately narrow (confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion` before implementing): the program→kernel→relay plumbing plus a *narrow*, `%ERRORLEVEL%`-only substitution — not the full general `%VAR%`/`%1` engine (real future work, with its own complexity: variable-length replacement text that can *grow* a line, unlike `%ERRORLEVEL%` which only ever shrinks it), and not `IF`/`GOTO` themselves.
  - **A pleasant surprise found while researching this**: the program-side "audit" was mostly already done. `grep -l "exit code" progs/*.asm` showed 30 of 39 programs already setting `D=0` on success / `D=1` on error before every `rtn`, with an explicit `; exit code 0 = success` comment convention dated 2026-07-21 — established well before anything consumed the value. The kernel side already threaded it too: `_prog_exec_now`/`prog_run` (`kernel/loader.asm`) already return "D = program exit code" all the way up to `run_loop`, which had `call prog_run ; D = exit code (unused), DF=0/1` sitting there literally unused. So the real gap was narrow: capture that already-flowing value into a relay slot, and give the shell a way to read it. The 9 programs without the convention (`envtest`/`pwd`/`malloctest`/`bumptest`/`export`/`reboot`/`seektest`/`unset`/`xcopy`) are left as a candidate list for a later full sweep, not touched here.
  - **New relay slot `RUN_ERRORLEVEL` (`PROG_BASE-110`)**, same convention as `RUN_PATH`/`RUN_ARGC`/`RUN_REDIR_OUT`/`RUN_BATCH_ECHO_OFF` (restated in both `include/kernel.inc` and `include/kernel_api.inc`, kernel/shell-internal — no ordinary program reads it directly by address). `kernel_init` zeroes it once at boot (right before `run_loop` begins, after the `autoexec.bat` `batch_start` call), so `%ERRORLEVEL%` reads `0` before any command has ever run. `run_loop` (`kernel/kernel.asm`) captures it after every `prog_run` call: on success, the real `D` value (stashed in `RC`'s low byte — already dead there, its own argc value already consumed by `prog_run`'s own `RA`/`RC` arguments — across the `mov rf, RUN_ERRORLEVEL` that would otherwise clobber `D`, gotcha #4); on the `run_bad_program` path, a fixed sentinel (`1`) instead of trusting `D`, since `prog_run`'s own header says `D` is only meaningfully an exit code on success ("DF=1 on error... nothing is run in that case").
  - **New `K_GET_ERRORLEVEL` (`$017C`, next free slot now `$017F`)** — a real jump-table call for ordinary userland access, rather than baking `RUN_ERRORLEVEL`'s fixed relay address into a program's own compiled binary (the exact mistake the argv/argc ABI redesign already moved away from). Trivial one-line body (`kernel_get_errorlevel`, next to `kernel_getshelldrive`): read `RUN_ERRORLEVEL`, return in `D`, `DF=0` always. New `progs/errorlevel.asm` (`ERRORLEVEL`, no arguments — same shape as `progs/ver.asm`'s version print) calls it and prints the value via `f_uintout`.
  - **`%ERRORLEVEL%` substitution — integrated into the shell's existing tokenizer, not a separate pre-pass, specifically to respect quoting.** A standalone pre-pass over raw `LINE_BUF` (the first design) would have no way to know it's inside a `'...'` span, so it would wrongly substitute even single-quoted text — breaking the existing "single quotes are 100% literal, bash-style" rule (`tok_in_squote`, 2026-07-19). Confirmed with the user (raised as a direct clarifying question, caught before implementation): `echo '%ERRORLEVEL%'` must print the literal text; `echo "%ERRORLEVEL%"` and `echo %ERRORLEVEL%` (double-quoted and unquoted) both substitute, matching how double quotes already allow backslash-escape processing rather than being fully literal. **Chose to integrate into `not_drive_cmd`'s tokenizer (`tok_ordinary`, its shared copy point for both the unquoted/double-quoted path and the single-quoted path) over a quote-aware standalone pre-pass** — reuses the tokenizer's own live `R8.0` quote-state register as the single source of truth, rather than duplicating quote/escape-tracking logic in a second place that could drift out of sync with the real tokenizer later (confirmed via `AskUserQuestion`, weighing this against the duplication-risk alternative and a documented-quote-blind-gap alternative).
  - **A real bug caught by the Python simulation before this ever reached hardware, in the very first draft's design, not just its transcription**: `ani $DF` (this codebase's established uppercase-fold idiom, used elsewhere only on confirmed letters, e.g. the shell's own drive-letter check) clears bit 5 — which folds a lowercase *letter* to uppercase, but `%` is `$25` (`0010 0101`), which already has bit 5 set, so folding it produces `$05`, not `%` itself. The first draft applied the fold uniformly across all 12 bytes of `"%ERRORLEVEL%"`, silently breaking the match exactly at the leading/trailing `%` positions — caught by an independent Python re-simulation of the algorithm (13 cases: unquoted/double/single-quoted, lowercase, multiple matches, partial/no match, `%` at end-of-line, a `%` not followed by the pattern) before any assembly was trusted, matching this project's own established practice. Fixed by checking the leading and trailing `%` *exactly* (no fold) and only folding the 10 middle letters, which are always genuinely letters — `tok_errlvl_pat`'s own data declaration shrank to just `"ERRORLEVEL"` (no percent signs) as a result. The corrected simulation passed all 13 cases (plus a 14th that turned out to be the test's own wrong expectation about quote-character stripping, unrelated to this feature, caught and corrected during review rather than mis-attributed as an algorithm bug).
  - **Register protection across the `f_uintout` call** (its own clobber footprint unconfirmed in this codebase, gotcha #8/#10, and `not_drive_cmd`'s own header documents `RF`/`RD`/`RB`/`R9.0`/`R8.0` as live across the *whole* tokenizing loop): `push`/`pop` all five around the call — the same established pattern this project already uses across other unconfirmed-footprint calls (e.g. `kernel/redir.asm`'s dispatchers protecting `RF`/`RC`/`RA` around `file_write`). The matched-text length itself is never hardcoded as a `+12` constant; the scan naturally leaves `R7` pointing exactly one past the trailing `%`, which becomes the new `RF` directly — self-consistent with wherever the pattern table's own contents end, rather than a separate constant that could drift out of sync with it.
  - **Verification**: full clean `make`+`make progs` (kernel changed as expected — `Highest address` `$3e66`→`$3e88`, **1912 bytes before `PROG_BASE`**, still healthy — `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, confirming zero boot-code impact; all programs including new `errorlevel` build clean, 0 errors/warnings). All 39 `K_*` jump-table slots (38 existing + `K_GET_ERRORLEVEL`) decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`; `K_GET_ERRORLEVEL`'s own target byte-decoded instruction-by-instruction (`LDI $45/PHI RF/LDI $92/PLO RF/LDN RF/CLC/RTN`) and confirmed it resolves `RUN_ERRORLEVEL` to exactly `PROG_BASE-110` (`$4592`). D-clobber/self-overwrite/gotcha-#18 sweeps clean on every touched file (`kernel/kernel.asm`, `progs/shell.asm`, `progs/errorlevel.asm`), no duplicate labels.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-25)**: quoting behavior (`echo '%ERRORLEVEL%'` literal vs. `echo "%ERRORLEVEL%"`/`echo %ERRORLEVEL%` substituted) confirmed first. The value-tracking itself needed one round of user-caught confusion to fully confirm, worth recording as a lesson about ERRORLEVEL semantics, not a bug: a first test script (`type nonexistent.txt` / `echo %ERRORLEVEL%` / `errorlevel`) was predicted by the assistant to print "1" then "1" ("nothing ran in between to change it") — wrong, since `echo %ERRORLEVEL%` is itself a command, and once it finishes running, `ERRORLEVEL` correctly reflects *its* exit code (`0`), not `type`'s anymore. The actual output was "1" then "0" — entirely correct, `%ERRORLEVEL%` substitution had already captured the `type` failure at tokenize time (before `echo` ran), and the standalone `ERRORLEVEL` afterward correctly showed the *next* most recent command's own code, exactly matching real DOS semantics (ERRORLEVEL updates after every command, including diagnostic ones inserted to check it). The user's own follow-up test — a failing command followed *immediately* by `ERRORLEVEL` with nothing in between — cleanly returned `1`, confirming the whole pipeline (program exit code → `run_loop` capture → `RUN_ERRORLEVEL` → both `%ERRORLEVEL%` and the standalone command) works correctly end to end. No code changes were needed; the fix was catching the assistant's own flawed expected-output prediction, not the implementation.
  - **Lesson worth keeping**: when constructing a test script for a "does state X persist correctly" question, count every command in the script as something that can *itself* change the state under test — a verification step is not exempt just because it's "just checking," a mistake made once here and worth watching for again.

- **Removed the "Type a command." boot banner (2026-07-25), build-verified, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** User's own framing: anyone who's gotten this OS booting on 50-year-old CDP1802 hardware already knows what to do at a prompt. Two lines removed from `boot/krnboot.asm`'s `boot_init2` (the `call f_inmsg` / `db "Type a command.",13,10,0` right before falling through to `KERN_ENTRY`) — the "ELF-DOS v0.1" version banner right before it is untouched. Verified the string is genuinely gone from the built `krnboot.bin` (direct byte search) and that the version banner still is present; full clean `make` succeeds, `krnboot.bin` still exactly 1536 bytes (as always — it's a fixed sector allocation, this just leaves a couple more of its already-mostly-unused bytes free). This is boot code, this project's own highest-stakes category (a boot mistake is silent, zero-console-output failure) — small as the change is, it still wants a real hardware round before being trusted, per standing practice.
- **Segregated test/diagnostic programs out of `progs/` into a new `test/` directory (2026-07-25), build-verified.** User's own framing: `bin/` (the on-device `/bin` mirror) should only hold what a normal install actually wants, not the programs that exist purely to exercise a specific subsystem. Moved 8 files total (`git mv`, preserving history), in two passes: `atest`/`wtest`/`wbtest` (file write/append/seek exercises), `seektest` (`K_FILE_SEEK`), `bumptest`/`malloctest` (the `lib/heap_bump.asm`/`lib/heap_malloc.asm` allocators), `envtest` (`lib/env.asm`) in the first pass; `errorlevel` in a second pass, after the user asked directly "does this gain us anything over `echo %ERRORLEVEL%`?" — the honest answer was no (it always prints exactly what `%ERRORLEVEL%` already would, and `%ERRORLEVEL%` is strictly more flexible), so it moved too, re-framed in its own header comment as "proves `K_GET_ERRORLEVEL` works," not a real end-user command (real MS-DOS has no standalone `ERRORLEVEL` command either, for the same reason). `args`/`echo` were deliberately NOT moved — real, if small, day-to-day utilities (argv/quoting debugging, batch scripting), not subsystem test suites, matching the user's own "test programs" framing rather than "anything diagnostic." `K_GET_ERRORLEVEL` itself stays in the kernel regardless of `errorlevel.exe`'s own move — a cheap, minimal, reasonable primitive for whatever future program wants programmatic access to the previous exit code, not worth the churn of pulling a jump-table slot back out for 3 bytes.
  - **Build system**: new parallel `test`/`TEST_SRCS`/`TEST_EXES` machinery in the Linux `Makefile`, mirroring `progs`/`PROG_SRCS`/`PROG_EXES` exactly — `test/*.asm` → `test/bin/<name>` (bare-named, same convention as `bin/`), via the same generic single-file pattern rule plus explicit override rules for the 3 that link against `lib/` (`bumptest`+`lib/heap_bump.prg`, `malloctest`+`lib/heap_malloc.prg`, `envtest`+`lib/env.prg` — same "explicit rule beats pattern rule" mechanism `bin/xcopy`'s own rule already established; `errorlevel` needs no such override, it's a plain single-file build picked up by the generic rule). `clean` now also sweeps `test/*.prg`/`test/*.lst`/`test/bin`. `Makefile.win`/`build_progs.bat` updated in lockstep (nmake has no `$(wildcard ...)`, so the same split this project's Windows port already used for `progs/`'s own lib-linked subset — explicit nmake rules for the 3 lib-linked test programs via a new `TEST_SPECIAL_BINS`, everything else including `errorlevel` auto-discovered by a new `build_test.bat`, mirroring `build_progs.bat` exactly) plus a new `test:` target; `.gitignore` gained `/test/bin/` alongside its existing `/bin/` entry.
  - **Verification**: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` — `bin/` now holds exactly 31 install-worthy programs, `test/bin/` holds exactly the 8 moved ones, all building 0 errors/warnings including the 3 lib-linked ones (confirmed their multi-file link commands actually ran, not just the generic single-file rule). `kernel.bin` byte-identical to before this change (pure build-system/file-location reorganization, zero kernel-resident impact) — only `krnboot.bin` differs, and only because of the separate "Type a command." removal above, landing in the same build. Windows-side files (`Makefile.win`/`build_progs.bat`/`build_test.bat`) reviewed for correctness by eye (no Windows environment available here to actually run `nmake`) — worth a real Windows build round whenever that's convenient, same as this project's existing standing note about the Windows port generally.

- **Batch scripting: `IF`/`GOTO` — IMPLEMENTED AND CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE 2026-07-25.** Roadmap item 3's last piece (`%1`/`%args` substitution stays deferred — its own separate complexity: variable-length replacement text that can *grow* a line, unlike `%ERRORLEVEL%` which only ever shrinks it). Full plan-mode design at `~/.claude/plans/glistening-marinating-moler.md`. Scope, deliberately minimal and confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion` before design: `IF [NOT] EXIST <path> <command>` and `IF [NOT] <str1>==<str2> <command>` (case-sensitive comparison, matching real DOS), `GOTO <label>` to a `:label` line. **Not** `IF ERRORLEVEL n` (superseded by `%ERRORLEVEL%==n`, an earlier decision already made) and not 4DOS/NT extensions. `GOTO` to an undefined label prints `"Label not found."` and ends the batch, matching real DOS — confirmed with the user, and falls out of the design for free (see below).
  - **`GOTO` + labels — kernel side** (`kernel/batch.asm`, extending the existing `batch_start`/`batch_readline` pair): new `batch_goto(RF=label name, no leading ':')` — copies the label into a new kernel-resident `batch_goto_label` (32 bytes) *before* anything else, since its own scan works by calling `batch_readline` repeatedly (reusing it rather than duplicating file-reading logic), and `batch_readline` overwrites `LINE_BUF` — where the caller's own label argument (typically `argv[1]`) lives — on every single call. Then `file_seek(batch_fcb, SEEK_SET, 0)` (new `extrn file_seek` in `batch.asm`) to rewind — `GOTO` always rewinds and scans forward regardless of direction, simpler than tracking it, and batch scripts are small enough that a full rescan is cheap. Loops `batch_readline`, `f_ltrim`s each line, checks for a leading `:`, and does a case-insensitive whole-word compare of the text after it against `batch_goto_label`. On `EOF` without a match: return `DF=1` — **and the batch is already fully torn down**, since `batch_readline`'s own `EOF` path already closes `batch_fcb` as an existing side effect — the "GOTO not found ends the batch" behavior needed zero extra cleanup code. New `K_BATCH_GOTO` jump-table slot (`$017F`, next free `$0182`), `k_batch_goto: lbr batch_goto` in `kernel/kernel.asm`, matching `K_BATCH_START`/`K_BATCH_READLINE`'s own exact pattern (`extrn batch_goto` alongside the existing pair, jump table points directly at the proc, no wrapper).
  - **`GOTO` + labels — shell side** (`progs/shell.asm`): a label line (`:name`) reached via ordinary top-to-bottom flow is silently skipped — checked right alongside the existing `REM` check in `start_have_line` (a label is never tokenized or dispatched, exactly like a blank line or `REM`). `GOTO`'s own dispatch lives in a new `check_special:` block (see `IF` below) — `argv[1]` is the label (no leading `:`), `call K_BATCH_GOTO`, `"Label not found."` on `DF=1`, either way `lbr start` (`GOTO` never falls through to ordinary program resolution).
  - **`IF` — pure shell side, no kernel changes.** Lives in the same `check_special:` block, inserted right after `tok_done`'s existing `RA`/`glob_expand` sequence, before `scan_slash`'s path-resolution logic begins — checked *before* `GOTO`'s own check. **Why `argv[]`, not raw text**: real batch use overwhelmingly looks like `IF EXIST x GOTO label` or `IF EXIST x DEL x` — by the time tokenizing has already happened, "the rest of the line" is already sitting in `argv[]` as real, already-quote-processed tokens; shifting the *already-tokenized* `argv[]` in place (rather than re-deriving a command string from raw text a second time, risking drift from what the tokenizer actually produced) is both simpler and more correct.
    - **Condition parsing**, via a new reusable `shell_match_word(RF=string, RD=uppercase pattern)` helper (factored out rather than repeating the `REM` check's own inline per-character shape three more times, matching this project's own standing lesson that duplicated fiddly per-character logic is where bugs live — safe to blind-fold with `ani $DF` specifically because every pattern it's called with, `"IF"`/`"NOT"`/`"EXIST"`/`"GOTO"`, is plain letters, the exact condition tok_ordinary's own `%ERRORLEVEL%` comment already establishes as safe): optional `NOT` (sets a negate flag), then either `EXIST <path>` (via `K_STAT`, reusing the existing `stat_result` scratch buffer — not live yet at this point in the flow, since `check_exists` itself hasn't run for this command cycle) or the `str1==str2` form as **one single token** (`1==1` tokenizes as one word, not three, matching real DOS) — scanned for the first `==` substring, then an exact two-cursor byte compare of the ranges before/after it (structurally similar to the `%ERRORLEVEL%` substitution's own pattern-match code). A bounds check (`if_check_bounds`, `if_idx < if_argc`) guards every point that's about to interpret `argv[if_idx]`'s content, printing a usage error on any malformed/truncated `IF` rather than reading stale/uninitialized argv data.
    - **On a true condition**: `argv[if_idx..argc-1]` is shifted down to `argv[0..]` in place (a plain copy loop, at most `ARGV_MAX_ARGS`=16 entries × 2 bytes), `RUN_ARGC` updated, `RA` reloaded from `RUN_ARGV_TABLE[0]`, and `lbr check_special` to re-enter the same check — this is what makes `IF EXIST x GOTO label` work (IF's own true-branch lands right back on `GOTO`'s own check) and, for free, supports a chained `IF ... IF ... command` (real but rare; costs nothing extra since it's the same loop either way). **On false**: `lbr start` — skip the whole line, matching `REM`'s exact behavior (no `ELSE`, matching real DOS's own core `IF`).
  - **Verification**: three genuinely new algorithmic pieces (`shell_match_word`'s whole-word matching, the `==` split-and-compare, the `argv[]` shift) independently Python-simulated before any assembly was trusted, per this project's own established practice given its repeated history of exactly this class of bug (the tokenizer's two hardware-found bugs, the `%ERRORLEVEL%` `ani $DF`-on-`%` bug found just last session). A full state-machine simulation of `IF`'s own condition parsing (including the bounds-check logic) caught a real off-by-one during design, before any assembly was written: the first bounds-check draft allowed `if_idx == argc` to pass as "valid" (checking only `argc >= if_idx` instead of the strict `argc > if_idx` a real index needs), which would have read one-past-the-end garbage for a truncated `IF` like bare `"IF"` or `"IF NOT"` — caught by testing exactly those malformed cases against the simulation before translating the bounds check into assembly, fixed to a two-step check (`DF=1` **or** `D==0`, both reject). Standard per-file checklist on every touched file (`kernel/batch.asm`, `kernel/kernel.asm`, `progs/shell.asm`): clean assemble, D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite scan, gotcha #18 sweep (the `sub16 r8, r9` in the `==` compare and the `shl16`s in `argv_at`/the shift code specifically re-checked by hand against gotcha #18's own scope — none run between a `str r2` and its consumer), no duplicate labels, proc/endp balanced (4/4 in `batch.asm`). All 40 `K_*` jump-table slots decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`; `K_BATCH_GOTO`'s own target cross-checked against `batch_goto`'s real linked address via `link02 -s`. `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (zero boot-code impact). Kernel margin: `Highest address` `$3e88`→`$3f1f` (+151 bytes — new proc + 32-byte label buffer + jump-table slot), **1761 bytes before `PROG_BASE`** — tighter than recent additions but still healthy. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`, all 0 errors/warnings.
  - **First hardware round (2026-07-25) found a real bug in the `EXIST` form, plus confirmed the rest of the design end-to-end.** `GOTO` (forward, undefined-label, label-skip-on-forward-flow), `IF EXIST`/`IF EXIST + GOTO`, `%ERRORLEVEL%==`, string `==`/`NOT`, and the `REM`/blank-line regression all passed on the first real run. The one genuine failure (`IF EXIST` on a file that hadn't been created yet still evaluating true) traced back to a real bug: the `if_idx++` (`adi 1`, "consumed the path") used to sit *between* `call K_STAT` and the `lbnf` that reads its result — `ADI` sets `DF` from its own carry, silently overwriting `K_STAT`'s real answer before it was ever checked. Since `if_idx` is always small (nowhere near 255), that add never actually carries, so `DF` always came out `0` regardless of what `K_STAT` returned — meaning `IF EXIST` always evaluated `true`, independent of the file's real state. Every *other* `if_idx++`/`argc` bump in `check_special` was confirmed safe by the same audit (each one's own transient `DF` is either consumed immediately by its own `lbdf`/`lbnf` before the increment runs, or the real result was already staged into `M(R2)` rather than carried in `DF`) — this was the one call site where a meaningful `DF` genuinely needed to survive across an intervening arithmetic instruction and didn't. Fixed by moving the increment to after the `DF`-into-`D` capture. Two of the three other apparent failures in the same hardware round turned out to be a stale/never-re-transferred copy of the test script (confirmed by the user's own `type test_ifgoto.bat` output still showing the pre-fix `echo.`/`goto skip_this` content) rather than implementation bugs — worth remembering as its own lesson: confirm the file actually on the device matches what was sent before trusting a "still failing" report as a fresh bug.
  - **Second hardware round (2026-07-25), after the `EXIST` fix — ALL TESTS PASSED, no implementation bugs left.** The only remaining noise was `progs/echo.asm`'s own `ECHO is off.` message on every bare `echo` used as a blank-line separator in the test script — not an `IF`/`GOTO` bug at all: bare `echo` (`argc<2`) deliberately reports batch-echo on/off state, matching real DOS (a pre-existing, already-documented, already-hardware-confirmed design choice in `echo.asm`'s own header). Fixed in the *test script*, not the implementation, by using `echo ""` (an explicit empty-string argument, `argc=2`) instead of bare `echo` — this already worked with zero new code, since the tokenizer already produces genuine empty-string tokens for `""` and `echo`'s own print loop happily prints nothing followed by the trailing newline for an empty `argv[1]`. Considered and declined building real `echo.`/`echo:` shell-level recognition (the real-DOS trick) for this, since it would need the same kind of raw-text special-casing the drive-letter check uses (the tokenizer sees `echo.`/`echo:` as one unsplittable word) for a problem `echo ""` already solves without touching the tokenizer at all. This closes out `IF`/`GOTO` with no open issues.

- **`history.dat` compaction — CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE 2026-07-25 (after two test-design misses, both explained below, neither a code bug). Zero kernel changes** (pure userland, `progs/shell.asm` only — the shell command-history feature's own file). Motivated directly by the user's own concern that `history.dat` grows without bound: past ~32KB it silently breaks recall entirely (`K_FILE_SEEK`'s own documented offset range is 0-32767, already noted in `hist_load`'s header), and even short of that it's wasted space, since `hist_load`'s own tail-read never looks further back than `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` bytes from the end regardless of how much more the file holds. User's own proposal, confirmed ("I like your history.dat approach; we'll go with that."): reuse `lib/env.asm`'s existing `setenv`/`unsetenv` temp-file-then-rename pattern for crash-safe replacement, rather than in-place truncation or a ping-pong scheme.
  - **New `hist_compact`, called from `hist_append` right after its existing `K_FILE_CLOSE`** (a no-op unless the file has actually grown past threshold). `K_STAT`s `history.dat` (reusing the shared `stat_result` scratch buffer, not live at this point in the flow — `check_exists` hasn't run yet this command cycle), reads `DIRENT_SIZE`'s 4 big-endian bytes directly (any nonzero high word is treated as "way over," no true 32-bit compare needed), and compares the low word against `HISTORY_COMPACT_THRESHOLD` (deliberately well above `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` so compaction doesn't run on nearly every append) via `sub16`. Below threshold: returns immediately, zero cost beyond one `K_STAT`.
  - **Above threshold**: calls `hist_load` **directly** (not through `hist_recall_up`'s own "already loaded this session?" guard) — safe and deliberate, since `hist_load` itself has no internal guard of its own (it always does a fresh `K_GETSHELLDRIVE`+seek+read+split whenever *called*; the "only once per session" behavior lives entirely in `hist_recall_up`, the guard's only caller). Calling it fresh here, *after* the append+close that just ran, means `hist_lines[]`/`hist_count` are guaranteed to include the line just appended — even if the user had already pressed Up earlier in this same command cycle (which would have loaded stale, pre-append data into the same arrays, had they been reused instead of refreshed).
  - **Writes `hist_lines[0..hist_count-1]` back out to a new `history.tmp`** (mode-1 create-or-overwrite, one `K_FILE_WRITE` per line + one for a trailing LF byte — the exact same two-call shape `hist_append` itself already uses for the live append, and the same shape `pipe_write_str` already established as safe to call `shell_strlen` immediately before `K_FILE_WRITE` with no need to reload `RF` afterward, since `shell_strlen`'s own header proves by direct inspection — "Makes no calls" — that `RF` truly survives it, unlike an unaudited BIOS call). `hist_tmp_path` is built by copying the drive-letter+colon prefix directly out of `hist_path` (already built by the caller) rather than a second `K_GETSHELLDRIVE` call.
  - **Swap**: `K_FILE_DELETE(history.dat)` then `K_FILE_RENAME(history.tmp -> "history.dat")` (a bare name, matching `K_FILE_RENAME`'s own documented "new name must be bare, applies within the old path's parent" contract) — same ordering and same accepted crash window (a crash between the two briefly leaves no `history.dat`) as `lib/env.asm`'s own precedent. Any failure at any step (can't stat, can't create the temp file, delete fails) gives up silently, leaving the real `history.dat` exactly as it was — compaction is a housekeeping nicety, never allowed to risk losing real history data.
  - **New scratch data**: `hist_tmp_fcb`/`hist_tmp_iobuf`/`hist_tmp_path`/`hist_tmp_suffix` (mirroring `hist_fcb`/`hist_iobuf`/`hist_path`/`hist_suffix` exactly), `hist_name_bare` (`"history.dat"`, the rename's bare-name argument), `hc_i` (the write-loop's own index). All ordinary program RAM above `PROG_BASE` — zero kernel-resident cost, zero impact on the tight kernel margin the `IF`/`GOTO` work above already used up.
  - **First hardware round: "doesn't seem to be compacting" — not a bug, a test-design gap.** `hist_append` only fires from the *interactive* prompt path (`start_interactive`), never from batch-echoed lines, so a `.bat` script can't bulk-generate history entries — testing needed a temporarily-lowered `HISTORY_COMPACT_THRESHOLD` (60 instead of 1024) to make the trigger reachable by hand-typing a handful of commands. At that lowered threshold with the *unmodified* `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` (still 255 at the time), no size drop was ever visible even well past 60 bytes, because the "kept tail" `hist_compact` computes is bounded by the budget — at file sizes under 255 bytes, the kept tail *is* the whole file, so there's nothing to visibly discard yet. Explained, not fixed in code (correct behavior for the values in play at the time).
  - **Second hardware round: still no visible compaction — this one WAS a real gap, in the test setup, not in `hist_compact`.** The test build's `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` was also temporarily lowered (to 40) to make a visible drop reachable quickly — but the transferred `bin/shell` turned out to be a stale copy (confirmed by comparing `ls -l /bin/shell`'s reported size, 7597 bytes, against the actual freshly-rebuilt binary's size, 7382 bytes, and reproducing the same MD5 from a from-scratch rebuild) — the *older* test build (threshold lowered but budget still 255) was what actually got transferred, exactly reproducing round one's symptom. Root-caused by direct byte/size comparison rather than assuming the code was at fault a second time; fixed by re-transferring the correct binary, not by touching source.
  - **Third hardware round, after re-transferring the correct binary — CONFIRMED WORKING.** User could directly perceive compaction firing (a command taking perceptibly longer than normal — the real temp-file-create/write/delete/rename I/O), matching the design.
  - **Follow-up (2026-07-25, same day): `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` raised 255→512 and `HISTORY_MAX_LINES` raised 25→50, user's own request after confirming compaction worked** ("What would be the impact if we increased the size of the history buffer to 512, and the max history to 50 commands... Could it be worth it?"). Crossing 256 required real code changes, not just constant bumps: `HISTORY_LOAD_BUDGET` had been deliberately kept under 256 so `hist_loaded_len` (the "bytes actually loaded this session" bookkeeping) and `hist_split`'s own remaining-byte scan countdown could both stay single-byte arithmetic. Both were widened to real 16-bit words: `hist_loaded_len` declaration `db`→`dw`, its two write sites in `hist_load` (the safe-default zero-init and the post-`K_FILE_READ` store) widened to write both bytes big-endian (matching the already-proven `hist_filesize` read/write convention elsewhere in the same file), and its read site in `hist_split` widened to a proper 2-byte load. `hist_split`'s `hsplit_loop` top-of-loop zero-check (previously a bare `glo rc / lbz end`) became a real 2-byte zero test (`glo rc / lbnz have_bytes` — low nonzero alone proves the word is nonzero, skipping the high-byte check entirely; only when low is zero does the high byte get checked, falling through to `have_bytes` if it's also nonzero, e.g. `RC=256`); its `hsplit_advance` decrement (previously `glo rc / smi 1 / plo rc`, which silently failed to borrow across the byte boundary) became `sub16 rc, 1` (the established, hardware-proven immediate-form idiom used throughout this codebase, e.g. `kernel/file.asm`'s own `sub16 rc, 1` sites). **Independently Python-simulated the modified zero-check + decrement as literal translated instructions (not a reimplementation of intent)** across the full 16-bit range including the critical 255/256/257 boundary the old single-byte code would have broken on, plus 511/512/513 and a 65535 stress case — all produced the exact expected consumed-byte count with no infinite loop or off-by-one, before trusting the assembly. `HISTORY_COMPACT_THRESHOLD` raised 1024→2048 in the same pass to preserve the original ~4x margin over the new budget (already fully 16-bit via `sub16`, so this required no code change, only the constant).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), full clean `make`+`make`+`make progs`+`make test` (39/39 progs+test files, kernel untouched — `Highest address` identical before/after, confirming zero kernel impact both for the original feature and this widening follow-up). D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, and gotcha #18 sweep all re-run via script across the whole file post-widening — zero hits. No duplicate labels. Every call site's register-liveness was checked against its real documented contract rather than assumed: `K_STAT`/`K_FILE_OPEN`/`K_FILE_WRITE`/`K_FILE_CLOSE`/`K_FILE_DELETE`/`K_FILE_RENAME`'s argument conventions confirmed directly from `kernel_api.inc`, and the `shell_strlen`-then-`K_FILE_WRITE` sequence (no `RF` reload in between) confirmed safe by matching it against the already-proven sibling `pipe_write_str`, not just trusted from `shell_strlen`'s own comment. `bin/shell` grew 7382→7920 bytes (mostly the larger `hist_buf`/`hist_lines` static arrays), well within ordinary program-RAM headroom. **The 255/25/1024-budget version is hardware-confirmed; the 512/50/2048-budget widening is build-verified only, not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: ordinary recall/append is unaffected at the new sizes; a file grown past 2048 bytes still compacts and drops back down correctly (the exact mechanism that broke at the old 256-byte boundary, now exercised for real at production scale); Up/Down recall still shows the expected several dozen lines after growing well past the old 255-byte ceiling; and a value straddling the old single-byte boundary (a `hist_loaded_len` genuinely > 255) is naturally exercised once the file exceeds ~256 bytes of kept tail, which the old code could never have reached correctly.

- **Batch `%0`-`%9` argument substitution and `$FOO`/`${FOO}` environment-variable expansion — first hardware round found and fixed one real bug (see its own entry below); testing is otherwise ongoing.** Full design at `~/.claude/plans/glistening-marinating-moler.md`. Third substitution mechanism alongside the already-shipped `%ERRORLEVEL%`, and the first to hit a problem `%ERRORLEVEL%` never had: its replacement text can be *longer* than the token it replaces (a `%1` expanding to a 30-character path, a `$PATH` expanding to a 60-character value), which the old in-place, `RD<=RF`-invariant tokenizer design can't safely absorb. First draft proposed a new fixed 256-byte buffer below `PROG_BASE` to hold the growth — rejected by the user, whose actual goal is moving `PROG_BASE` *down* toward `$4000`, reclaiming margin, not spending more of it. Final design, built around the user's own proposal (refined further mid-design after discovering an already-existing, better-suited kernel mechanism — see below): relocate the line to the *end* of the existing 128-byte `LINE_BUF` and expand forward into the freed space at the front, so the whole substituted line is capped at `LINE_BUF`'s own existing size with **zero new fixed buffers**.
  - **Architecture shift**: substitution moves out of `tok_ordinary`'s own character-by-character scan (where `%ERRORLEVEL%` used to live, woven directly into the tokenizer specifically to see its live quote-state register) into a new, separate pass — `shell_expand_line` — that runs *before* the existing tokenizer, producing a fully-expanded line back in `LINE_BUF` for the *unmodified* tokenizer to process exactly as before (quote-stripping/escape-collapsing are NOT touched by the new pass — it only replaces `%`/`$` tokens verbatim, leaving quote characters and backslash-escapes in place for the real tokenizer to handle afterward, in the same later pass it already did). `%ERRORLEVEL%`'s own old in-place code was retired and folded into this same new mechanism rather than left as a second, parallel substitution system — confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` as the right call before touching already-hardware-confirmed code.
  - **Step 1 (trigger scan, always runs, cheap)**: `shell_strlen` for the line's length, plus one linear scan tracking the same 3-state quote/escape shape the real tokenizer already has (`none`/`'...'`/`"..."`, plus a pending-backslash-escape flag) — for gating substitution eligibility, not for stripping/collapsing. If no eligible (unescaped, non-single-quoted) `%` or `$` is found anywhere: returns immediately, `LINE_BUF` untouched — the common case (most lines have no substitution) pays only this one scan.
  - **Step 2 (relocate, only if Step 1 found something)**: moves the line's current content to the end of `LINE_BUF`, **reserving the very last byte (offset 127) as an explicit NUL sentinel** — content occupies `[127-length, 126]`, one byte less budget than a naive "128 total" in exchange for every scan in Step 3 being able to rely on hitting a real NUL when it runs out of content, exactly like the *old* tokenizer's own established idiom, instead of needing a separate bounds-tracked countdown through every inner dispatch scan. Copies from the highest address down to the lowest — **required** for correctness once the line is over half of 127 bytes (source/destination ranges overlap, and a forward copy would corrupt not-yet-copied source bytes) — independently Python-verified byte-for-byte across every length 0-126 before trusting this, including a deliberate side-by-side proof that a forward copy really does self-corrupt for the overlapping case (not just a style preference).
  - **Step 3 (expand)**: read cursor (`RF`) starts at the relocated content, write cursor (`RD`) at `LINE_BUF+0`. Copies bytes through; on an eligible `%`/`$`, dispatches to `%ERRORLEVEL%` (bookended exact match, reused unchanged from the retired inline version, direct `RUN_ERRORLEVEL` read — no kernel call) → `%<digit>` (open 2-char match, no closing `%` needed, unlike `%ERRORLEVEL%`) → `${FOO}` (braced, checked first since it starts with an unambiguous different next-character) → bare `$FOO` (name ends at the first non-`[A-Za-z0-9_]` character; can't start with a digit, deliberately avoiding any ambiguity with `%1`'s different sigil and matching bash's own identifier rule) → literal, in that dispatch order. **Checked after every single byte written, not just per token** (a long replacement value could otherwise walk `RD` past `RF`'s *current* position — and past `LINE_BUF`'s own physical end — mid-copy, before a boundary-only check ever ran): if `RD` ever reaches or passes `RF`, prints `"Line too long after substitution."` and returns `DF=1`, aborting the line entirely — never a silent truncation or overrun. No recursive re-expansion: a copied replacement's own bytes are never re-scanned for further `%`/`$`, matching bash/DOS's own non-recursive default, for free (the copy loop just walks source bytes into the output; nothing revisits them).
  - **`%<digit>` backed by a new `K_BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE`/`K_BATCH_ARGS_GETARG` pair** (`kernel/batch.asm`, next free jump-table slots `$0182`/`$0185`) — discovered mid-design that `_himem_reserve`/`_himem_release` (`kernel/redir.asm`) is already a general, length-parameterized, flag-agnostic mechanism (generalized 2026-07-21, fully redesigned 2026-07-22 after a real hardware bug in its original stack-relocation approach — the current design never touches `R2` at all, which lives permanently in a fixed margin at the true top of RAM) with two already-proven simultaneous callers (`kernel/redir.asm`'s own dual-redirect FCB pair, `kernel/glob.asm`'s glob-expansion buffer). `kernel_batch_args_reserve`/`_batch_args_release` mirror `kernel/glob.asm`'s own `kernel_glob_reserve`/`_glob_release` shape closely rather than inventing a new reservation routine or touching the existing redirect-specific one (this project's own documented highest-risk kernel change to date). Reserves 149 bytes (`BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE_LEN`): a 128-byte packed-text region (`BATCH_ARGS_TEXT_OFF`=0), a 10-entry pointer table (`BATCH_ARGS_ARGV_OFF`=128), a 1-byte count (`BATCH_ARGS_ARGC_OFF`=148) — all three offsets restated in both `kernel.inc` and `kernel_api.inc`, same "restated, not shared" convention as `RUN_PATH`/`GLOB_BUF_LEN`. One new permanent 1-byte kernel-resident flag (`batch_args_reserved`, same tiny category as `redir_stack_reserved`/`glob_stack_reserved`) — the ~149-byte block itself is temporary, carved out of ordinary program RAM only while a batch is actually running.
    - **Population happens shell-side**, not inside the kernel call — mirrors `K_GLOB_RESERVE`'s own split (kernel reserves and hands back a base address; the caller, which already has the data, writes into it directly). `is_batch:` (`progs/shell.asm`, right after `K_BATCH_START` succeeds) copies up to the first 10 `RUN_ARGV_TABLE` entries' own *string content* into the reserved block — **not** their pointers, called out explicitly per the user's own direct catch during design review: `RUN_ARGV_TABLE[i]` points into `LINE_BUF`, which the very next shell reload overwrites, so `BATCH_ARGV[i]` must be a *new* pointer into the reserved block's own text region, computed as the running write cursor's value at the start of each argument's own copy. `RUN_ARGV_TABLE`/`RUN_ARGC` are confirmed still exactly as `tok_done` left them at this point (neither `K_BATCH_START` nor `K_BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE` touches them) — though the `RA`/`RC` *registers* are already dead by here (`K_STAT` inside `check_exists` clobbers `RA` earlier on this path), so the population loop reads `RUN_ARGV_TABLE`/`RUN_ARGC` fresh from memory, never from a register. On `K_BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE` failure (`DF=1`, insufficient headroom): the batch still runs, just without `%N` support for that run — graceful degradation, not an aborted batch.
    - **`K_BATCH_ARGS_GETARG`'s own contract closes a real gap the user caught before implementation**: rather than a naive design that could return a pointer into an *unpopulated* `BATCH_ARGV` slot (leftover RAM garbage) for an out-of-range index, it always checks the requested index against the *stored* count first. `DF=1` is reserved for exactly one case — no reservation active at all (caller falls back to literal `%<digit>`); whenever a reservation *is* active, it always returns `DF=0` with a valid pointer — either the real argument (index < count) or a dedicated, always-valid empty-string constant (index >= count) — so `%N` beyond what was actually passed comes out as an empty substitution, matching real DOS, never literal text and never garbage. The expansion pass needs no separate "is N in range" logic of its own as a result.
    - **Release**: a new `_batch_args_release` (kernel-internal, no jump-table slot, mirrors `_glob_release`'s exact shape) hooked into `batch_readline`'s own existing EOF and I/O-error close paths (`kernel/batch.asm`) — the same points that already auto-close `batch_fcb`, and that `batch_goto`'s own "label not found" case already reuses to tear the batch down, so both natural batch-end paths release the reservation for free. Ordering with per-command dual-redirect/glob reservations needs no special handling: since all three go through the same `_himem_reserve`/`_himem_release` mechanism (computing every delta relative to whatever `mem_top` currently is, never an assumed baseline), and this reservation is always made before any per-line reservation for the batch ever could be, releases naturally unwind LIFO — exactly the nesting scenario that mechanism's own 2026-07-22 redesign was already proven against.
  - **`$FOO`/`${FOO}` backed entirely by the already-existing `lib/env.asm`** (`env_getenv(RF=name)` → `RF`=value pointer or `0` if unset) — no new storage layer. `progs/shell.asm` didn't previously link against `lib/env.prg`; added an explicit multi-file Makefile rule (`bin/shell: progs/shell.prg lib/env.prg`, both `Makefile` and `Makefile.win`/`build_progs.bat` updated in lockstep), mirroring the already-established `bin/ls`/`bin/more`/`bin/edlin` pattern for the same library. `env_getenv`'s own documented clobber list is broad ("R7, R8, R9, RA, RB, RC, RD, RF") — every register the expansion pass needs to survive the call (`RF`=old read position, `RD`=write cursor, `R8`=quote state; the *new* read position is stashed to memory before the call, safe across it for free) is push/pop-protected, matching the same discipline `%ERRORLEVEL%`'s own retired code already used around `f_uintout`. Unset variable substitutes nothing (matches bash); `\$`/`'$FOO'` need no new code at all, since the expansion pass's own escape/quote gating already treats an escaped or single-quoted `$` as an ordinary character, copied through unchanged for the real tokenizer to finish collapsing exactly as it does today. Accepted cost: each `$VAR`/`${VAR}` reference triggers its own full `env_getenv` call (open+scan+close of `/cfg/env.dat`) — no caching layer, matching this project's established preference for correctness-by-construction and the already-accepted cost profile of `hist_compact`'s own repeated `K_STAT` calls.
  - **Batch-echo timing, confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` before implementing**: batch echo shows the *expanded* text (matches real DOS — substitution happens before a line is displayed or executed), not the raw `%1`/`$FOO` text. Needed the expansion call placed at **two** sites rather than one: in `start_check_echo_off`'s own "echo will actually fire" branch, right before the print (the one path where the echo would otherwise run before any later call got a chance), and unconditionally at `start_have_line`'s own top (covers interactive lines, `@`-prefixed batch lines, and echo-off batch lines — the three paths that never go through the echo-printing branch at all). The ordinary-batch-line-with-echo path calls `shell_expand_line` twice as a result (once before the echo, once at `start_have_line`'s top) — harmless, since Step 1's own trigger scan makes the second call a cheap no-op (nothing left to expand).
  - **Verification**: independent Python simulation, run before trusting any assembly (matching this project's own repeated practice for exactly this class of bug — the tokenizer's two hardware-found bugs, the `%ERRORLEVEL%` `ani $DF`-on-`%` bug), of every genuinely new algorithmic piece: the backward relocate-copy (all lengths 0-127, plus an explicit proof a forward copy self-corrupts), the trigger-scan/expand quote-and-escape state machine, the `%ERRORLEVEL%`/`%N`/`${FOO}`/`$FOO`/literal dispatch ordering (including edge cases like `%9ERRORLEVEL%` and a bare `%E`), the `$FOO` name-boundary scan, and the per-byte overflow check — both its basic firing behavior and a deliberately-documented, deliberately-accepted conservatism: since the check during a token's own replacement-copy compares against the read cursor's *pre-token* position (not yet advanced to reflect that token's own consumption), the very last substitution in a line can't use quite all of the theoretically-available slack — always safe (never under-rejects an actually-overflowing line), just not byte-exact at that one position; confirmed via dedicated multi-substitution-accumulation test cases that this conservatism doesn't affect any point *before* the final token. Standard per-file checklist on every touched file (`progs/shell.asm`, `kernel/batch.asm`, `kernel/kernel.asm`): clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, gotcha #18 sweep, no duplicate labels, proc/endp balanced (7/7 in `batch.asm`) — all clean. Beyond the scripted sweeps (which only catch the cheap, adjacent-instruction form of a clobber bug), a full manual register-liveness re-trace of `shell_expand_line`'s entire body was done against a fresh read of the file, confirming: every push/pop block unwinds in correct LIFO order on *every* exit path including early-exit branches (e.g. `K_BATCH_ARGS_GETARG` returning `DF=1`), `RF` genuinely stays untouched across a *failed* `%ERRORLEVEL%`/`%N` match attempt (allowing a clean fallback restart from the original `%` with no rewind needed), the final NUL-terminator write is provably always in-bounds by construction (the loop's own per-byte invariant already guarantees the write cursor ends strictly below the sentinel's position), and no register-register `add16`/`sub16` ever runs between a `str r2` staging and its consumer anywhere in the new code. All 42 jump-table slots (40 existing + 2 new) decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`; `K_BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE`/`K_BATCH_ARGS_GETARG`'s own targets cross-checked against their real linked addresses. `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (1536 bytes, zero boot-code impact). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (33 `progs/` files including the newly-relinked `bin/shell`, all 0 errors/warnings). Kernel margin **unchanged** at 1599 bytes before `PROG_BASE` (confirms the whole redesign's own point: this feature costs nothing in that gap, only the one 1-byte flag and two jump-table slots) — `bin/shell` itself grew 7920→11459 bytes from the new code plus the `lib/env.prg` linkage, well within ordinary program-RAM headroom.
  - **First hardware round (2026-07-25) found a real bug in `kernel_batch_args_getarg`, root-caused via a targeted diagnostic rather than guessing.** Symptom: `%0`-`%2` (in-range, should be real values) all came back empty, while `%9` (out-of-range, should be empty) produced garbled console output — a wild pointer. A temporary diagnostic added right after `is_batch:`'s own population loop (printing the stored count and `argv[0]`'s dereferenced text via plain `f_inmsg`/`f_uintout`/`f_msg`, matching this project's own "print directly to console, unaffected by anything being tested" convention) confirmed population itself was correct (`count=3`, `argv0text=[/test_args1.bat]`), narrowing the bug to the lookup side. Root cause: **`SM` computes `D = D - M(R(X))`, not `M(R(X)) - D`** — the opposite of what was assumed while writing the comparison. Confirmed by re-deriving the polarity against `_himem_reserve`'s own already-hardware-proven comparison (`kernel/redir.asm`) — its own `"DF=0 means new_mem_top < mem_base"` comment only makes sense under the corrected polarity. The original code staged the *minuend* (index) into `M(R2)` and loaded the *subtrahend* (count) into `D` last — backwards — so it silently computed `count - index` instead of `index - count`: an in-range index (`count > index`, no borrow) wrongly took the "empty" branch, while the out-of-range index (`count < index`, borrow) wrongly fell into the "real arg" branch and read an uninitialized `BATCH_ARGV` slot — exactly the observed wild-pointer garbage. Fixed by swapping the staging order (subtrahend staged first, minuend loaded last, right before `SM`). Swept the rest of this session's new code for the same mistake: every other raw (non-`SUB16`-macro) `SM` usage introduced this session is an *equality* check (`lbz`/`lbnz`), where the sign is irrelevant, so this was the only real instance — confirmed by checking each one individually, not assumed. The temporary diagnostic and its scratch data were fully removed once root-caused (`grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC"` clean). Re-verified after the fix: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`, all sweeps clean, all 42 jump-table slots decoded and confirmed, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, kernel margin unchanged at 1599 bytes.
  - **Second hardware round (2026-07-25), after the `SM`-polarity fix — `%N`/`%0` now correctly resolve, but found a SECOND real bug: `%ERRORLEVEL%` printed garbage-looking numbers (137, 159) instead of the real 0/1 exit codes.** `type.asm` was confirmed (by direct source read) to correctly return `D=1` on "file not found," and `run_loop`'s own capture of that into `RUN_ERRORLEVEL` (`kernel/kernel.asm`) was confirmed unchanged and correct — so the bug had to be in the *new* `shell_expand_line` code specifically. Root cause: `f_uintout`'s own calling convention takes the value to convert in `RD` (matching the original, already-proven inline `%ERRORLEVEL%` code this replaced), but the rewrite loaded `RUN_ERRORLEVEL`'s value into `R9` instead and never actually copied it into `RD` before the call — so `f_uintout` silently converted whatever `RD` *already* held at that point (the write cursor's own current address, a value like `$0080`-something) to decimal, instead of the real errorlevel value. This exactly explains "137"/"159": not garbage, but the write cursor's own address read out as a plain number. Fixed by adding `mov rd, r9` immediately before the `f_uintout` call, after `RD` (the real write cursor) had already been pushed to protect it — matching the exact register roles the original inline code used, just carried across the same push/pop protection block the rewrite already had in place for other reasons. Checked for the same class of mistake elsewhere in the new code (any other call where a value needs to land in a *specific* register by convention, not just whichever register happens to hold it) — `K_BATCH_ARGS_GETARG` (`D`=index, set correctly via `glo r9`) and `env_getenv` (`RF`=name, set correctly) were both already right; `f_uintout` was the only place this specific mistake was made. Re-verified after the fix: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep clean, no duplicate labels, full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`, kernel untouched this round (margin/`krnboot.bin` unchanged, confirmed byte-identical, since only `progs/shell.asm` changed).
