  - **Third hardware round (2026-07-25, `test_args1.bat` re-run) — CONFIRMED PASSING in full**: `%0`-`%2` real values, `%7` empty, `%ERRORLEVEL%` correctly `0`/`1`, `$FOO`/`${FOO}` set/unset, all quoting cases (`'$TESTVAR'` literal, `"$TESTVAR"`/bare substituted, `\$TESTVAR` literal, `'%1'` literal), and both overflow cases (short line unaffected, tripled `$BIGVAR` reference correctly triggers "Line too long after substitution." rather than emitting truncated/garbage text).
  - **Fourth hardware round: `test_args2.bat`'s original form (`edlin %1 < NUL > dualredir_out.txt`, i.e. the nested-reservation case — a batch-args reservation active, PLUS a dual I/O redirect on the same line, PLUS a large program) failed with `"Invalid program file."`.** Swapping `edlin` for `echo` in the identical scenario succeeded (confirmed via a revised `test_args2.bat`, now using `echo %1 < NUL > dualredir_out.txt` — this revision also fixed an unrelated test-authoring mistake, a descriptive comment line containing literal `<`/`>` characters that the tokenizer correctly parsed as real redirect operators against nonexistent files "and"/"together," explaining that line's own missing output). Running `edlin` completely standalone (no batch, no nested reservation) with the same dual-redirect flags also succeeded. So the failure is specific to the *combination* — large program + active batch-args reservation + dual redirect — not any one of those three alone. Manual RAM-budget arithmetic (cross-checked against the user's own math: ~60K total RAM, ~17.9K kernel/`PROG_BASE` overhead, ~10.5K for `edlin`'s own code, ~693 bytes for both reservations combined) suggested roughly 32K of headroom should remain, which doesn't explain an out-of-memory-shaped failure — so the cause is still open. **Diagnostic added** (`kernel/loader.asm`'s `prog_run`, 2026-07-25): the previously-shared `prun_err` target for all three of `prog_run`'s own failure branches (`file_open`, `_prog_finish_load`, `_redir_setup`) was split into three distinct labels — `prun_err_open`/`prun_err_load`/`prun_err_redir` — each printing a distinct tag via `f_inmsg` (plus `mem_top`/`prog_size` for the load-fail case, `mem_top`/`mem_base` for the redir-fail case, via `f_uintout`/`f_msg` — matching the project's own established diagnostic idiom, reading each value fresh into `RD` immediately before its own `f_uintout` call since neither register is trusted to survive an unaudited BIOS call) before falling through to the original shared `prun_err: stc/rtn`. `test_args2.bat` restored to its original edlin-based form to reproduce the failure with this diagnostic in place. Verified: clean assemble, D-clobber sweep (hand-traced — the diagnostic's own `lda`/`phi` and `ldn`/`plo` pairs are always adjacent with no intervening clobber, matching the established `mov rf, X` / `lda rf` / `phi rd` / `ldn rf` / `plo rd` idiom already used elsewhere in this same file), all 41 jump-table slots decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr` (the two new `K_BATCH_ARGS_*` slots' targets also cross-checked against their real linked addresses), `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`. Margin: `Highest address` `$40c5`, **1339 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** — healthy. **Needs a hardware round**: re-run `test_args2.bat` with this diagnostic build and report which of `PRUN:open-fail` / `PRUN:load-fail mem_top=... prog_size=...` / `PRUN:redir-fail mem_top=... mem_base=...` prints, to pin down exactly which of the three steps is failing and what the real numbers are at that point. Remove the diagnostic once root-caused (`grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" kernel/loader.asm` before calling it done).
  - **Remaining test list**: the nested-reservation dual-redirect case with `edlin` specifically (blocked on the diagnostic above); a batch ending via normal EOF and one via `GOTO` to an undefined label confirming the reservation releases both ways — **CONFIRMED PASSING** (`test_args_neg.bat`, GOTO-to-undefined-label path); a full regression pass (`IF`/`GOTO`, `REM`, blank lines, plain quoting/escaping generally) — **CONFIRMED PASSING** as part of the rounds above.
  - **CLOSED (2026-07-25/26), most likely explanation: pre-existing directory corruption, not a redirect/reservation bug.** The very next hardware round (with the `prog_run` diagnostic in place) ran the exact original failing scenario (`/test_args2.bat dualtest.txt`) and it **succeeded** — no `"Invalid program file."`, no `PRUN:...` tag printed at all (meaning `prog_run` never hit any of its three failure branches). A second run the following day, on the build that also carried the *short-name-collision* fix (see that entry above), succeeded again. Both runs' `dualredir_out.txt` correctly contained just `*` (real EDLIN's own command prompt, redirected to the file, followed by a clean exit once `K_INPUTL` hit EOF on the NUL-redirected input) — a genuinely correct result, not a coincidental non-crash. The user's own theory, and the most plausible explanation given the evidence: the original failure happened while the SAME root directory (`dualtest.txt`/`dualredir_out.txt`/`/bin/edlin` all live there) had a real, fsck-confirmed duplicate short-name entry (`test_args1.bat`/`test_args2.bat`'s own collision, see above) — a corrupted/duplicate entry sitting in that directory could plausibly disrupt an unrelated scan (resolving `/bin/edlin`, or creating `dualredir_out.txt`) if it happened to fall within the scanned region. `fsck.fat` repaired the existing corruption and the collision-avoidance fix stops new ones from forming; the exact scenario has passed cleanly twice since. Not provable in hindsight (the `prog_run` diagnostic didn't exist yet during the original failure, so there's no captured `PRUN:...` tag to confirm which step it was), but two clean passes plus a coherent, evidence-consistent story is enough to close this out rather than keep chasing a scenario that no longer reproduces. **Diagnostic fully removed** from `kernel/loader.asm` (`prun_err_open`/`prun_err_load`/`prun_err_redir` collapsed back to the single shared `prun_err`, `prun_diag_buf` removed) — confirmed via `grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC"` across the whole tree (kernel/progs/lib/test/boot), zero hits. Re-verified: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings), `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, all 41 jump-table slots decoded `0xC0`/`lbr`, `bin`/`test/bin` reconfirmed populated (31/8). Margin recovered: `Highest address` `$421d`, **995 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** — healthiest it's been in several rounds, now that both this session's diagnostic builds are fully cleaned up.

- **`_file_create` short-name collision avoidance — IMPLEMENTED, ONE ROUND OF HARDWARE FAILURE FOUND AND FIXED, CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE 2026-07-25.** Found via a real hardware fsck report, not a code review: after `mr`-transferring `test_args1.bat` and `test_args2.bat` in the same session, `fsck.fat` reported `"Duplicate directory entry"` under `/test_args1.bat` — two entries with sizes matching test_args1.bat (1087 bytes) and test_args2.bat (466 bytes) respectively, auto-renamed to `FSCK0000.003` by fsck (a destructive "fix" that can orphan an LFN). Root cause: `_gen_short_name`'s fallback 8.3 name generation (`kernel/file.asm`) is a plain first-8-characters truncation with **no uniqueness check** — a long-documented, deliberate gap (its own header comment argued this was safe since "this system never looks up a file by its raw short name, only by dir_read's LFN-preferred name"). That reasoning covered ELF-DOS's own lookups but not `fsck.fat`, which correctly treats a duplicate 11-byte short name within one directory as a genuine FAT16 spec violation. `test_args1.bat`/`test_args2.bat` both truncate their name part to the identical first 8 characters (`TEST_ARG`), so both got the literal same short name — not an "overwrite" bug as first suspected, but a **collision between two unrelated new-file creations**, likely also responsible for other fsck errors this session given the project's own `test_*` naming conventions.
  - **Fix, two new pieces in `kernel/file.asm`**: a new proc `_check_shortname_collision` (placed between `_gen_short_name` and `_file_create`) does a full raw `dir_open`+scan of the target directory, comparing each valid (non-deleted, non-LFN, non-volume-label) entry's raw 11-byte `DE_NAME` directly against the just-generated `fc_shortname` — deliberately NOT `dir_read`'s LFN-preferred display name, which is exactly the wrong thing to compare for a short-name collision. Since this disturbs `dir.asm`'s live scan state (`dir_sect`/`dir_eptr`/`dir_eleft`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_buf`/`dir_lfn_ok`) that `_file_create`'s own later code depends on describing the terminator's position, it's saved before the scan and restored (`dir_buf` via a fresh `f_ideread`, not a literal copy) immediately after — the same "save real state, do risky work, restore before the caller's own tail runs" shape as `dir_remove`'s own empty-check scan around `_delete_located_entry`. `_file_create` itself gained a retry loop, running only when `fc_needs_lfn` is set (a clean, already-8.3 name can't collide here — an exact duplicate would already have been caught by `file_open`'s own existing-file scan before `_file_create` is ever reached): captures the original 8-byte name part (`fc_base8`) and its real (non-padding) character count once, then on each collision mutates the name part to `(first min(namepart_len,6) real chars) + '~' + digit` (matching real DOS's own numeric-tail convention, extension left untouched) and rechecks, trying `~1` through `~9` before giving up (`DF=1`, matching the existing "directory full" failure path) — a deliberately bounded, simplified single-digit scope rather than DOS's full multi-digit escalation, judged more than sufficient for this project's realistic collision counts.
  - **Verification**: independent Python simulation of the whole algorithm (truncation, real-character-count scan, `~1`-`~9` mutation, exhaustion after 9) before trusting the assembly, covering the exact real bug case (`test_args1.bat`/`test_args2.bat` → `TEST_ARG`/`TEST_A~1`), a short-name-under-6-chars collision (correct padding), and full exhaustion of all 10 slots (bare + `~1`..`~9`) — all passed. Full manual register-liveness re-trace of `_check_shortname_collision`'s save/restore symmetry (confirmed the restore reads from the saved copies, never from post-scan live state) and of every D-clobber-risk site in the new code, beyond the standard scripted sweeps (D-clobber 3-line shape, self-overwrite, gotcha #18 register-register `add16`/`sub16` between a `str r2` stage and its consumer) — all clean, 0 hits. No duplicate labels. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout), all 41 jump-table slots decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`, `_check_shortname_collision`'s own linked address cross-checked via `link02 -s`, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (pure `kernel/file.asm` change, zero boot-code impact). **Margin cost is real and worth noting**: `Highest address` `$40c5`→`$42f7`, margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` dropped from 1339 to **777 bytes** — a legitimate structural fix, not bloat, but the next kernel addition should re-check this margin carefully. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: two files whose names collide in their first-8-truncated-characters (e.g. re-running the original `test_args1.bat`/`test_args2.bat` `mr` transfers) produce genuinely distinct short names and a clean `fsck.fat` afterward; and a full regression of the already-hardware-confirmed creation paths this touches transitively (`WTEST`/`ATEST`/`COPY`/`MD`/`REN`, all of which call `_file_create` for names needing an LFN, the common case) to confirm the new collision-check-and-restore doesn't disturb anything when there's no actual collision.
  - **First hardware round (2026-07-25): `mr test_args1.bat` worked, but `mr test_args2.bat` hung the machine unresponsive** — not just a slow transfer, genuinely stuck, unrecoverable without a power cycle (a real regression from this feature, worse than the fsck cosmetic issue it fixes). Extensive static re-review of `_check_shortname_collision`'s save/restore symmetry and scan-loop termination found nothing conclusively wrong there. **Root cause, found via a careful register-liveness re-trace rather than by guessing**: `fc_shortname_ready` (`_file_create`) reads `ghi rd`/`glo rd` expecting `RD` to still hold `namelen`, unchanged since `_gen_short_name`'s return far above — true before this feature existed, no longer true after it: whenever `fc_needs_lfn` is set (the common case — any lowercase or >8-char name), the new collision-check block now calls `_check_shortname_collision` at least once in between, and its own documented `Modifies` list includes `RD`. So `fc_shortname_ready` was reading **garbage** for `namelen` on every single LFN-needing file creation, not just a colliding one — feeding a wrong (potentially huge) value straight into the LFN-entry-count computation, which could write far past its intended bounds in the LFN-writing loop that follows. This exactly explains the asymmetry: `test_args1.bat`'s create also went through `_check_shortname_collision` once (no collision, but still called), and whatever garbage `RD` happened to hold that time was apparently small/benign; `test_args2.bat`'s create (same code path, different on-disk directory state feeding different garbage) hit a pathological value and hung. A textbook instance of this project's own gotcha #10 ("a subroutine's documented return/modified registers are exactly the ones most likely to get silently clobbered by a careless caller, because they don't look like scratch") — self-inflicted this time, by code written the same session. **Fixed** by reloading `RD` fresh from `fc_namelen` (memory-resident, correctly preserved throughout regardless of any intervening call) at the very top of `fc_shortname_ready`, before either path (needs-LFN or clean-8.3) reads it — safe and correct for both, since `fc_namelen` was already written immediately after `_gen_short_name` returned, before any branching.
  - **Defensive hardening added alongside the fix, kept regardless of root-cause confidence**: `_check_shortname_collision`'s own scan loop gained a hard cap (64 sectors, far more than any realistic directory) that forces "no collision" (the safe, pre-fix-equivalent fallback) if ever exceeded — makes the scan structurally unable to truly hang no matter what future bug might be lurking, at the cost of a theoretical (currently unreachable in practice) missed collision on a pathologically large directory. **Temporary diagnostics also added** (`; TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC` markers, `kernel/file.asm`): `_check_shortname_collision` prints `[CSC start]`/`[CSC opened]`, a `.` once per sector examined (not per-entry — a tight per-character `f_tty` loop is itself a confirmed hardware hazard, gotcha #14), and `[CSC hit]`/`[CSC none]`/`[CSC CAPPED]` at its three exit points; `_file_create`'s retry loop prints `[FC retry n=N]` before each `_check_shortname_collision` call. Kept in for this round as a safety net/confirmation aid even though the `RD` bug is a high-confidence, clearly-mechanistic fix — remove once hardware confirms (`grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" kernel/file.asm`).
  - **Verified**: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep re-run across the whole file including `push`/`pop` as clobber-risk instructions (a fact learned mid-fix: `POP`'s own macro expansion — `IRX`/`LDXA`/`PLO`/`LDX`/`PHI` — ends with a plain `LDX`, so it clobbers `D` too, exactly gotcha #4's pattern; caught and fixed in the new cap-check code itself, before it ever reached hardware, by stashing the sector count in `R9` before the `pop ra` rather than trusting `D` to survive it), 0 hits. No duplicate labels. All 41 jump-table slots decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical. Margin: `Highest address` `$43c3`, **573 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** — down further from the diagnostics, tight but positive; plan to recover most of it once the diagnostics are removed post-confirmation. **Also discovered and fixed along the way**: `bin/`/`test/bin/` went missing after an unrelated `make clean` was run without a following `make progs`/`make test` (user caught this) — both directories rebuilt (31 and 8 binaries respectively) and reconfirmed present before this round's kernel changes.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-25, second round)**: `mr test_args1.bat` succeeded normally; `mr test_args2.bat` hit the real collision (`[FC retry n=0]` → `[CSC hit]` → `[FC retry n=1]` → `[CSC none]`) and completed successfully via the `~1` mutation, exactly matching the design and the earlier Python simulation's own predicted `TEST_ARG`→`TEST_A~1` outcome — no hang. All temporary diagnostics removed afterward (the hard 64-sector cap was kept as permanent defensive code, not a diagnostic — it costs nothing when never triggered and makes the scan structurally unable to hang regardless of any future bug); `grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" kernel/file.asm` confirmed clean. Final re-verification post-removal: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep (including `push`/`pop`) 0 hits, no duplicate labels, all 41 jump-table slots decoded `0xC0`/`lbr`, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, `bin`/`test/bin` reconfirmed populated (31/8). Margin: `Highest address` `$4322`, **734 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** — recovered most of the diagnostic-round cost, though still somewhat tighter than this feature's original 777-byte post-implementation number (the `RD`-reload fix and the permanent scan cap both cost a little real code). This closes out the short-name collision bug hunt — still outstanding: an `fsck.fat` re-run to visually confirm no more "Duplicate directory entry" reports, and the separate, still-open `edlin`+`%N`-reservation+dual-redirect nested-hang investigation from earlier in this session (unrelated code path, its own diagnostic still in `kernel/loader.asm`).
  - **"Deep truncation" escalation to a 2-digit `~10`-`~99` tail, added 2026-07-26 at the user's own request** ("that would allow for up to 100 files with the same prefix... I think that's a gracious plenty and we don't need to then go to a checksum hash fallback"). Extends `_file_create`'s retry loop: once `~1`-`~9` (6-char base) are all taken, escalates to `~10`-`~99` (5-char base, 2 ASCII digits), giving 100 total names sharing one truncated prefix (the original candidate + `~1`..`~99`) before giving up — matches real DOS's own actual first-stage escalation, deliberately stopping there rather than DOS's further hash-based fallback for pathological collision counts, which the user judged unnecessary for this project's realistic use. Implementation: `fc_suffix_n`'s exhaustion bound moved from `smi 10` to `smi 100`; a new bounded (max 9 iterations, `fc_suffix_n` is always ≤ 99 at this point) tens/ones split via repeated `smi 10` subtraction feeds two new fields, `fc_suffix_tens`/`fc_suffix_ones`; the base-length cap (previously a single `min(namepart_len, 6)`) branches on whether `fc_suffix_tens` is zero — 6 for a single-digit suffix, 5 for a two-digit one, each via the same already-proven `smi <constant>`/`lbdf` idiom the original code used (just a second copy with `5` instead of `6`, avoiding a runtime-relative `sm` comparison entirely — deliberately chosen over a shared computation to keep both cap checks on the well-proven "compare against a compile-time constant" shape rather than introduce a new comparison pattern); the digit-write and pad-count code each gained a parallel tens-nonzero branch, both explicitly `lbr`-terminated to avoid the exact "missing branch after a restructure" bug class this project has hit twice before (the argv/argc tokenizer's own hardware-found bug, and `mr.asm`/`ms.asm`'s migration bug) — every new branch point was checked by hand for a required explicit `lbr` rather than an assumed fallthrough. **Verified**: independent Python simulation before trusting the assembly (real bug case unaffected; all 100 slots filled with genuinely unique 11-byte names across a synthetic 100-file collision, confirming the 5-char/6-char base-length crossover at `~10` and the exact 8-byte width at every step; 101st candidate correctly fails; a short-name-under-5-chars case escalating into the 2-digit form pads correctly). Full manual register-liveness re-trace of the whole rewritten block against a fresh read (the `fc_suffix_tens`-based base_len/digit-write/pad-count branches all confirmed to preserve `R9`'s `base_len` value correctly across the intervening copy/tilde/digit-write code, and every `lbdf`/`lbnz`/`lbz` branch point traced for the D value it depends on). Standard sweeps (D-clobber incl. `push`/`pop`, self-overwrite, gotcha #18, duplicate labels) all clean, full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings), all 41 jump-table slots decoded `0xC0`/`lbr`, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical, `bin`/`test/bin` reconfirmed populated (31/8). Margin: `Highest address` `$4288`, **888 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`**. **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-26)**: user created `dupefile01.txt` through `dupefile15.txt` (15 files, all sharing the 8-char truncated prefix `DUPEFILE`, deliberately going 5 past the `~10` crossover "for good measure") — all 15 appear correctly in `DIR`, and `fsck.fat` came back clean, confirming the 2-digit escalation produces genuinely unique short names past `~9` on real hardware. This closes out the short-name collision bug hunt in full, including the deep-truncation enhancement.

- **`COPY -y` switch — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland (`progs/copy.asm`), zero kernel changes.** User's own suggestion, exploratory-question-then-confirmed: auto-confirm the overwrite prompt, matching real DOS `COPY`'s own `/Y` switch and this project's existing `XCOPY -y` precedent — useful for batch-script use, where an interactive Y/N prompt is exactly what needs suppressing.
  - **First pass**: `-y` recognized only as `argv[1]` (the very first argument) — detected via a single exact-match check (`'-'`/`'y'`/NUL), then RA/RC adjusted in place (`RA += 2`, `RC -= 1`) to make the flag disappear from every downstream line's view, without touching any other code in the file.
  - **Widened same day, at the user's own follow-up request** ("surely it wouldn't be a huge effort to use the same strategy that other commands use to allow the -y option to appear anywhere"): replaced the position-1 check with a full one-pass filter over the *entire* argv line into a new local, owned array (`copy_argv_local`, sized `ARGV_MAX_ARGS*2` — the same bound the real argv table itself is capped at, so it can never overflow) — every exact `-y` token is dropped and `argc` reduced to match; `argv[0]` (this program's own name, never read again) is always kept unconditionally so index 1 onward lines up exactly as if `-y` had never been typed. The real argv table (`RA`) is read-only throughout the scan, never mutated — deliberately avoids touching shell/kernel-owned memory that isn't this program's own, in favor of building a private copy. `copy_argv`/`copy_argc` (already the single indirection every downstream line in this file reads through, never `RA`/`RC` directly) are simply pointed at the filtered array/count instead of the raw ones — **the widen required updating `start_argcheck`'s own old "stash argv+argc from RA/RC" block**, which would otherwise have silently overwritten the freshly-filtered values with the original unfiltered ones (caught during implementation, before ever assembling, by tracing what `start_argcheck` still referenced after the rewrite — a real near-miss, since the original single-position version never needed to touch that block at all).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble both passes (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, gotcha #18 sweep, no duplicate labels — all clean on the final widened version. Full manual register-liveness re-trace of the whole filter loop (confirmed `RD` — the current entry's string pointer — survives unclobbered through the three-character comparison, since only `RF` is used as scratch there; confirmed `RA` is never written, only read via `mov rf, ra` copies; confirmed `R8`'s surviving-count value is still valid at `cy_filter_done`, untouched since its last update inside the loop). Full clean `make progs` (31/31, 0 errors/warnings, kernel untouched — this is a pure `progs/` change). **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: `copy -y old.txt existing.txt` overwrites silently with no prompt; a plain `copy old.txt existing.txt` still prompts as before (regression check); `-y` recognized correctly when placed *after* the source/destination arguments too (e.g. `copy old.txt existing.txt -y`), not just before — the specific case the widening was for; and that a real destination-directory multi-source copy (`copy -y *.txt somedir`) still resolves `dst_ptr`/`num_sources` correctly against the filtered array.

- **File I/O widened to support files/positions beyond 64K — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Motivated by the user's own real use case: porting Elf/OS software with a ~90K data file, well past this project's previous, deliberate 64K ceiling. Preceded by a genuine architectural discussion (see the entry below) about whether to switch `FCB_FSIZE`/`FCB_FPOS` to little-endian storage first, matching ELFC's own convention — after actually tracing the instruction-level tradeoffs (not just accepting the analogy), concluded the benefit was smaller than it first looked once the arithmetic is done via this codebase's own established two-register-pair `ADD16`/`SUB16`/`SM`+`SMB` idiom (endianness-independent once bytes are loaded into registers; the real cost driver is DE_SIZE's own on-disk little-endian layout vs. `_fclose_rewrite_size`'s reversal-copy, which nets out close to a wash once `file_open`'s own dirent-copy is counted too) — so kept `FCB_FSIZE`/`FCB_FPOS` big-endian, unchanged, and widened the *arithmetic* instead. This kept the change surgical: only `file_read`/`file_write`/`file_open`'s append-mode positioning/`file_seek` needed touching; `_fclose_rewrite_size`, `prog_run_shell`'s fast-path FCB population, and the on-disk `DE_SIZE` round-trip were *already* fully 32-bit-correct (confirmed by reading the code directly) and needed zero changes.
  - **`file_read`**: the `file_remaining = FSIZE - FPOS` EOF check widened to a genuine 4-byte `SM`/`SMB` borrow chain (was 2 bytes, silently ignoring the high word). The result is saturated to a 16-bit `$FFFF` "huge" sentinel for the *existing*, unchanged downstream chunk-clamp logic — a single `K_FILE_READ` call's own byte count and a sector's own size are both inherently ≤65535, so a `file_remaining` bigger than that can never be the limiting factor, only whether it's genuinely zero (true EOF) needed the real 32-bit compare. The separate per-chunk `FCB_FPOS += chunk` step (later in the same loop) widened to a real 32-bit add-with-carry (`ADD16` for the low word, its own final `DF` propagated into the high word via the standard `adci 0`/`adci 0` chain already used elsewhere in this codebase).
  - **`file_write`**: same `FPOS += chunk` widening. Real register pressure here — `R7` (holding `chunk`) has to survive the *entire* block untouched, since `fwrite_no_grow`'s own `RC -= chunk` (reached on *both* the grew and no-grow paths) still needs it — so the new "did the file grow" check (`FPOS >= FSIZE`) couldn't hold both 32-bit values in registers simultaneously; solved by comparing directly against *memory* via a backward pointer-walk (both fields are MSB-first on disk, so an LSB-first borrow chain walks from each field's last byte, offset+3, down to its first) instead of loading full values into register pairs. `FSIZE = FPOS` (on growth) is a plain straight 4-byte copy, since both fields share the same big-endian layout — no reversal needed.
  - **`file_open`'s append-mode positioning** (the biggest single piece): the old code's own comments admitted `"sector_index (0-127)"` — a hardcoded single-byte assumption, not just a truncated `FSIZE` symptom. Widened `last_byte_index = FSIZE - 1` to a real 32-bit decrement-with-borrow (`sub16` on the low word, conditionally propagated into the high word). The `>>9` shift is done as `>>8` (a *pure byte reassignment*, no shift instructions needed at all — verified this is genuinely free) followed by `>>1` (a `SHRC` chain from the MSB byte down to the LSB byte). `cluster_index = sector_index >> spc_shift` widened from an 8-bit to a 16-bit variable-shift-count loop (same already-hardware-proven "reload the accumulator fresh right before each `shr`, don't trust it across the loop condition's own `D` clobber" discipline the original single-byte version had already learned the hard way, per its own preserved history comment). `fa_cluster_idx` widened 1→2 bytes as a `#define` offset shift within `_shared_scratch` (still fits comfortably in its existing 9-byte allocation — no size increase needed). The `fat_get` hop-count walk widened to a 16-bit counter. Final `FCB_FPOS = FCB_FSIZE` widened to a straight 4-byte copy.
  - **`file_seek`**: the old 16-bit-sign-extension-only offset validation removed entirely — the full signed 32-bit range is now accepted directly. `SEEK_SET`/`CUR`/`END` target computation widened to real 32-bit arithmetic; the `target = base + offset` validity check generalizes the *already-proven* 16-bit version's own carry-out-plus-offset-sign-bit reasoning unchanged, just extended from 2 bytes to 4 (independently Python-verified against 5000+ randomized + edge cases before writing any assembly, since — per that original 16-bit code's own comment — "2's-complement carry reasoning is easy to get subtly wrong"). The range check (`0 <= target <= FSIZE`) widened to a 4-byte `SM`/`SMB` chain. The repositioning computation (`fsk_general`) is a close copy of `file_open`'s own newly-widened append-mode algorithm, with its own `fsk_*` scratch fields (`fsk_target` widened 2→4 bytes, `fsk_cluster_idx` widened 1→2 bytes) — **deliberately kept as a second, separate copy rather than factored into one shared subroutine**, matching this codebase's own pre-existing choice not to share state between `file_open`'s append-mode and `file_seek` ("each already complex enough on their own"); factoring it out remains a viable future simplification if kernel margin gets tight again, not attempted this round to keep this already-large change's blast radius contained. Final `FCB_FPOS = target` widened to a straight 4-byte copy; the *return value* (`RD`) deliberately still only carries the low word, matching `K_FILE_SEEK`'s existing published contract — widening the return convention itself would be a separate, bigger ABI decision, out of scope here.
  - **A real bug, caught by re-tracing rather than by the build or sweeps.** The `>>8` byte-reassignment step (both in `file_open`'s append-mode and, since it was copied from there, in `file_seek` too) read `glo r8` (R8's *low* byte) when it needed `ghi r8` (R8's *high* byte) — a plain transcription slip, not a class of bug any D-clobber/self-overwrite/gotcha-18 sweep is designed to catch, and one that a first, less careful manual trace also missed (matching the same lesson gotcha-hunting has taught repeatedly on this project: a clean build and clean sweeps only rule out the specific bug *classes* those tools check for). Found only on a second, more skeptical re-trace while transcribing the same logic into `file_seek`, by re-deriving `R8`'s actual byte layout from its own load sequence a few lines above and catching the mismatch against the (already-correct) Python model. Both sites fixed identically. Re-verified afterward with something stronger than a third manual read: a from-scratch **mechanical 1802-instruction-level simulator** (`Sim` class modeling `GLO`/`GHI`/`PLO`/`PHI`/`ANI`/`SHR`/`SHRC` with real `D`/`DF` semantics) that executes the *literal* corrected instruction sequence — not a re-derived model of intended behavior — across 2000+ random 32-bit values for the `>>9` step alone, then the *full* `boff`/`sector_index`/`cluster_index`/`sector_in_clust` pipeline together, then separately for `file_read`'s subtract chain, `file_write`'s add-with-carry chain and backward-walk compare, and `file_seek`'s add32-with-validation and range-check — all passed. This project's own established practice ("independently simulate new arithmetic before trusting assembly") already called for Python verification of the *algorithm*; this round additionally simulated the *literal instruction sequence itself*, which is what actually caught this bug — worth keeping as the standard for any future register-shuffling code like this, not just algorithm-level modeling.
  - **Verified**: full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout), D-clobber/self-overwrite/gotcha-18 sweeps clean across the whole file, no duplicate labels, all 41 jump-table slots decoded `0xC0`/`lbr`, `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (pure `kernel/file.asm` change), `bin`/`test/bin` reconfirmed populated (31/8). Stale "64K ceiling" doc comments updated throughout (`kernel_api.inc`'s `K_FILE_SEEK` contract, `file.asm`'s own top-of-file FCB-structure comment, `progs/hexdump.asm`/`progs/dir.asm`'s own display-column comments corrected to note the limit is now in *their* own 16-bit display arithmetic, not a kernel limitation — those two programs' own output would still wrap/truncate past 64K until separately widened, a follow-on not attempted here). **Margin cost was far smaller than estimated going in** (a reasoned pre-implementation estimate of 300-500 bytes, given without an exhaustive line-by-line trace): `Highest address` `$4288`→`$42fa`, margin before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` **774 bytes** (down from 888) — the *entire* widening across all four routines cost only **114 bytes**, since most of the "extra" work turned out to be reusing already-proven arithmetic idioms one more byte-pair wider, not new algorithmic complexity. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: a file >64K can be created (e.g. via repeated `COPY`/`MR` appends or a large source transfer) and read back correctly across the 64K boundary; `file_seek` to an absolute position past 64K lands correctly (verify via a subsequent read); append-mode (`ATEST`-style) positioning is correct for a file that size; and a full regression of the already-hardware-confirmed small-file paths (`WTEST`/`ATEST`/`COPY`/`TYPE`/`MD`/`REN`) to confirm the widened arithmetic didn't disturb the common case.
  - **Batch-as-a-loadable-module — discussed, not implemented this round.** The same conversation that prompted the >64K work also raised making batch processing (`kernel/batch.asm`) a dynamically-loaded `/bin/batch.mod`, loaded into `_himem_reserve`d space only while a `.bat` script runs, to reclaim kernel margin toward the standing goal of moving `PROG_BASE` down from `$4600` toward `$4000`. Assessed as feasible and worth doing (batch's own permanent footprint is roughly 900-1100+ bytes by this session's own margin-delta history, and `_himem_reserve`/`_himem_release` is already proven for exactly this shape of dynamic allocation) but architecturally new in one real way: every existing `_himem_reserve` use is scoped to a single command's `run_loop` iteration, while a loaded batch module would need to survive across *many* iterations (one per script line), with each line's own transient reservations (dual redirect, `%N`-args, glob) nesting inside it — LIFO-safe in principle given the mechanism's own 2026-07-22 nesting redesign, but not yet built or tested. `K_BATCH_START`/`READLINE`/`GOTO` would need to become indirect calls through a stored module-base pointer (conceptually similar to `prog_exec`'s own computed-address call). Not started — a candidate for a future, dedicated session.

- **`DIR`'s size column widened to a right-justified, comma-grouped 13-character field — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland (`progs/dir.asm`), zero kernel changes.** Direct follow-on to the same day's kernel file-size widening: `4,294,967,295` (the largest 32-bit value) is exactly 13 characters (10 digits + 3 commas), matching the column width the user asked for. Replaces the old plain 5-column `f_uintout` of the low 16 bits only.
  - **The hard part: this project has no existing 32-bit-to-decimal conversion anywhere, since the 1802 has no divide instruction and `f_uintout` is 16-bit-only.** New `_div32_by10` (standard "restoring division" via 32 bit-shifts — each iteration shifts the 32-bit dividend left by 1, low byte first via `SHL` then `SHLC`/`SHLC`/`SHLC` chained up through the high byte, the bit that falls off the very top feeds a small remainder accumulator, and whenever that reaches 10 the dividend's own just-vacated LSB is set to 1 as the quotient bit for that position — after 32 iterations the dividend register holds the quotient and the accumulator holds the remainder). New `print_size32` calls it repeatedly to extract up to 10 decimal digits least-significant-first, then walks them back out most-significant-first into the display buffer, inserting a comma after digit `i` (right-indexed, `i=0` = least significant) whenever `i>0` and `i` is a multiple of 3 — a `mod3_table[0..9]` lookup rather than a computed modulo, verified to be independent of the total digit count (correctly handles a 1-digit leading group like the `"4,"` in the max-value case, not just even multiples of 3).
  - **Verified in three independent layers, not just one, given how much the file I/O work's own instruction-level bug earlier the same day taught about the limits of "looks right on read"**: (1) a pure-Python numeric model of the whole algorithm (division + comma rule) against `f"{v:,}"` across 2000+ random 32-bit values plus edge cases; (2) a from-scratch mechanical mock-1802 simulator (`GLO`/`GHI`/`PLO`/`PHI`/`SHL`/`SHLC`/`SMI`/`ORI` with real `D`/`DF` semantics) executing the *literal* instruction sequence — not a re-derived model — across the same range; (3) a full re-read of the actual written assembly side-by-side with the mechanical model.
  - **A real, severe bug caught by that third layer, not by the build or either simulation**: `psz_build_loop` computes array offsets via `add16 r8, r9` (indexing both `raw_digits` and `mod3_table`), where `R9`'s low byte holds the loop index `i` (0-9) — but `R9`'s *high* byte is never written anywhere else in this file (confirmed by grepping for `phi r9` across the whole file: zero matches), so `ADD16`, which uses `R9` as a full 16-bit value, would compute a wild address using whatever garbage happened to be sitting in `R9`'s high byte. Neither simulation caught this because both idealized `R9` as starting at `[0,0]`, which is exactly the assumption real hardware doesn't guarantee. A follow-up mechanical simulation modeling `R9.hi` as genuinely arbitrary garbage confirmed the severity directly: an unfixed run with `R9=[0xAB,5]` computes array index `43781` against a 10-byte array. Fixed with one `ldi 0`/`phi r9` right after the index is first set, confirmed correct afterward across the same garbage-modeling simulation for six different garbage values × hundreds of cases each. **Lesson for next time**: a simulation that hand-initializes every register to a "clean" starting state can hide exactly this class of bug — real hardware doesn't zero-initialize registers, only what your own code explicitly sets is defined, and grepping for "is this register's *other* half ever written anywhere in this file" is a cheap, concrete check worth doing whenever a register that only ever received a small `plo`/`ldi 0-9` value gets fed into a 16-bit `ADD16`/`SUB16`.
  - **Old `spaces5` (unused after the widen) removed entirely** rather than left as dead code, matching this project's own convention. `size_buf` (the old 6-byte scratch) is kept, now used only for `wr_year`'s own digit printing (an unrelated, still-16-bit case) — the new size column has its own `size_buf13`/`spaces13`/`raw_digits`/`mod3_table`. Header comment's column-position table updated throughout (the widened size column shifts every subsequent column's start position by 8 relative to the old 5-column width).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, no duplicate labels — all clean on the fixed version. Full clean `make progs` (31/31, kernel untouched — pure `progs/` change). **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: a small file's size still displays correctly and right-justified (regression check against the old behavior); a file over 64K (once the kernel side is also hardware-confirmed) displays with correct comma grouping; a directory entry's blank size field still lines up correctly against the wider column; and ideally a size landing exactly on a 3-digit boundary (e.g. 1000, "1,000") to exercise the comma-insertion rule's own boundary case.

- **`STAT`'s size field widened to match `DIR`'s new 32-bit comma-grouped format, and the conversion itself factored out of `dir.asm` into a new shared `lib/fmt32.asm` — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland, zero kernel changes.** Direct follow-on to `DIR`'s own widening, at the user's own suggestion ("Might the 32-bit number formatting routine be a good addition to a library? Then dir, stat, and user programs could all use it."). `lib/fmt32.asm` follows this project's established `lib/` convention (no EDF header, no `org PROG_BASE`, assembled separately and linked in via an explicit Makefile override rule, same as `lib/heap_bump.asm`/`lib/env.asm`/`lib/move.asm`) and exposes one public entry point, `fmt_size32(RD:R8=32-bit value, RF=destination buffer >=14 bytes) -> buffer filled and null-terminated`. It's a pure buffer-filling conversion — no printing, no right-justification — matching how `f_uintout` itself already works, so each caller decides its own display formatting on top. `_div32_by10` (the restoring-division routine) and the digit-extraction/comma-insertion logic are carried over byte-for-byte from `dir.asm`'s own original, already-independently-verified implementation (see the entry above), including the `R9.hi`-never-initialized fix already found and fixed there. `progs/dir.asm`'s own local `print_size32`/`_div32_by10`/`raw_digits`/`mod3_table` (roughly 190 lines) were deleted entirely in favor of `extrn fmt_size32` + a `mov rf, size_buf13 / call fmt_size32` call; the old, now-fully-unused `spaces5` data declaration was also removed (confirmed unused via grep before deleting). `progs/stat.asm`'s own "Size:" section (previously `f_uintout` on just the low 16 bits of `DIRENT_SIZE`) now reads the full 4-byte `DIRENT_SIZE` into `RD:R8` and calls `fmt_size32` into a new 14-byte `size_buf14`; its existing `num_buf` (6 bytes) is kept, now used only for `Cluster:` and the write-year digits — both genuinely-16-bit values that don't need the wider conversion.
  - **A real, severe bug caught during the refactor itself, before ever reaching hardware**: the first draft of `fmt_size32` stashed the caller's destination-buffer pointer in `R7` (`mov r7, rf`) at entry, to free `RF` as scratch for the digit-extraction loop — but that loop calls `_div32_by10` repeatedly, and `_div32_by10`'s own header explicitly documents `R7` as one of its clobbered registers (its 32-iteration loop counter, always ending at exactly 0 when the call returns). By the time the routine tried to retrieve the buffer pointer from `R7` after the extraction loop finished, it no longer held the caller's real address — it held `0`. This bug **could not have existed** in the code's original, single-caller form inside `dir.asm`, since that version wrote directly into a hardcoded `size_buf13` and never needed to carry a caller-supplied pointer across the division call at all; it was introduced specifically by generalizing the routine into a real library with a caller-parameterized destination. Caught by a deliberate, skeptical re-check — for every register this routine holds a value in, cross-checking it against `_div32_by10`'s own documented "Modifies: R7, R9" list — exactly the discipline this project's own gotcha #10 already established (a callee's documented clobber list is exactly what a distant caller is most likely to wrongly assume survives), self-inflicted here while doing the refactor. Fixed by switching the stash/retrieve to `RB` throughout (confirmed via grep to be untouched by both `fmt_size32`'s own logic and `_div32_by10`'s body).
  - **Re-verified with a stronger method than a second read**: a call-boundary-aware mechanical 1802 simulator (extending the same `Sim` class built for the earlier file-I/O and `DIR`-widening work) that models `call _div32_by10` as a real subroutine invocation — clobbering `R7`/`R9`/`RD`/`R8` exactly as documented, leaving everything else untouched — run against the *actual* corrected instruction sequence (stash-in-RB, extract loop, retrieve-from-RB, digit-reassembly loop) across 1000+ random 32-bit values, each also run three times with deliberately different garbage seeded into `R7`/`R9` beforehand (to prove the fix doesn't depend on those registers happening to start clean) — all 3027 cases matched Python's own `f"{v:,}"` formatting exactly, including an explicit assertion that no array index generated during the run ever fell outside `raw_digits`'/`mod3_table`'s real 0-9 bounds.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble on the corrected file (94 lines, 168 bytes, 0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, no duplicate labels, proc/endp balanced (3/3) — all clean; gotcha #18 doesn't apply (the file contains no `str r2` staging at all, pure register-to-register arithmetic throughout). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout; `bin/dir`/`bin/stat` both relinked cleanly against the corrected `lib/fmt32.prg`, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` binaries present). Kernel confirmed byte-unchanged (`Highest address` still `$42fa`, `krnboot.bin` untouched) — this whole feature has zero kernel-resident cost, exactly as expected for a pure `progs/`+`lib/` change. New Makefile rule: `lib/fmt32.prg: lib/fmt32.asm include/opcodes.def`, plus explicit override rules `bin/dir: progs/dir.prg lib/fmt32.prg` / `bin/stat: progs/stat.prg lib/fmt32.prg` (the same "explicit target-specific rule beats the generic pattern rule" mechanism `bin/xcopy`'s own rule already established for `lib/heap_bump.prg`). **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: `STAT` on a small file displays the same size it always has (regression check); `STAT` on a large (or, once >64K support is hardware-confirmed, >64K) file displays with correct comma grouping matching `DIR`'s own display for the same file; `STAT` on a directory (no size line at all) is unaffected; and ideally a size landing exactly on a 3-digit boundary to exercise the shared comma-insertion rule's own boundary case through this second call site.

- **`LS -l`'s size column widened to Linux-`ls -h`-style human-readable output (K/M/G suffixes), closing out this session's 32-bit-size work — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland (`progs/ls.asm`), zero kernel changes.** User's own request, with one design decision confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` before implementing: `-l` shows human-readable sizes **always**, not behind a separate opt-in `-h` flag (real Linux `ls` requires `-lh`; the user picked the simpler, no-new-flag option, consistent with this project's own established willingness to deviate from POSIX/GNU flag conventions when it simplifies things). `LSENT_SIZE` (the per-entry struct field `ls_add_entry`/`ls_print_long` share) widened from a 2-byte low-word-only field to a full 4 bytes, matching `DIRENT_SIZE`'s own on-disk width and this session's kernel-side >64K work — every later struct field (`LSENT_NAMEPTR`/`NAMELEN`/`QUOTE`/`LEN`) shifted `+2` as a result (`ls_entries`' own static allocation grew from 2805 to 3315 bytes, ordinary program RAM, irrelevant to the kernel's own tight margin).
  - **Format spec** (Linux `ls -h` style, not attempting byte-for-byte GNU compatibility): a byte count under 1024 prints as a plain decimal integer with no suffix (up to 4 digits, e.g. `1023`); at or above 1024, picks the **smallest** unit (K/M/G) whose whole part comes out under 1000 — deliberately not "the largest unit whose whole part is ≥1", which can leave a 4-digit whole part (e.g. `1023K`) — guaranteeing the display is never more than 4 characters wide (3 digits + suffix, or `D.D` + suffix). A decimal digit is shown only when the whole part is a single digit (0-9); a 2-3 digit whole part shows no decimal. **Two deliberate, documented approximations, both quantified in Python before any assembly was written**: (1) floor, not round — a value just below a unit boundary (e.g. 1047552 bytes, exactly 1023K) displays as `0.9M` rather than `1023K` or a rounded `1.0M`; (2) the decimal digit itself is computed from a *reduced-precision* remainder (see below) rather than the mathematically exact value, differing from the exact digit by at most 1, in 0.394% of decimal-digit cases (measured across 500,000 random values) — accepted as a reasonable tradeoff for a single cosmetic digit rather than adding the extra rounding/overflow-avoidance complexity a bit-exact result would need.
  - **Why the decimal digit needs a reduced-precision remainder at all**: computing it exactly as `floor(10*remainder/scale)` needs a `remainder*10` intermediate that overflows 32 bits for the G unit (`remainder` can be up to `2^30-1`, `×10` exceeds `2^32`). Solved by first reducing `remainder` to a common `<1024` range via a new `ls_pairshr10` helper (shift the 32-bit pair right by 10 bits — 0 calls for K's own remainder, 1 call for M's, 2 calls for G's, since each unit's own shift amount is a multiple of 10 more than K's), after which the decimal digit is just `(reduced*8 + reduced*2) >> 10` — pure 16-bit arithmetic (`reduced<1024`, so the product tops out at 10230), no overflow risk at all. `ls_pairshr10` itself is 10 repeated `SHR16 rd`/`SHRC16 r8` pairs (`SHRC16`'s own defining property — continuing a shift using the *existing* `DF` as carry-in rather than starting fresh — is exactly what chains a single-register shift into a true 32-bit pair shift) — no byte-reassignment shortcut needed for a count this small, matching this file's/`DIR`'s/`STAT`'s own established convention of just repeating a shift macro inline for small fixed counts rather than reaching for a cleverer technique. `M`'s and `G`'s own **whole-part** computations (not remainder) get an even cheaper shortcut: `N>>20` is *exactly* `(N's high word alone) >> 4`, and `N>>30` is exactly `(N's high word alone) >> 14` — proven algebraically (N's low word, capped at 65535, can never accumulate a full unit at either shift depth) before relying on it, then confirmed by the same value-level simulation.
  - **Two real bugs caught by the design's own multi-layer verification, before any of this reached real hardware (or even a first assemble)**: (1) a Python **value-level** simulation (2,000,000+ cases, matching the algorithm as designed) passed cleanly first — but a from-scratch **instruction-level** mechanical 1802 simulator (modeling `GLO`/`GHI`/`PLO`/`PHI`/`SHR16`/`SHRC16`/`SHL16`/`ADI`/`SMI` with real `D`/`DF` semantics, executing the literal planned instruction sequence rather than a re-derived model of intent) caught **two** real bugs the value-level check couldn't see: **checking `ghi rd` alone to test "is the 32-bit value's high word zero"**, in *two* separate places (the plain-byte `N<1024` gate, and the K-vs-M unit-selection gate) — `RD=16` (for example) has a zero *high byte* even though the 16-bit register itself is not zero, so `ghi rd` alone silently mis-classified any `N` whose high word happened to be 1-255 (e.g. `N=1048576`, `RD=16`) as if the high word were fully zero, then evaluated only the low word as though it were the whole value. Fixed both sites to check the *full* 16-bit `RD` (`ghi rd` **and** `glo rd`) before proceeding — the exact same "check the whole register, not just one byte" lesson this project has hit before, caught here specifically because the simulator modeled the literal `ghi`/`lbnz` sequence rather than the intended "is RD zero" logic. (2) `M`'s own remainder-reduction masked the **wrong byte/nibble entirely** in the first draft — `remainder_M`'s extra 4 bits beyond its own low word live in the *low nibble of N's high word's low byte*, but the first draft masked N's high word's *high* byte instead and left the low byte fully unmasked (backwards) — caught by the same instruction-level simulator (300 mismatches, all showing the wrong unit selected for large `N`) before being re-derived correctly and re-verified clean. **A third, separate layer of verification, beyond what this project's own established practice has previously done for arithmetic like this**: after fixing both bugs and reaching 0 mismatches across 155,047 simulated test cases (including exhaustive coverage right around every unit boundary ±5), the real, linked `bin/ls` binary's own bytes (all 646 bytes spanning `ls_pairshr10` through most of `lfh_print_plain`) were decoded directly from the built binary and diffed byte-for-byte against the assembler's own `.lst` output — zero mismatches, confirming (since `progs/ls.asm` uses no `proc`/`endp` blocks, so `-r` relaxation never touches its own internal layout — only `lib/heap_bump.asm`/`lib/env.asm`'s own linked-in code was relaxed) that the simulated instruction sequence is exactly what real hardware would execute, not just what was intended.
  - **`ls_add_entry`'s existing 4-byte `DIRENT_SIZE` copy simply extended** (was 2 bytes with an `add16 rd, 2` skip-to-low-word, now a plain 4-byte copy with the skip removed) — the struct-populating code after it (`LSENT_NAMEPTR` onward) needed no changes beyond the equ-constant offset shifts, since it already worked purely off the write cursor's own advancing position, not a hardcoded offset.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, gotcha #18 sweep (extended to also check for a register-register `add16`/`sub16` between a `str r2` staging and any of `sm`/`smb`/`xor`/`or`/`and`/`add`/`adc`/`sd`/`sdb`), no duplicate labels — all clean on the corrected file. Full manual register-liveness re-trace of the entire new code (beyond the sweeps, which only catch the cheap adjacent-instruction form of a clobber bug) — confirmed every `mov`/`add16`-then-consumer pair is safe, and that `ls_long_size`'s own read of the widened `LSENT_SIZE` field reloads the entry struct address fresh from `ls_curentry` (memory) rather than trusting a register to survive the intervening `K_INMSG` call in the `"d  "`-vs-`"-  "` type-indicator branch just above it (only `R9` is confirmed to survive `K_MSG`/`K_INMSG` in this codebase, gotcha #8 — this file's own established discipline elsewhere already reloads `ls_curentry` for exactly this reason). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout, kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$42fa`, `krnboot.bin` untouched, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` binaries present) — this feature has zero kernel-resident cost. `ls_sizebuf` (already `ds 6`) needed no resize — the new format's max content (`"999K"`/`"9.9K"`/`"1023"`, 4 chars) is smaller than the old plain-decimal max (`"65535"`, 5 chars). **CONFIRMED WRONG ON HARDWARE (2026-07-26)** — see the entry directly below, which replaces the flooring approach entirely.

- **`LS -l`'s human-readable sizes fixed to ROUND (not floor) — IMPLEMENTED, VERIFIED, AND CONFIRMED HARDWARE-CORRECT AGAINST THE USER'S OWN TWO REPORTED VALUES 2026-07-26.** The floor-based version above shipped as build-verified, but the user tested it on real hardware against values independently verified with their own host `ls` and found it *wrong*, not just imprecise: 290421 bytes showed `283K` where real `ls -h` shows `284K`; 1462695 bytes showed `1.3M` where real `ls -h` shows `1.4M`. Both are exactly what floor-vs-round predicts (290421/1024 = 283.61…, 1462695/1048576 = 1.3948…) — genuinely closer to 284/1.4 than 283/1.3, not a rounding-direction judgment call. Rewrote `progs/ls.asm`'s `ls_fmt_human` to round to nearest using **exact** 32-bit arithmetic throughout — deliberately not another reduced-precision shortcut, since the whole point of the fix is to stop approximating. Four new small subroutines (`ls_add32`/`ls_sub32`/`ls_cmp32_ge`/`ls_dbl32`, genuine 32-bit register-pair add/subtract/compare/double, RD:R8 convention) replace the old `ls_pairshr10`-based remainder reduction; a new `ls_human_decdigit` computes `round(10*remainder/scale)` via 10 repeated add+compare+subtract iterations (exact, no precision loss, and no overflow either — the accumulator stays bounded below `2*scale <= 2^31` throughout, so the earlier fear of a `remainder*10` overflow for the G unit never actually arises with this technique) plus a final round-to-nearest check against the loop's own leftover; a new `ls_human_whole_round_check` handles the simpler yes/no rounding decision for a 10-999 whole part. A new shared tail, `lfh_round_and_print`, replaces the old `lfh_have_reduced`/`lfh_print_plain` — and, new in this version, can signal "this unit's whole part rounded up to exactly 1000, retry at the next larger unit" back to K's/M's own call sites (a case flooring could never hit, since flooring never crosses a threshold rounding can).
  - **Design verified in three layers before any assembly was trusted, matching this project's own established discipline for exactly this class of arithmetic**: (1) a Python reference implementation using arbitrary-precision integers (no approximation anywhere) confirmed against the user's own two reported values plus exhaustive coverage around every unit/digit boundary; (2) a from-scratch instruction-level mechanical 1802 simulator (not a re-derived algorithm model — the literal planned instruction sequence) run across 66,000+ cases against the reference; (3) a final fresh read-through of the actual assembled file, cross-checked instruction-by-instruction against the verified simulator model.
  - **Layer 2 (the mechanical simulator) caught two real bugs before either reached real assembly**: (a) the first draft of `ls_cmp32_ge` (the 32-bit "is A >= B" compare) only compared 2 of the 4 relevant bytes — it staged and compared `R8` against `RA`'s two bytes correctly, then *reused* `RA` a second time for what should have been a comparison against `R9` (the high word), silently never referencing `R9` at all. Fixed to a proper 4-byte SM/SMB chain, LSB to MSB, using both `RA` and `R9`. (b) rounding a decimal digit all the way up to 10 does **not** always mean "drop the decimal point": e.g. `whole=1, dec=10` carries to `whole=2, dec=0`, which must still print `2.0K`, not `2K` — only an *original* whole of exactly 9 crosses all the way to a bare `10K`. The first draft assumed "dec==10 implies no decimal" unconditionally; fixed by re-checking `whole<10` *after* applying the carry, exactly matching the reference implementation's own two-step logic, rather than inferring the display mode from the carry alone.
  - **A third, independent bug — not caught by either simulation, only by a subsequent manual hand-trace of the actual byte-mapping** — surfaced while writing the real assembly for G's own remainder computation: unlike K's and M's remainders (both genuinely narrower than 32 bits, needing a real leading zero byte in their 4-byte representation), G's remainder (`N & 0x3FFFFFFF`) is a full 30-bit value whose 4-byte representation needs **no** leading zero byte at all — masking `N`'s own byte0 to its low 6 bits already zeroes the excluded top 2 bits *in place*. The first draft copied K's/M's own "leading zero byte" shape by pattern-matching instead of re-deriving the byte mapping for G's own, different case — inserting a spurious leading zero, shifting every real byte one position to the right, and silently dropping `N`'s own LSB (byte3) entirely. Caught by hand-verifying the byte mapping in Python against 20,000 random 32-bit values before trusting the fix (`remainder_G = (N.byte0 & 0x3F, N.byte1, N.byte2, N.byte3)`, exactly 4 bytes) — a instance of this project's own recurring lesson that pattern-matching a fix from a *similar* but not *identical* neighboring case is exactly the kind of shortcut that hides a real bug (see gotcha #17's own note about a "misdiagnosis" from generalizing too far, or the `_himem_reserve` redesign's own repeated warnings against assumed-similar cases).
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep (5 flagged hits, all confirmed by hand to be intentional D-reuse writing the same byte value into two consecutive positions — e.g. two zero bytes of a scale constant — not bugs), gotcha #18 sweep, no duplicate labels — all clean. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$42fa`, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` present) — zero kernel-resident cost, pure userland change. **CONFIRMED CORRECT ON HARDWARE (2026-07-26)** against the user's own two originally-reported values (290421→`284K`, 1462695→`1.4M`, both matching their host `ls -h` exactly) — the specific regression this fix exists for. Still worth a broader regression round covering values across all three units, near-boundary cases (to confirm the new "bump to next unit" path triggers correctly, e.g. a value whose K-unit whole rounds up to exactly 1000), and directory entries (unaffected by any of this).

- **`DIR`'s column order changed to date/time-first, matching later MS-DOS/Windows `dir` output (post-LFN) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-26, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** User's own request, after testing the >64K work and finding the *old* size-first layout (this project's own original design, predating any real-`dir`-parity goal) "a bit ragged with various right-justified numbers at the beginning of the line." New order: `MM/DD/YYYY HH:MM` (unchanged format) → the dir-tag-or-size field → name. Pure reorder of `progs/dir.asm`'s `print_dir_entry` — none of the three existing blocks' own internal formatting logic changed, just which one runs first.
  - **One real design refinement made during implementation, not just a mechanical reorder**: the old layout's size-vs-`<DIR>`-tag fields were two *differently-sized* columns (13-column right-justified size for files, 7-column `" <DIR> "` tag for directories) sitting *before* a shared name column, so names never had to align between the two entry types. Now that this field is the *last* thing before the name, a naive reorder would have left names visibly staggered — a 7-char tag followed immediately by a directory's name, but a much-longer 13-char size field before a file's name. Fixed to match real Windows `dir`'s own convention instead: `<DIR>` is right-justified within the *same* 13-column field the byte count uses (a fixed 6 leading spaces, since `dir_tag` is always exactly 7 characters — no dynamic character-counting needed the way the size field's own variable-width count already requires), so every entry's name starts at the same column regardless of type. The now-fully-unused `tag_blank` field (the old "pad the size field out for files, since a directory's shorter tag used to need matching" scratch, no longer needed once the size/tag field became the *last* thing before the name rather than a *middle* column) was removed as dead code rather than left behind.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, no duplicate labels — all clean. Full manual re-trace of the restructured routine against a fresh read of the file (confirmed the `wr_year` digit-printing code's own comment, which used to correctly say "this entry's size has already been printed" when size came first, was updated rather than left stale and misleading now that size comes *after* — a real risk of a pure reorder task specifically, where the *code* still works but a comment describing execution *order* silently becomes wrong). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$42fa`, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` present) — zero kernel-resident cost, pure userland change. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: a mixed directory listing shows date/time first for every entry, `<DIR>` and byte-count sizes both right-justify to the same column (names visibly align), and a regression check that `-a`/`-F`/multi-path/single-file-argument output are all unaffected (none of their own logic changed, only `print_dir_entry`'s internal ordering).

- **A real, hardware-reported `COPY` bug under investigation: `copy C:/cfg/*.* .` from a subdirectory on a *different* drive (F:) fails with `"Destination must be an existing directory for multiple sources."` even though `.` genuinely exists — TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC ADDED 2026-07-26, root cause not yet confirmed.** User's own question, worth recording verbatim since it directly shaped the investigation: "Do you think it had trouble with '.' as the destination. Could it have been because the sources and the destination were on different drives?"
  - **Extensive static analysis found no definitive bug, despite the cross-drive hypothesis being plausible on its face**: `kernel/path.asm`'s `path_resolve` unconditionally calls `_switch_drive(presolve_drive)` — for a no-prefix path like `.`, `presolve_drive = cur_drive` — *before* any directory lookup, regardless of what drive glob expansion's own earlier resolution of the wildcard *source* pattern (`C:/cfg/*.*`, which does have a drive prefix and activates C: via the exact same mechanism) left active. This should self-correct on every single call, by design — confirmed by reading `_switch_drive` itself (`kernel/fat.asm`): it fully copies the target drive's own 23-byte BPB block and force-invalidates the FAT cache (`fat_csec = $FFFF`) on every real switch, no partial/stale-state path found. `progs/shell.asm`'s own glob-expansion code (`ge_check_wildcard`/`ge_copy_literal`) correctly identifies `.` as containing no wildcard character and copies it through as a *pointer* into `LINE_BUF` unchanged — never touched by anything drive-related. `kernel_setcurdir`/`kernel_getcurdir` (`kernel/kernel.asm`) correctly index `drive_cur_dir[]` by the real, specific drive rather than a single global. `progs/copy.asm`'s own `-y`-flag argv filter (added earlier the same session) was checked and confirmed not to disturb `dst_ptr` when `-y` isn't present. No single-byte or single-instruction bug was found anywhere in this chain despite a thorough read of every routine involved.
  - **Diagnostic added to `check_dst_is_dir`** (`progs/copy.asm`) rather than continuing to guess, matching this project's own standing practice for exactly this situation (no local emulator, a real hardware-reported bug, code reading alone insufficient to confirm root cause): stashes `K_PATH_RESOLVE`'s own `DF`/`RD` (parent cluster)/`RF` (final component) to memory *before* any print can clobber them, prints `"PR:df=<0/1> clust=<N> final=<text or <empty>>"`, restores from the stash, and continues with the **original, unmodified logic** exactly as before (the diagnostic is purely observational, never changes what runs) — then, if the scan proceeds to `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ`, prints `"SCAN:found attr=<N>"` or `"SCAN:eof (not found)"` at the point the original code would have branched on the outcome anyway. This pins down exactly one of four possible failure points: `K_PATH_RESOLVE` itself failing (`PR:df=1`, contradicting the static analysis above); a wrong or empty final component (`PR:df=0 final=<empty>`, also unexpected for a bare `.`); the directory scan never finding a `.` entry at all (`SCAN:eof`, meaning the wrong drive/cluster is being scanned); or finding `.` but with the wrong attribute byte (`SCAN:found attr=<N>` with `ATTR_DIR` unset, essentially unthinkable for a real `.` entry). Prints straight to the console via `K_INMSG`/`K_MSG`/`f_uintout` (not through anything redirect-aware), matching this project's own diagnostic convention.
  - **A real bug was found and fixed in the diagnostic code itself, before it ever reached hardware**: this project's own D-clobber sweep caught a classic gotcha #4 instance self-inflicted while writing the attribute-byte diagnostic — `ldn rf` (D = attribute byte) followed immediately by `mov rb, cdd_diag_attr` (which clobbers D) followed by `str rb`, storing `cdd_diag_attr`'s own address low byte instead of the real attribute byte. Fixed by reordering the `mov` before the `ldn`.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep, self-overwrite sweep, no duplicate labels — all clean on the corrected diagnostic. Full manual re-trace confirming the diagnostic never changes real control flow (every stashed value is faithfully restored before the original branch logic runs) and never trusts a register across a `K_INMSG`/`K_MSG`/`f_uintout` call (only `R9` is confirmed safe across those in this codebase, gotcha #8 — everything the diagnostic needs afterward is reloaded fresh from memory). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, kernel untouched, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` present). **Needs a hardware round**: re-run `copy C:/cfg/*.* .` from a subdirectory on a non-active-at-glob-time drive with this diagnostic build and report exactly what prints, to pin down which of the four failure points above is the real one. Remove the diagnostic once root-caused (`grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" progs/copy.asm` before calling it done).

- **⚠️ ACTIVE, SERIOUS BUG HUNT (2026-07-26): the >64K kernel file-I/O work has a real data-corruption bug — a file's cluster chain silently stops growing once its size crosses 65536 bytes (2^16) via repeated append writes, while `file_write` itself keeps reporting success. TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC ADDED to `kernel/file.asm`, root cause NOT YET FOUND. This is the highest-severity bug found on this project to date — treat with the same caution as a boot-code change until resolved.** User-reported, with a full console transcript: copied a file to ~62KB, then ran `ATEST` (a test utility that opens in append mode and writes 200×11-byte lines = 2200 bytes per run) repeatedly. Two runs succeeded normally (62125→64325→66525 bytes, crossing 65536 *during* the second run). The **third** run failed with `"Write error."`, as did a fourth attempt — then the **next shell prompt** failed with `"Shell not found or invalid."`, meaning something on disk got corrupted badly enough to break the kernel's own cached shell-lookup fast path (`prog_run_shell`) *and* its full-path fallback. `fsck.fat` afterward reported: `"File size is 66525 bytes, cluster chain length is 65536 bytes. Truncating file to 65536 bytes."` — the *directory entry's* size field is correct (32-bit tracking from the earlier >64K work is working), but the actual FAT cluster chain backing the file is short by exactly one cluster's worth, cut off precisely at the 2^16 boundary. The partition itself is nowhere near full (`63/36842 clusters` in use), ruling out a genuine "disk full" explanation for the later `"Write error."`s.
  - **Extensive static review found no definitive bug, despite the exact 2^16 coincidence strongly suggesting one exists somewhere.** Confirmed clean: (1) the FCB struct layout in `kernel.inc` — `FCB_FSIZE`(4 bytes, offset 8)/`FCB_FPOS`(4 bytes, offset 12) were correctly widened with every *subsequent* field (`FCB_ELBA`/`FCB_EOFF`/`FCB_IOBUF`/`FCB_DRIVE`) re-offset in lockstep, no overlap; (2) `file_write`'s own sector/cluster-boundary-crossing logic (`FCB_BOFF`/`FCB_CSECT` comparisons against 512/`bpb_spc`) — both fields are inherently small, per-cluster-relative values with no dependency on the file's overall byte offset, and the chunk-clamping logic (`chunk = min(remaining, sector_remaining)`) provably prevents `FCB_BOFF` from ever *skipping past* 512 without landing on it exactly; (3) the FPOS-vs-FSIZE growth check (the piece this session's own >64K widening touched most directly) — a proper 4-byte backward SM/SMB borrow chain with the corrected subtrahend-staged-first polarity; (4) `fat_get`/`fat_set`/`fat_alloc` (`kernel/fat.asm`) — none of these were touched by the >64K work at all (cluster numbers are inherently 16-bit under FAT16, not something needing "widening"), and their own scan/claim/link logic reads cleanly. The crossing itself happens mid-write during the *second* `ATEST` run (which starts at 64325, comfortably below the boundary, and only crosses it partway through its own 200-write loop) — ruling out `fopen_check_append`'s one-time positioning computation (already exhaustively verified earlier this session) as the site of the actual failure, and pointing squarely at `file_write`'s own repeated cluster-crossing logic instead, even though that logic reads correctly on paper.
  - **Diagnostic added to `kernel/file.asm`'s `file_write`, three observation points, all purely printing via raw BIOS calls (`f_inmsg`/`f_msg`/`f_uintout` — kernel code, not `K_INMSG`/`K_MSG`, matching this project's own established kernel-diagnostic convention) and changing no real logic**: (1) every time a sector boundary is crossed, prints the full 32-bit `FCB_FPOS`, `FCB_CCLUST`, and the just-incremented `FCB_CSECT` — lets us see exactly what these values are doing as the file's position crosses 65536; (2) prints `fat_get`'s own raw return value every time it's consulted at a cluster boundary (a real next-cluster number, or the end-of-chain marker $FFF8+) — reveals which of the two branches (extend an existing chain vs. allocate) is actually being taken at the critical moment; (3) prints whatever `fat_alloc` hands back whenever a new cluster genuinely gets allocated. Every stashed value uses the corrected "dest pointer via `mov` set up *before* any `lda`/`ldn` that supplies the real byte" ordering (gotcha #4) — this project's own D-clobber sweep script caught exactly this mistake self-inflicted in the `COPY` diagnostic earlier the same day (see the entry directly above), so it was checked with extra care here from the start, and the sweep confirms 0 hits in the new code.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep (0 hits), self-overwrite sweep (29 hits, all confirmed pre-existing — outside the new diagnostic's own line range entirely, an intentional D-reuse pattern already established elsewhere in this file), no duplicate labels. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, 31/8 `bin`/`test/bin` present). `krnboot.bin` unchanged (1536 bytes) — this diagnostic doesn't touch boot code or the jump table at all (no new `K_*` slots). **Kernel margin is genuinely tight with this diagnostic build in place**: `Highest address` `$44f9` (up from `$42fa`), only **263 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`, 256 bytes boot headroom** — still positive/boot-safe, but this is a temporary cost from the diagnostic's own print calls, expected to mostly reverse once removed.
  - **Needs a hardware round, on a disposable test file/directory (not important data)**: reproduce the original scenario — copy or create a file in the 55-63KB range, then repeatedly run `ATEST <file>` until its size crosses 65536 bytes — and report the full console output, including every `FW:fpos=... cclust=... csect=... fatget=...`/`alloc=...` line printed.
