  - **`kernel/batch_mod.asm` tail hoist** (`115220c`): `batch_readline`'s `brl_eof`/`brl_ioerr` carried an identical 7-instruction close/clear/return tail, hoisted with zero new branches needed (`brl_eof`'s own zero-count check already falls straight into what is now the shared label). **Zero effect on the kernel's own `Highest address`/`PROG_BASE` margin** — `kernel/batch_mod.asm` is not part of `KOBJ`/`kernel.bin`, it's the dynamically-loaded batch-script module (Phase 2, loaded into himem-reserved RAM only while a `.bat` script runs); the real benefit is less RAM the module needs reserved while resident. Confirmed safe against the module's own fixed-offset entry-table design (`MOD_START_OFF`/`MOD_READLINE_OFF`/`MOD_GOTO_OFF`, `include/batchmod.inc`) — internal size changes inside `batch_readline`'s own body cannot desync those separately-fixed-up trampoline offsets, confirmed by rebuilding `bin/batch.mod` directly (`Module size` `0380`→`036b`, `Lowest`/`Start address` both still `0000`, matching the unchanged trampoline).
  - **Net result**: kernel `Highest address` `$423e`→`$41f9` (69 bytes), margin before `PROG_BASE` (deliberately left at `$4400` throughout this whole pass, per the coordinator's explicit instruction to keep it as a separate, later variable — see the next entry) up from 450 to 519 bytes. `progs/ls.asm`'s own margin gain is separate and unrelated (ordinary program RAM, not kernel-resident).
  - **Verification, per-commit**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings) at every step; D-clobber sweep (explicitly including `push`/`pop`, since `POP`'s own expansion ends in a plain `LDX` that clobbers `D` — this project's own established gotcha #4 shape, checked here since several of these commits stage a value across a `pop` block)/self-overwrite/gotcha-18/duplicate-label sweeps all clean; proc/endp balanced on every touched file. All 47 `K_*` jump-table slots decoded directly from each build's `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`; `K_FILE_WRITE`'s and (separately) `K_TYPE`/`K_MSG`/`K_INMSG`/`K_READ`/`K_INPUTL`'s own targets specifically cross-checked against their real linked addresses via a matching `-r`-relaxed `link02 -s` dump for each build (not a differently-relaxed throwaway link — this project's own standing lesson that `-r` renumbers everything per-build) — all matched, confirming internal restructuring deep inside a proc never disturbed its own entry point. `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (1536 bytes) across every commit — none of this touches boot code. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` after the final commit: 0 errors/warnings throughout, 39 `bin/`+9 `test/bin/` binaries present.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-08-02)**: flashed and run with `PROG_BASE` still at `$4400` (isolated from the separate `PROG_BASE` move below, per the user's own explicit test-ordering decision) — several ordinary commands plus the existing batch test scripts (exercising `batch_mod.asm`'s own reloaded module, one of the 7 commits) all passed with no issues. Ready to merge to `main`; the `PROG_BASE`→`$4300` move gets its own separate round next, on top of `main`.

- **`PROG_BASE` decoupled from `KERN_LOAD` again (reversing part of the 2026-07-10 "deliberately merged" decision) and moved to `$4300` — CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE 2026-08-02.** Motivated directly by the user's own stated goal: more usable program RAM for 32K-RAM systems specifically, not more kernel growth room — confirmed via a direct question-and-answer exchange before making the change (`"Can't KERN_LOAD stay at $4400 while PROG_BASE moves to $4300?"` → yes, since the two have always been independently-defined constants — `PROG_BASE` an `equ` in `include/kernel.inc`/`kernel_api.inc`, `KERN_LOAD` a `#define` in `boot/mbr.asm` — with no assembler/linker-enforced equality between them, confirmed by reading `kernel/loader.asm`'s own `PROG_BASE` usage, which is already fully symbolic via `high PROG_BASE`/`low PROG_BASE` per gotcha #2's own retracted-bug fix, 2026-07-10).
  - **Why this is safe to decouple, despite the 2026-07-10 entry's own "DELIBERATELY EQUAL... not just numerically close" framing**: that merge's real benefit — reclaiming krnboot's own dead 512-byte sector for program loads, since nothing ever jumps back into it once `KERN_ENTRY` is reached — doesn't require the two addresses to be numerically identical, only that `PROG_BASE` not fall *below* wherever `krnboot`'s own sector logically ends. `KERN_LOAD` staying at `$4400` while `PROG_BASE` drops to `$4300` still fully preserves that reclaim (the 256-byte gap between them is simply extra kernel-resident-code headroom, exactly like any other kernel margin) — confirmed directly rather than assumed, by checking both margins independently after the move (see below).
  - **Two margins, now genuinely separate again and each needing its own check going forward** (the 2026-07-10 merge had collapsed them into "one boundary to check, not two" — that's no longer true): (1) **kernel-resident margin**, `PROG_BASE − kernel.bin's Highest address` — governs whether a loaded program overwrites the still-running kernel; dropped from 519 bytes (at `PROG_BASE=$4400`, post-consolidation-pass) to **263 bytes** at `PROG_BASE=$4300` — the direct, expected cost of the move. (2) **boot-loading margin**, `KERN_LOAD − kernel.bin's sector-rounded on-disk size` — governs whether `krnboot`'s own sector-by-sector load loop overwrites its own currently-executing code before `KERN_ENTRY` — **unchanged at 256 bytes**, confirmed by direct computation (`kernel.bin` = 16634 bytes = 33 sectors = rounds to exactly `$4300`; `KERN_LOAD − $4300 = $100 = 256`), still positive/boot-safe. `boot/mbr.asm`/`boot/krnboot.asm` are **completely untouched** (`git diff main -- boot/` empty) — this move only ever edited the two `PROG_BASE` `equ` declarations (`include/kernel.inc:79`, `include/kernel_api.inc:64`), kept in sync as always.
  - **Net effect for every program**: since `mem_base ≈ PROG_BASE` and `mem_top` (the true top of physical RAM) is unaffected by this move, every program's own usable RAM (`mem_top − PROG_BASE`, published via `LOADER_ARGS`) grows by exactly the 256 bytes `PROG_BASE` moved down by — confirmed directly in the build: every program's own `Lowest address` in its link output now reads `4300` instead of `4400` (checked on `bin/dir` specifically, and true for all 39 `bin/`+9 `test/bin/` binaries by construction, since they all `org PROG_BASE`).
  - **Verification**: kernel `Highest address` confirmed **unchanged** at `$41f9` after the move (proves `PROG_BASE` genuinely isn't kernel-resident code — moving it costs nothing there, only the margin computed against it shrinks). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`: 0 errors/warnings throughout, 39/9 binaries built. `include/kernel.inc`/`include/kernel_api.inc` re-confirmed in lockstep (`$4300` in both). This is this project's Nth `PROG_BASE` move (`$2000`→`$3000`→`$4000`→`$3E00`→merged-with-`KERN_LOAD`→`$4600`→`$4200`→`$4400`→now `$4300`) and follows the exact same "real headroom check, both include files in sync, boot files untouched unless `KERN_LOAD` itself moves" discipline every prior move in this file's own history already established.
  - **Tested in isolation, as planned**: staged as an uncommitted `git stash` until the `redir-consolidation-agent` branch's own 7 commits were hardware-confirmed and merged to `main` first (2026-08-02), then popped and committed on its own (`fc5ac33`) as a single-commit follow-on, rebuilt on top of the merged consolidation work (kernel `Highest address` reconfirmed unchanged at `$41f9`, both margins reconfirmed identical to the numbers above) before this hardware round.
  - **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-08-02)**: boot succeeded cleanly at the new `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD` split (the single highest-stakes thing this move needed to confirm — a wrong margin computation here would have been a silent, zero-console-output boot failure), plus ordinary commands, the batch test scripts, and `test/bin/big64test` all run with no issues — a program actually loading and running correctly at the new `$4300` base, matching the margin math. Ready to merge to `main`.

- **`CORRUPT` (`test/corrupt.asm`) — a deliberate-FAT16-corruption injector, built specifically to test `CHKDSK`'s own check-only mode against real, known corruption scenarios (and cross-check its findings against `fsck.fat`'s own detection/repair of the same scenarios) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-08-02, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** Test/diagnostic tooling, not a real end-user command, so it lives in `test/` (matching this project's own established `test/` vs `progs/` convention) and links against `lib/ymodem.prg`+`lib/fmt32.prg` (reusing `ym_parse_uint32` for its own decimal arguments — `lib/fmt32.prg` is pulled in only because `lib/ymodem.asm` itself needs `_div32_by10`, the exact same two-library pairing `bin/yr`/`bin/ys` already use). **Zero kernel changes** — built entirely on `K_GETCURDIR`/`K_SECREAD`/`K_SECWRITE`/`BPB_DATA_PTR`/`f_strcmp`, plus hand-traced copies of `progs/chkdsk.asm`'s own already-independently-verified BPB-read/cluster-to-LBA/FAT-entry-read/raw-directory-sector-walk routines (reused rather than re-derived, per this project's standing practice).
  - **`CORRUPT <mode> <args...>`, always operating on the current directory (no path support), six modes**: `SIZE <name> <newsize>` (rewrite the directory-entry size field directly, leaving the real cluster chain untouched — a size/chain-length mismatch); `LOST <name>` (mark the directory entry deleted, `$E5`, but leave its FAT chain fully allocated — every cluster becomes lost); `XLINK <nameA> <nameB>` (point `nameB`'s own first cluster at `nameA`'s — a cross-link); `DUPNAME <nameA> <nameB>` (overwrite `nameB`'s own raw 11-byte short name with `nameA`'s — a duplicate directory entry); `TRUNC <name> <hops>` (walk `<name>`'s cluster chain `<hops>` hops from its first cluster, then mark that cluster's own FAT entry end-of-chain — the exact shape of this project's own real >64K bug history and of `fsck.fat`'s own "cluster chain length is N bytes, truncating" report); `BADSECTOR <name> <hops>` (same walk, marks the target cluster `$FFF7` instead). Filenames must already be typed in uppercase, valid 8.3 form — the tool does its own raw 8.3 packing/11-byte compare, deliberately bypassing `K_DIR_READ`'s LFN resolution, matching exactly what `CHKDSK`'s own duplicate-short-name check looks at.
  - **Prominent, repeated warning in the tool's own header and usage text**: reboot before running `CHKDSK` (or `fsck`) afterward — the kernel's own in-RAM FAT-sector cache (`kernel/fat.asm`'s `fat_csec`) is bypassed entirely by this tool's raw `K_SECREAD`/`K_SECWRITE` writes, and a later, unrelated file operation in the same boot session could flush a stale cached copy of the exact sector just corrupted, silently undoing the injection before anything else sees it — the identical caution `CHKDSK`'s own deferred `-f` fix mode already documents needing, for the same reason. Also flagged as dangerous-by-design (direct raw sector I/O, matching `K_SECREAD`/`K_SECWRITE`'s own `kernel_api.inc` warning) — use only on a disposable test volume/directory.
  - **`crp_fat_write` mirrors the write into every FAT copy** (`bpb_num_fats`, one BPB field `chk_read_bpb` never needed to read since `CHKDSK` itself only ever reads the FAT, never writes it), matching `kernel/fat.asm`'s own `fat_flush` convention — writing only one copy would itself create an unrelated FAT-copy mismatch, muddying the specific corruption this tool means to inject.
  - **Five real bugs found and fixed during implementation, all caught before this ever reached hardware** — a genuinely dense bug hunt for a first-draft file this size, none of them caught by a clean assemble alone:
    1. **Gotcha #4** (D-clobber): `crp_read_bpb` never populated `crp_spc` (used only by the subdirectory cluster-chain walk) — caught not by a sweep but by a direct grep cross-check ("is every declared variable actually written somewhere") before the sweeps even ran.
    2. **Gotcha #4** again, in `crp_fat_write`'s copy-index zero-init (`ldi 0` / `mov rf,...` / `str rf` — the `mov` clobbers `D` between the `ldi` and the `str`) — caught by the standard automated D-clobber sweep.
    3. **A genuine byte-swap bug in `crp_fat_write`**: the new FAT-entry value was stashed into memory high-byte-first, then later copied byte-for-byte straight onto disk with no reordering — but on-disk FAT16 entries are little-endian (low byte first), so a value like `$FFF8` would have been written as `$F8FF`. Every OTHER 16-bit value in this file that gets copied to/from disk goes through a real register reconstruction (`lda`/`phi` + `ldn`/`plo`, which naturally preserves LE order since the 1802's own low/high-byte convention already matches it) — this was the one place a value was staged into memory and copied directly instead, and the staging order didn't match. Fixed by storing low-byte-first to match on-disk order directly.
    4. **An unzero-extended register feeding a 16-bit shift** (the same class of bug this project's own `DIR`/`LS` size-formatting work hit earlier): `crp_find`'s own entry-offset computation (`entry_idx * 32` via 5×`shl16`) never explicitly zeroed the working register's high byte before the shift — unlike `crp_get_entry_ptr`, which does this correctly and was used as the reference to catch the mismatch. Since `crp_read_next_dir_sector`'s own root-path writes that same register's high byte from `root_sector_idx` moments earlier (usually, but not provably, zero), this could have silently corrupted the recorded match offset. Fixed with an explicit `ldi 0`/`phi` before the shift.
    5. **An under-sized field discovered by the fix above**: `crp_found_off` was declared as a single byte, but `entry_idx*32` can reach 480 (entry 15 of 16), which doesn't fit in one byte — widened to 2 bytes, with all 7 read sites and the one write site updated to load/store the full 16-bit value (a scripted bulk fix for the 7 identical read sites, hand-verified against the corrected write-side byte order).
    6. **A second, more severe byte-order bug**, found only by hand-deriving a concrete numeric example against `progs/chkdsk.asm`'s own proven `chk_cluster_to_lba` tail: the subdirectory-cluster-chain data-area-base addition (`BPBBLK_DATA_LBA`, a 3-byte big-endian value) was read and added in the SAME sequential (MSB-first) order it's stored in memory — but a correct 3-byte add needs LSB-first addition for carry to propagate correctly, meaning the addend's most-significant byte was being added to the LBA's least-significant byte and vice versa. `chkdsk.asm`'s own version avoids exactly this by staging all 3 bytes into temporary registers first, then adding in the correct order — copied that exact technique into both of this file's own occurrences (root vs. already-in-progress subdirectory-chain-follow paths) rather than re-deriving a fix independently.
  - **Verification**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings) after every fix; D-clobber (including `mov`/`add16`/`sub16`-then-consumer)/self-overwrite (`lda X` immediately followed by `phi X`/`plo X`)/gotcha-18 (`str r2` staging followed by a register-register `add16`/`sub16` before the real consumer)/duplicate-label sweeps all run via script, 0 hits on the final version. The one genuinely new, non-mechanical algorithm in the file (`crp_pack_name`'s 8.3 name-splitting/padding) was hand-traced against four concrete cases (a name with extension, no extension, a name longer than 8 characters, an extension longer than 3) before being trusted — all four produced the exact expected packed output. Two harmless dead instructions (a `glo` whose result was immediately discarded by an unconditional `lbr` right after it, in two of the file's loops) were found and removed during this same pass, for cleanliness, not correctness. A full top-level-label fallthrough scan (checking every block ends in `rtn`/`lbr` before the next label, matching this project's own established "mr.asm/ms.asm missing-branch" lesson) found several intentional, by-design fallthroughs (argc checks proceeding into their own success path, `TRUNC`/`BADSECTOR` sharing a common tail) and zero real ones. Links cleanly against `lib/ymodem.prg`+`lib/fmt32.prg` (`Lowest address: 4300`, matching `PROG_BASE`, confirming no gotcha #20 issue). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`: 0 errors/warnings throughout, kernel `Highest address` confirmed byte-unchanged at `$41f9` (this tool touches zero kernel files), boot files untouched, `test/bin/corrupt` present with the correct `'EDF'` magic.
  - **Not yet hardware-tested** — this is precisely the tool that will provide `CHKDSK`'s own first real hardware exercise: create a handful of test files, run each `CORRUPT` mode against them, reboot (per the tool's own prominent warning), then run `CHKDSK` and `fsck.fat` back to back on the same corrupted volume and compare what each one reports — and, for `fsck.fat`, whether its own repair produces a filesystem `CHKDSK`'s own check-only pass then reports as clean.

## Toolchain gotchas (Asm/02) — check for these by hand when reviewing 1802 code

1. **Every `proc` needs a matching `endp`.** Without it, a new `proc` resets the fixup count and silently drops all relocation entries for the *previous* proc, breaking every cross-proc `call`/`lbr` reference into it.
2. **RETRACTED (2026-07-10), kept for history — do not treat as current guidance.** This originally claimed `mov reg, symbol+CONST` compound expressions silently drop the symbol's address, recommending a plain `mov reg, symbol` plus separate `add16 reg, CONST` instead. Revisited while investigating Asm/02's `symbol+offset` support for the scratch-overlay size-reduction idea: an isolated Asm/02/Link/02 test of `mov rf, real_block+2`/`real_block-2`/`real_block+300` (`real_block` a genuinely relocatable, cross-file `ds`-declared label) computed the correct value in every case, both same-file and cross-file. **Could not reproduce this bug in several attempts** — it may have been a misdiagnosis of a `mov`/`add16`-clobbers-D instance (gotcha #4, which cites overlapping examples) from earlier in the project, or may need a trigger not yet found. Treat `mov reg, symbol+CONST` as probably safe, but don't go rewrite the existing `mov`+`add16` workarounds on the strength of this alone — they're correct either way, just more verbose. **Related, CONFIRMED-working discovery from the same investigation**: Asm/02's `high`/`low` operators (`ldi high SYMBOL`, `ldi low SYMBOL`) correctly extract a symbol's high/low byte at assemble time, including `high (symbol+CONST)` across a byte-boundary-crossing offset, for both absolute `equ` constants (`PROG_BASE`-style) and genuinely relocatable cross-file labels — verified via isolated test before ever using it for real. `kernel/loader.asm`'s two hardcoded `PROG_BASE`-high-byte literals (the exact bug class that bit this project twice, at `$20`→`$40` and `$40`→`$3E`) were replaced with `high PROG_BASE`/`low PROG_BASE` on the strength of this (2026-07-10) — verified **byte-for-byte identical** kernel output before/after the change, so it's a pure fragility fix, not a behavior change. Any future `PROG_BASE` move no longer needs a manual literal hunt in `loader.asm`. **What's still genuinely broken** (not retracted): `equ ALIAS: equ RELOCATABLE_LABEL+CONST` — see gotcha #15.
3. **Comma-separated `extrn a, b, c` on one line only registers the first name.** One `extrn` per line.
4. **`mov` and `add16` both clobber D as a side effect**, unrelated to whatever was in D before them (`mov` leaves D = source's low byte; `add16 reg,CONST` leaves D = the computed address's final high byte). If a value needs to survive a `mov`/`add16`, stash it in a spare register first and reload immediately before the instruction that needs it. This bug has recurred at least five times in this codebase (DIR's attribute/checksum bugs, `file_read`'s FCB-index capture, `hexdump`'s hex-byte-column bug below) — treat "does D need to survive a mov/add16 here?" as a standing review question for any 1802 code in this project.
   - **`progs/hexdump.asm`'s hex-byte-column bug (found on hardware 2026-07-14, user's own diagnosis)**: every byte in the hex columns printed identically (`5b 5b 5b ...`) regardless of the real file content, while the ASCII column printed correctly. Root cause: `ldn rf` loaded the real byte into D, but the very next instruction, `mov rf, hd_digits` (repositioning RF for the hex-digit output buffer), immediately clobbered D — so `hex_byte` always received `hd_digits`' own address low byte (a compile-time constant, `$5B` in the built binary) instead of the real byte. The offset column and the ASCII column, at their own separate call sites, both happened to stash the value (via `plo rc`) *before* any intervening `mov`, so neither showed the bug — only the hex-column call site had the ordering backwards. Fixed by stashing the byte into `RC` immediately after the `ldn`, before the `mov`, then reloading it right before the call — the standard pattern this gotcha already describes.
5. **`f_hexout2`/`f_hexout4` write ASCII hex digits into the buffer at `*RF`** (advancing RF) — they do not print to console. To print, point RF at a scratch buffer, call the routine, null-terminate, then separately call `f_msg`/`K_MSG` on that buffer. `f_uintout` follows the same convention for decimal (and does *not* null-terminate itself — you must add the null after it).
6. Same-file cross-`proc` references (a label defined in one `proc`, used in another, in the *same* source file) still need `extrn` + `public`, exactly as if they were in a different file.
7. On the 1802, `ADD`/`ADC`/`SD`/`SDB`/`SM`/`SMB` operate against `M(R(X))`, the *currently selected* X register — normally `R2` by convention throughout this codebase (no code here calls `SEX`). Don't assume a raw BIOS routine leaves X on R2 after return without checking its source, though in practice the SCRT return path (`sep sret`) resets X=2, so this hasn't bitten us yet.
8. Register-preservation across a call is otherwise *unconfirmed by default* — verified so far: `R9` survives `f_msg`/`f_inmsg`, and (per the user, source-confirmed) `f_idewrite`. Don't assume any other register survives a BIOS or kernel call without checking the routine's actual source or testing it; when in doubt, stash the value in memory rather than a register across calls whose internals you haven't audited.
9. A shared, index-keyed cache (e.g. `file.asm`'s `io_owner`/`io_buf`) needs invalidation on **both** acquire and release if the index can be reassigned to a different logical resource (FCB slots get reused across different files). Invalidate-on-release alone isn't sufficient.
10. **A subroutine's *documented return registers* are exactly the registers most likely to get silently clobbered by a careless caller**, because they don't look like "scratch" — they look like they might hold something useful. `_cluster_to_lba` returns its result in `R7`/`R8`; `file_write` called it from inside a block that still needed `R7` to hold a live "chunk" byte count for code *after* the call, and didn't protect it (only `RA`/`RB`/`RC` were pushed) — `_cluster_to_lba` overwrote `chunk` with part of the LBA it computed, so every subsequent `+= chunk` added garbage and the write loop never advanced (looked exactly like an infinite loop, confirmed via a state-tracing diagnostic showing `RC`/`BOFF` frozen for 250+ iterations). The same class of bug hit `fat_alloc`, which internally calls `fat_set` (clobbering `RB`, `fat_set`'s own "value" argument) to claim the cluster it just found — a caller relying on `RB` across `fat_alloc` needs to protect it too, even though nothing in `fat_alloc`'s own signature suggests it touches `RB`. When calling any subroutine, check its *actual body* (not just the args you're passing) for which registers it uses as scratch or output, and protect anything you need afterward — don't assume a register is safe just because it's not part of the call's "obvious" argument list.
    - **A third instance of this exact lesson, hit for real on hardware (2026-07-15) and structurally invisible until a large-cluster partition existed to expose it**: `kernel/dir.asm`'s `_dir_next_sector` (`dns_in_cluster` branch) computed a subdirectory sector's LBA as `_cluster_to_lba`'s result plus the within-cluster sector offset — reading that offset via `glo rc`, trusting `RC` to still hold the `dir_sect` value stashed into it at the top of the proc. But `_cluster_to_lba`'s own documented `Modifies:` list includes `RC` (its own internal shift-count scratch for the `cluster << spc_shift` multiply) — so by the time it returned, `RC.0` was always `0` (that loop's own terminal value), not the real sector offset. Every sector-within-a-cluster read silently computed the *same* LBA as sector 0 of that cluster, regardless of which sector was actually wanted. Invisible on every card tested before this one because every prior test card used `spc=1` (one sector per cluster), so `dir_sect` was structurally always `0` at this point anyway — a 512MB card's `mkfs.vfat -F 16`-assigned `spc=16` was the first configuration where a directory could need more than one sector within a single cluster, and the first `bin/` directory anywhere in this project to exceed 16 directory-entry slots (9 short/LFN-pair files + `.`/`..`) actually hit it: the scan re-read sector 0 sixteen times running (matching `spc`), never advancing, then hit real end-of-chain and reported not-found — surfacing as the boot-time `K_SHELL_INIT` lookup failing with `"No shell on C:."`, since `/bin/shell` sorts alphabetically past the `spc`-sector boundary. Diagnosed via a hardware-transcript pattern match (repeating groups of exactly 16 slots) plus a `DNS <clust><sect><spc>` diagnostic confirming `dir_sect` genuinely incremented while the *entries read* never changed. Fixed the same way as every other instance of this lesson: re-read the needed value fresh from its own memory location (`dir_sect`) instead of trusting any register to survive an audited-but-ignored clobber list. All 5 *other* call sites of `_cluster_to_lba` in `kernel/file.asm` were checked and already do this correctly (they read their own within-cluster offset — `FCB_CSECT` — fresh from memory, never from a register) — `dir.asm`'s was the only vulnerable one. **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-15)**: the 4×512MB card now boots cleanly, and the user moved between partitions, typed files, and read directories with no issues. Diagnostics removed (grep-verified clean); final margins `Highest address` `$38ec`, 1300 bytes before `PROG_BASE`, 1280 before `KERN_LOAD`.
    **The worst instance of this class found so far (2026-07-10), and the actual root cause of the entire multi-week "phantom directory corruption" saga** (the REN duplicate-entry bug, and later a general `COPY`/any-directory-scan bug — see the `LINE_BUF` corruption note below): `kernel/fat.asm`'s `_fat_load_sector` used `R9` as its own scratch (`mov r9, rd` to hold the cluster number across its internal `fat_flush`/`f_ideread` calls), and `fat_flush` used `R9.0` too (for the cached FAT sector index). Neither routine's own header mentioned `R9` as a caller-visible concern — by every *local* reading, `R9` looked like ordinary internal scratch. But four call levels up, `dir.asm`'s `dir_read` stashes its *own* caller's result-buffer pointer in `R9` (`mov r9, rf` at entry) specifically so it survives `dir_read`'s internal call to `_dir_next_sector` — and `_dir_next_sector`'s cluster-chain-follow path (`dns_subdir`) calls `fat_get`, which calls `_fat_load_sector`. So every time a directory scan crossed a cluster boundary (needing exactly one `fat_get` call to find the next cluster), `R9` silently flipped from "the caller's result buffer" to "the FAT cluster number currently being looked up" — and the *next* directory entry decoded after that point got its attribute/cluster/size/timestamp fields written to `(garbage R9 value) + DIRENT_offset` instead of the real result buffer. On the test hardware this consistently computed to an address inside `LINE_BUF` (`$0080-$00FF`, the shell's own command-line buffer) — silently corrupting whatever command the user was about to type next, entirely unrelated to the directory scan that caused it. Explains every earlier-observed symptom in one shot: only ever the *first* entry after a chain-follow was affected (subsequent entries in the same scan get a fresh, correct `R9` from `dir_read`'s next per-entry setup); single-cluster directories never triggered it (no `fat_get` call needed); and the original REN "duplicate entry" symptom was this same write landing inside a *directory* sector instead of `LINE_BUF` on an earlier, less-lucky test layout — real memory corruption via a wild write, dressed up as a filesystem bug. Found by iterative hardware bisection (about a dozen rounds of progressively narrower `LINE_BUF`-snapshot diagnostics, each one splitting the remaining suspect code in half) after purely static tracing of the "obvious" suspects (`f_strcpy`, `_dir_proc_lfn`, `drd_got_name`'s own field-copy code) turned up nothing — the actual clobber was two call levels below anything directly touched by directory-entry decoding. Fixed by moving both routines' scratch out of registers entirely, into dedicated memory locations (`fls_cluster`, `ffl_sector_idx`) — memory survives an unknown callee's internals unconditionally, so this class of bug structurally can't recur in these two routines regardless of what any future caller happens to be keeping in a register. **Lesson for next time this class of bug is suspected**: when a value only corrupts *sometimes*, and correlates with an otherwise-unremarkable code path being taken (here: "did this call need to follow the FAT chain, or was everything in one cluster") rather than with *what data* is involved, suspect a register clobber several call-levels down, not a logic bug in the code you're directly looking at — and bisect by *call depth*, not just by data flow, once the obvious direct suspects are cleanly ruled out.
11. **When walking a pointer sequentially through a multi-field struct (`str rb; inc rb` repeated per byte), count the increments against the byte count for *every* field, not just the ones being changed.** A 4-byte zero-init block in `file_open`'s FCB population had 4 `str rb` but only 3 `inc rb` — a plain miscount, invisible for a long time because that field used to be the *last* one written (nothing after it relied on `rb`'s exact value). Adding new fields after it later inherited a silent one-byte shift in all of them. This kind of bug hides indefinitely if nothing downstream depends on the exact end position — don't assume an old sequential-write block is correct just because it's never caused a visible problem; recount it by hand when you build something new on top of it.
12. **Don't carry an accumulator through D across loop iterations if the loop's own condition check also reads a register via `glo`/`ghi`.** A shift-by-variable-count loop (`sector_index >> spc_shift`, computing an append-mode cluster offset) loaded the value into D once *before* the loop, then relied on it surviving into `shr` each iteration — but the loop's own top-of-loop condition check (`glo r9`, reading the remaining shift count) clobbers D on *every* pass, including the first, before `shr` ever runs. The loop ended up shifting the decrementing *counter's* own value instead, which — since a counter counted down to zero via the same shifts+decrements always reaches exactly 0 regardless of the real input — silently produced the same wrong answer (0) for every input and every shift count, not just the one case that happened to get tested. Keep the accumulator in a real register (reload it into D fresh, immediately before the instruction that needs it, right after the condition check) instead of assuming D survives from before the loop into the loop body.
13. **After hitting this D-clobber bug repeatedly in one session (2026-07-09: six-plus instances across `file.asm`, `loader.asm`, `dir.asm`), a systematic grep/script sweep is worth running proactively, not just reactively per hardware failure.** Two sweep patterns catch most real instances cheaply: (a) `mov`/`add16`/`sub16`/`shl16`/`shr16`/`shlc16`/`shrc16` immediately followed by `str`/`plo`/`phi`/`ani`/`ori`/`xri`/`adi`/`smi`/`lbz`/`lbnz` with no intervening `ldn`/`lda`/`ldi`/`glo`/`ghi` to refresh D — caught a real bug this way (`dir.asm`'s `dir_open` leaving `dir_lfn_ok` set to a fixed garbage byte, `$49`, instead of 0, on every single directory scan). (b) `mov`/`add16`/etc. immediately preceding a `call` to a routine whose own header documents `D` as an argument — in this codebase that's `file_open`/`file_close`/`file_read`/`file_write`/`file_seek` and their `K_FILE_*` jump-table equivalents (grep each routine's own `; Args:` comment to build the current list — it can grow). **Both sweeps have real limits**: neither catches a clobber separated from its eventual use by a `call`-that-doesn't-clobber-the-value plus a branch plus a label (the `file_open` mode-0 `FCB_FLAGS` bug that broke `COPY` spanned exactly that shape: `ldi` → `mov` (clobbers) → `ldn` (refreshes, but with the WRONG value) → `lbz` → label → `str`) — that class still needs a human tracing actual data flow through the routine, not a line-adjacency script. Don't treat a clean sweep as proof a routine is safe; treat it as having ruled out the cheap, common form of the bug.
14. **`f_tty` (BIOS+4Eh, single-character console output) is dangerous to call in a tight loop for diagnostic dumps — confirmed on hardware to corrupt subsequent shell input, not just cosmetic garbling.** A diagnostic added 2026-07-10 (dumping `LINE_BUF`'s 32 raw bytes via a `lda rf` / `call f_tty` loop, `push rf`/`push rc` protected around each call) caused every command typed *after* the one that triggered it to arrive at the shell with a stray extra character prepended (`dir` read as `8dir`, `copy` read as `8copy`) — a full regression of basic shell input, not a diagnostic-output artifact. Root cause not isolated (reverted immediately rather than root-caused, since a broken shell blocks all further testing) — candidates considered but not confirmed: `f_tty`'s actual register/side-effect contract is unconfirmed (unlike `f_msg`/`f_inmsg`, gotcha #8's confirmed list), and this was likely the first time `f_tty` had ever actually been invoked at runtime in this project (previously only wired as `K_TTY`'s passthrough target, never exercised) — so nothing about it was actually hardware-proven despite being defined in `bios.inc`. Notably, a *different*, smaller `f_tty` loop (11 iterations, no push/pop, printing `_gen_short_name`'s output in `file.asm`) worked correctly across four separate hardware rounds with no corruption — so the failure isn't "call `f_tty` at all," it's specific to *this* loop shape/scale (32 iterations × 2 calls per `file_open`, invoked repeatedly across `prog_load`'s multiple candidate-name attempts) in a way not yet understood. **Until `f_tty`'s contract is actually confirmed (ask the user for its BIOS source, per this project's standing practice for exactly this situation), prefer the already-proven-safe pattern for any diagnostic that needs to print a raw/dynamic byte buffer: build a small null-terminated buffer in memory (manual hex-nibble-to-ASCII or plain byte copy) and print it with one `f_msg` call**, instead of looping `f_tty` per character — this is what the `dir.asm` `_dir_next_sector` cluster-print diagnostic and `progs/copy.asm`'s pointer-address diagnostic both already do, and neither has caused any issue across many hardware rounds.

15. **PARTIALLY RETRACTED, then RE-NARROWED with a NEW real bug found (2026-07-21) — read this whole entry before using `equ` to alias a relocatable label; the short version is "safe for one alias, unsafe the moment that alias name also needs `extrn`."** Originally reported (2026-07-10): `equ label: equ OTHER_LABEL+CONST` silently computes a wrong address when `OTHER_LABEL` is relocatable.
    - **First re-test (same day, 2026-07-21) found this apparently fixed**: an isolated test (single alias, several offset shapes, same-file and cross-file) and a real-kernel test (temporary `eav_alias0/1/N` aliases into `dcr_sect_lba`, read from a dead never-called proc, built through the full `-r`-relaxed pipeline) both resolved correctly, byte-decoded against the real `kernel.bin`. This looked like the same root cause as gotcha #17 (both `equ`'s `processArgs` call and `mov`/`lbr` operands go through the same expression evaluator) fixed as an unlogged side effect of the v1.6 fix. **This conclusion was too broad** — see below.
    - **Real payoff attempt exposed a SECOND, DIFFERENT bug**: applying the same technique for real — merging `fa_*`/`fdel_*`/`dcr_*`/`drm_*`/`ren_*` (5 mutually-exclusive scratch groups in `kernel/file.asm`, ~20 real usage sites across `file_open`/`file_delete`/`dir_create`/`dir_remove`/`file_rename`) into one shared 9-byte block via 15 `equ` aliases — silently collapsed almost every alias to the SAME wrong value (`kernel/file.prg`'s own `=SYMBOL VALUE` fixup lines showed `=dcr_parent 00d1`, `=dcr_new_clust 00d1`, `=dcr_sect_lba 00d1`, all identical, ignoring their `+0`/`+2`/`+4` offsets). Caught by decoding the real `.prg`/`kernel.bin`, not trusted from `-s` alone — its symbol-table dump also showed the collapse, but that alone wouldn't have ruled out a display-only bug, so the raw `.prg` fixup text was checked directly.
    - **Root cause, isolated via bisection** (a from-scratch reproduction using the exact real `kernel/file.asm` content, standalone-assembled, progressively trimmed): **declaring a name via `extrn` earlier in a file, then LATER defining that same name via `equ`, causes the equ's value to collapse to the base symbol's own address, discarding any `+offset`.** Confirmed by removing just the pre-existing `extrn` declarations for the 15 field names (leaving everything else identical) — the exact same `equ` block then resolved every offset correctly. This is a NEW, distinct bug from the first re-test's finding: the first test's single alias name (`eav_alias0` etc.) was a BRAND NEW name, never previously `extrn`'d anywhere, so it never hit this path. But every one of the 15 real scratch-group field names NEEDS its own `extrn` for cross-proc use within the same file (gotcha #6's own required convention) — making this exact shape unavoidable for the real coalescing use case, not an edge case to route around.
    - **Practical rule, current and load-bearing: `equ ALIAS: equ RELOCATABLE_LABEL+CONST` is safe ONLY when `ALIAS` is a name with no pre-existing (or later) `extrn` declaration anywhere in the file. The moment the SAME name that's being `equ`-defined also needs `extrn` (e.g. any field shared across multiple procs in one file, the exact shape the scratch-coalescing win needs), `equ` silently breaks — use `#define` instead.**
    - **`#define ALIAS RELOCATABLE_LABEL+CONST` is the correct mechanism for this case**, confirmed via the same rigor (isolated test, then a full real-kernel build with all 15 fields, byte-decoded at 36 real usage sites across all 5 consuming routines — see the `_shared_scratch` bullet under the kernel-history log for the complete verification). It sidesteps the bug structurally, not by luck: `#define` is pure preprocessor text substitution with no symbol-table entry of its own, so there's nothing for a later `extrn` to collide with — every use site gets the literal text `BASE+N` substituted before the assembler's parser ever sees the old field name, then evaluated by the same expression path gotcha #17 already proved correct. One real constraint from being a textual macro: **the `#define` must appear before the first use of each name anywhere in the file** (unlike `extrn`/`public`, which apply file-wide regardless of position) — for a field used starting deep in the file's first consuming proc, this typically means declaring the `#define` block (and the shared storage's own tiny proc) near the top of the file, not co-located with the rest of that data area.
    - **Lesson for next time an "isolated test now passes" conclusion is drawn**: a narrower test (one alias, one name, never previously referenced elsewhere) does not generalize to a structurally different shape (many aliases, names that collide with pre-existing `extrn` declarations) — the real payoff use case needs to be tested in its ACTUAL shape, not a simplified stand-in, before trusting a "this is fixed now" conclusion. Exactly the case for decoding real bytes of the real construct, not an adjacent easier proxy.

16. **`.link .align page` (the mechanism used to page-align a proc for a hand-written short branch — see `PROG_BASE`/mr.asm/ms.asm precedent) only works immediately before a `proc` statement — placed *inside* a proc, after some content has already been emitted, it silently computes the wrong address, confirmed via an isolated Asm/02/Link/02 test (2026-07-12).** Mechanism: Asm/02's `.link` directive passes its argument through to the `.prg` verbatim with zero side effects on Asm/02's *own* address bookkeeping — it has no idea `.align page` means anything, so it keeps numbering every byte after it as if the directive were a no-op (i.e., sequentially, with no gap). Link/02, when it later processes that same `.align page` line, *does* insert real padding to reach the page boundary — but the content run immediately after it is still tagged with the stale, gap-less offset Asm/02 assigned, and Link/02 writes it at `(page_boundary + that_stale_offset)`, not at `page_boundary` itself. Test: a proc with 9 bytes of content, then `.link .align page`, then a labeled instruction Asm/02 numbered as proc-relative offset 9 — final linked position was `page_boundary + 9`, not `page_boundary` as intended. The *only* case that works is `.link .align page` immediately before `proc X` (no content emitted yet for that proc, so Asm/02's per-proc offset counter is still sitting at 0 when the proc's real content starts, regardless of where the proc's true base ends up) — this is exactly the pattern `mr_receive`/`ms_send`/`ms_sendbytes` all use safely. **If a second aligned region is needed within what's conceptually one routine, split it into a genuinely separate proc instead** — see `progs/ms.asm`'s `ms_sendbytes`, pulled out of `ms_send` for exactly this reason (the handshake + 5-field header-echo exchange ahead of the send loop pushed too far into the page for `ms_send`'s own leading alignment to also cover a short branch that deep into the same proc). Do not attempt `.link .align` a second time mid-proc as a shortcut — confirmed broken, not just untested. Two possible real fixes, neither implemented, for whenever this class of need comes up again: (a) teach Asm/02 to recognize its own `.align` directive as a real event even inside a proc, padding its own byte count (and thus every subsequent proc-relative offset) to match, so the `.prg` it emits is internally consistent regardless of where `.align` appears; or (b) make Asm/02's plain `.align` (currently assemble-time-only, see the note in the `.link`/`-r` relaxation history above about it never being emitted into the `.prg`) always emit a real `.align` line into the `.prg`, deferring the actual alignment decision to Link/02 uniformly rather than splitting the responsibility between the two tools depending on whether `.link` was used to smuggle it through.

17. **FIXED UPSTREAM in Asm/02 v1.6 (2026-07-15, `Elf/Asm-02` commit `ae1139a`) — kept here for history and because the practical rule below still matters for anything built with an older Asm/02.** `mov reg, RELOCATABLE_SYMBOL+EQU_CONSTANT_NAME` used to silently drop the relocatable symbol's contribution when the offset was itself a *named* `equ` constant (not a bare numeric literal) — confirmed via direct byte-decode of a real built kernel, 2026-07-15, after it caused a real boot failure (`"No shell on C:."`). This is NOT the same bug as the retracted gotcha #2 (which only ever tested numeric-literal offsets like `real_block+300`, and correctly found those safe) and NOT gotcha #15 (`equ`-aliasing a label *definition* — a different construct entirely; this one is about an ordinary instruction operand). Root cause, found in `asm.c`'s expression evaluator: parsing each operand of a `+` expression goes through the same identifier-token code path (`asm.c` ~line 1017-1032), and for *any* label token — not just the first one — it unconditionally executes `usedReference = isExternal(i)`. For an expression like `file_dirent+DIRENT_ATTR`, parsing `file_dirent` (a real cross-file relocatable symbol) correctly sets `usedReference` to its external index; but parsing `DIRENT_ATTR` immediately after (a plain, non-external, non-local `equ` constant) sets `usedReference = isExternal(DIRENT_ATTR's index)` = `-1`, silently *clobbering* the valid reference just recorded for `file_dirent`. By the time the addition (`numbers[nstack-1] += numbers[nstack]`, plain arithmetic, no reference-awareness) completes and the instruction is emitted, `usedReference < 0` means no relocation fixup gets written at all — the assembler just emits the bare constant (`DIRENT_ATTR`'s value, `128`) with zero contribution from `file_dirent`'s real address. Confirmed via direct kernel.bin decode: `mov rf, file_dirent+DIRENT_ATTR` resolved to `RF = 0x0080` (just the constant) instead of `file_dirent`'s real linked address plus 128 — landing in kernel scratch RAM instead of the real buffer. **A numeric-literal offset (`cur_time+2`, `dcr_sect_lba+2`) does NOT trigger this** — a bare number is parsed via `asm_convertNumber` (a completely different code path that never touches `usedReference`), so the base symbol's reference survives untouched. This is exactly why the retracted gotcha #2's isolated test (which used `real_block+300`, a numeric literal) found no problem, while this project's real kernel code — which almost always uses a *named* `equ` constant as the field offset (`DIRENT_ATTR`, `FCB_ELBA`, `DE_SIZE`, etc.) — hit it immediately and severely. **Practical rule: never fold `mov reg, symbol` + `add16 reg, CONST` into `mov reg, symbol+CONST` when `CONST` is a named `equ` label and `symbol` is a relocatable (cross-file or in-proc `ds`/`dw`/`db`) reference — only when `CONST` is a bare numeral, or when `symbol` itself is a fully-resolved absolute constant (like `PROG_BASE`-derived `LOADER_ARGS`, where no relocation is needed for either term regardless).** Caught only because a kernel-wide fold pass attempting exactly this (2026-07-15) reached hardware and produced a boot failure; verification that only checked jump-table `lbr` entries (a different, `!`/`#`-tagged fixup path — see the size-reduction bullet above) did not catch it. The fold pass was fully reverted the same day, then **re-applied successfully after Asm/02 v1.6 fixed the root cause** (only update the evaluator's reference tracking when the current token is itself a genuine reference, so a later plain-constant token can no longer clobber one recorded earlier in the same expression) — verified both by isolated tests and by direct byte-decode of the previously-broken site in the real kernel. As of Asm/02 v1.6, the practical rule above no longer applies to `mov`/`lbr`-style operands. **Correction (2026-07-21): the claim that `equ alias: equ symbol+CONST` (gotcha #15) "remains a separate, still-open bug, confirmed unaffected by this fix" was itself wrong** — re-testing found `equ`-aliasing now resolves correctly too (isolated tests plus a real kernel-build byte-decode), almost certainly because it shares this exact root cause (`OT_EQU`'s `processArgs` call goes through the same expression evaluator) and was fixed as an unlogged side effect of this same v1.6 change. See gotcha #15's own updated entry for the full re-verification.

18. **`ADD16`/`SUB16`'s register-register form clobbers `M(R2)` as its own internal scratch — confirmed by decoding its real opcode expansion (2026-07-20).** `include/opcodes.def`'s `.op "ADD16","NR",...` and `.op "SUB16","NR",...` entries both emit `STR R2` (byte `0x52`) twice as part of computing the sum/difference between two registers — the CDP1802 has no register-to-register ALU path, so a register-register 16-bit add/sub is implemented by staging one operand into `M(R2)` and using the memory-operand `ADD`/`ADC` (or `SD`/`SDB`) form against it. This is invisible almost everywhere in this codebase, because the established idiom used *everywhere else* is "`str r2` (stage one comparison/arithmetic operand), immediately followed by `sm`/`smb`/`xor`/`add`/`adc`/etc (consume it)" with nothing in between — but if a register-register `add16`/`sub16` call is inserted between a `str r2` and the instruction meant to consume it, it silently overwrites the staged value first. **The immediate-constant form (`add16 reg, CONSTANT`) is NOT affected** — it expands to `ADI`/`ADCI` (add immediate), which never touches memory at all; only `add16 regA, regB` (both operands registers) is risky.
    - **Found the hard way as the root cause of edlin's `S` (search) command always reporting "Not found." even when the substring was provably present** — `ed_line_contains` (`progs/edlin.asm`) computed the haystack byte's address, staged it via `str r2`, then computed the needle byte's address via a *third* `add16` before ever reading the staged byte back for the `xor` comparison — silently destroying it every single time. This was invisible to extensive, repeated static review of the comparison *algorithm* itself (which was always logically correct) because the destructive side effect lived a level below the algorithm, in a toolchain macro's own implementation detail nobody had previously discovered in this project. Took 5 rounds of increasingly targeted hardware diagnostics to isolate: needle text confirmed correct, haystack line content confirmed correct (byte-identical to what `L` already displayed), address arithmetic confirmed correct (`computed_addr - ed_li_ptr == outer`, exactly as expected), the staged byte confirmed correct *immediately* after being stored — and then, decisively, confirmed *wrong* when re-read moments later after the needle-address `add16` ran, consistently, every time, regardless of which of the 4 needle characters was being compared. Fixed by reordering: compute *both* the haystack and needle addresses first (into separate registers), so no `add16`/`sub16` call ever runs between the `str r2` and the `xor` that depends on it.
    - **Proactive codebase-wide sweep** (script-based: for every `str r2`, scan forward for either a safe consumer — `sm`/`smb`/`xor`/`or`/`and`/`add`/`adc`/`sd`/`sdb` — or a risky intervening register-register `add16`/`sub16`) found `ed_line_contains` was the **only** instance of this pattern anywhere in `kernel/`, `progs/`, `lib/`, and `boot/`. The first pass of the sweep flagged one more site (`progs/edlin.asm`'s insert-mode line-shifting code) that turned out to be a false positive on manual inspection — its own `str r2` is consumed by an `adc` two lines later (a self-contained hand-rolled 16-bit add, the same pattern `ADD16`'s own macro uses), fully resolved *before* the register-register `add16` the scanner had flagged even runs; the scanner's consumer list was widened to include `add`/`adc`/`sd`/`sdb` (not just the comparison ops) to eliminate that false positive.
    - **Practical rule: never let a register-register `add16`/`sub16` run between a `str r2` (staging a comparison/arithmetic operand) and the instruction meant to read it back via `M(R(X))` — compute all needed addresses/offsets *first*, then stage-and-consume as one tight, uninterrupted sequence**, matching every other comparison already in this codebase. This rule does not apply to the immediate-constant form, which is unaffected.

19. **FIXED UPSTREAM in Asm/02 (2026-07-24, `Elf/Asm-02` commit `f5ba6f7`) — kept here for history and because the byte-decode discipline below still matters regardless.** Originally reported (2026-07-23) as "a same-file `equ` constant referenced before its own declaration gets corrupted under `-r` relaxation." **That diagnosis was wrong on both counts**, found by going back and actually isolating the mechanism (2026-07-24) rather than trusting the first plausible explanation: forward-vs-backward reference order turned out not to matter at all (an isolated repro with the `equ` declared *before* its own use, still inside the same `proc`, corrupted identically), and `-r` wasn't the trigger either (the identical corruption reproduced in a plain, non-relaxed link — the original case only *looked* `-r`-specific because that one proc happened to link at address 0, where the buggy fixup math is a no-op by coincidence). **The real rule was: a same-file `equ` constant is unsafe to reference in an expression anywhere *inside the same `proc`* it's declared in — regardless of order, regardless of `-r`.** Root cause, found by decoding Asm/02's own `.prg` output byte-for-byte for a minimal repro: `asm.c`'s expression evaluator (`usedLocal = 1` in the proc-local-reference case) tagged *any* reference to a same-proc label as needing a local relocation fixup, with no check for whether that label was a real address (which legitimately needs one) or a plain `equ` constant (a fixed, position-independent value that never does) — `equ` constants go through `setLabel()` into the exact same label table as address labels, with nothing previously distinguishing the two. The bogus fixup marker made `link02`'s local-fixup handler treat the constant's own operand byte, plus whatever instruction byte immediately followed it, as a real 2-byte relocatable target — corrupting both. This project's kernel only ever hit it once (`REN_NEW_NAME_MAX` in `kernel/file.asm`, chasing a MOVE/REN bug) because it's the only same-file `equ` this codebase ever declared inside a `proc` at all — moving it outside the `proc` entirely (not "moving it earlier," which alone would NOT have fixed it) worked around the bug at the time. **Now fixed at the root in Asm/02 itself** (a `labelIsEqu[]` table, checked before setting `usedLocal`), verified via the same isolated repros (forward, backward, non-zero placement address, with and without `-r`) plus a full byte-identical regression check against this project's own kernel and `proc`-using libraries — no source change needed here as a result, the old `REN_NEW_NAME_MAX` workaround remains correct but is no longer load-bearing. **Standing lesson, independent of the above and still fully valid**: when verifying anything about a `-r`-relaxed build, decode the bytes of the ACTUAL linked `kernel.bin` directly — the `.lst` file is a pre-relaxation artifact and its addresses/offsets do not reflect the final, renumbered layout once relaxation has shrunk anything in the same proc. **Second standing lesson, from how the original diagnosis went wrong**: a plausible-sounding root cause backed by ONE confirming test (moving the `equ` fixed the observed symptom) isn't the same as an ISOLATED, mechanism-level repro — the fix that appeared to confirm "forward reference under `-r`" actually changed two variables at once (declaration order AND proc-scope), and only re-testing each variable independently revealed which one actually mattered (neither, as it turned out — it was a third variable, proc-scope, that both earlier tests happened to also change together).

- **File globbing redesigned as a library (`lib/file_glob.asm`) instead of shell-side pre-expansion — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-27, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Zero kernel changes.** Root-fixes the `ARGV_MAX_ARGS=16` truncation bug found and merely worked around earlier the same day (`copy C:/cfg/*.* .` misresolving its destination once `/cfg` had 15+ matches — that fix added an error message but the 16-slot ceiling itself was structural). `progs/shell.asm`'s tokenizer no longer expands `*`/`?` at all — the entire `glob_expand`/`ge_*` block (~750 lines) was deleted, keeping only `glob_match` (relocated verbatim into the new library). A wildcard pattern now passes through `tok_done` as one unexpanded argv token; each of the 6 glob-consuming programs (`COPY`, `MOVE`, `DEL`, `ATTRIB`, `DIR`, `LS` — `XCOPY` and single-filename programs don't need it) detects and expands it itself via three new calls: `is_glob(RF=name)->DF` (pure string scan), `glob_init(RF=pattern,RD=caller-owned GLOB_CTX_LEN=133-byte context)->DF` (resolves and stashes the prefix directory, does NOT scan), and `glob_next(RD=context)->RF=match,DF` (re-opens and re-scans the directory from scratch every call, skipping `context.scanned_count` already-seen entries, returning the next match into the context's own small fixed `result_buf`). This eliminates the `ARGV_MAX_ARGS` ceiling entirely — match count is now bounded only by available RAM, not by argv slots — and needs zero dynamic memory (no `K_GLOB_RESERVE`, no himem reservation of any kind).
  - **Design correction during planning, direct user feedback**: the first draft reused `kernel/glob.asm`'s existing `K_GLOB_RESERVE` dynamic himem arena, collecting ALL matches at once before the caller ever saw the first one. The user directly asked why, since a caller only ever consumes one match at a time via `glob_next` — a lazy re-scan-and-skip design needs a buffer sized for exactly one filename, not a number proportional to match count. Redesigned around this on the spot: eliminated the two-sided arena, the reservation lifecycle, and the "must stash the reservation's base address before something else nests on top" hazard entirely — `glob_next` re-derives everything fresh from the small fixed context block every call. Accepted cost: O(matches × directory size) `K_DIR_READ` calls worst case, judged a good trade for a DOS-scale embedded filesystem, directly consistent with this project's own precedent (`hist_compact`'s repeated `K_STAT` re-checks, `$FOO`'s per-reference environment re-open). `kernel/glob.asm`'s `K_GLOB_RESERVE` mechanism itself is untouched, just no longer used by this feature.
  - **Per-program integration, mechanical 3-way branch at each "this argv entry" point**: (1) `is_glob` DF=1 (not a glob) → process the literal filename exactly as before; (2) `is_glob` DF=0, `glob_init` DF=1 (bad prefix path) → this argv entry's own error, distinct from case 3; (3) `is_glob` DF=0, `glob_init` DF=0 → loop `glob_next`, processing each match the same way case 1 would; zero matches (nullglob-off) falls back to processing the original unexpanded text as a literal. `COPY`/`MOVE` additionally decide "does the destination need to already be an existing directory" statically, up front, from `is_glob` on every source argument (confirmed with the user via `AskUserQuestion`, choosing this over exactly matching bash's own two-pass "expand first, then decide" behavior) — if ANY source is a glob pattern at all, even one that turns out to match exactly one file, the destination must already exist as a directory. `DIR`'s single-argument case (`argc==2`) now checks `is_glob` on `argv[1]` *before* its old `K_PATH_RESOLVE`-and-maybe-list-a-directory logic — a glob (even one matching exactly one file) always routes into the same per-match `K_STAT`+`print_dir_entry` body `dir_multi_arg` uses, closing an old accidental quirk where match count (via old shell pre-expansion) decided which of two different code paths ran. `LS` got the identical treatment for its own single-path case, plus its pre-existing `ls_multi_stat` per-path loop, sharing a new `ls_stat_and_add` helper factored out of what used to be `ls_multi_stat`-only inline code.
  - **A serious, real bug — present in all 6 programs (7 loop sites total, `DIR` has two) — caught in review before any hardware round, not by the D-clobber/self-overwrite sweep scripts (neither is designed to catch this class).** Every glob-consuming loop's first draft read: `call glob_next` (returns the match pointer in `RF`) → `mov rf, xxx_glob_found` / `ldi 1` / `str rf` (sets a "found at least one match" flag) → `call xxx_process_one` (expects `RF` = the match pointer). The flag-set step silently overwrote `RF` with `xxx_glob_found`'s own address *before* the per-item call ever ran — every glob-driven `DEL`/`COPY`/`MOVE`/`ATTRIB`/`DIR`/`LS` operation would have operated on a data-byte address interpreted as a garbage filename string instead of the real matched path. Caught by re-reading `glob_next`'s own contract (`RF` = match pointer) against each loop's actual instruction sequence, immediately after writing the last of the 7 sites (`ls.asm`) for an unrelated reason (double-checking a different register). Fixed identically in all 7 sites: stash `RF` into `R9` (free scratch at that point in every case) immediately after `glob_next` returns, before the flag-set clobbers `RF`, then reload `RF` from `R9` right before the per-item call. Re-verified via a full re-assemble of all 6 files (0 errors/warnings) after the fix.
  - **Verification**: `lib/file_glob.asm` (505 lines) and all 6 touched `progs/*.asm` files assemble clean (0 errors/warnings each); D-clobber/self-overwrite/gotcha-18/duplicate-label sweeps run across all 7 files, 0 real hits (a handful of `K_FILE_CLOSE`/`READ`/`WRITE` false positives in `copy.asm`/`move.asm`'s pre-existing, untouched copy+delete fallback code, where `RD` — not `D` — is the FCB argument per this project's caller-allocated-FCB convention). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test`: kernel confirmed byte-unchanged (`git status` shows zero diffs under `kernel/`/`include/`/`boot/` besides the new, kernel-unrelated `include/file_glob.inc`), all 31 `bin/`+9 `test/bin/` programs build 0 errors/warnings. New Makefile rules: `lib/file_glob.prg`, plus explicit link overrides for `bin/copy`/`bin/del`/`bin/move`/`bin/attrib` (new) and `bin/dir`/`bin/ls` (added to their existing multi-file rules alongside `lib/fmt32.prg`/`lib/heap_bump.prg`/`lib/env.prg`).
  - **A second, more severe bug — a build/link-time defect, not a logic bug — found on the FIRST hardware round (2026-07-27): every program linking against `lib/file_glob.asm` (`DIR`/`LS`/`COPY` all tried; presumably `MOVE`/`DEL`/`ATTRIB` too) failed to load at all, printing `"Invalid program file."`** (`CD`/`PWD`/`TYPE`/`PRINTENV` — none of which link `file_glob.prg` — worked normally, immediately narrowing it to something about the new library specifically). Root cause: see toolchain gotcha #20 — `gn_dot`/`gn_dotdot` (two tiny `.`/`..` string constants used by `glob_next`) were declared as bare top-level data sitting in the gap between two `proc`/`endp` blocks rather than inside the file's own shared data proc (`_file_glob_data`, which already correctly held `gi_ctx`/`gn_ctx`/`gn_examined`/`gn_dirent` the proper way) — Asm/02 emitted them as literal, non-relocatable content at a stray low address, which Link/02 then used as the WHOLE linked binary's base, corrupting every program built against this library (confirmed by direct inspection: the broken `bin/dir`/`bin/ls`/`bin/copy` didn't start with the `'EDF'` magic at all, and were 4-8x their expected size). **Fixed** by moving `gn_dot`/`gn_dotdot` into `_file_glob_data` alongside the other shared fields, with matching `extrn`/`public` declarations — the exact same treatment every other cross-proc-referenced label in the file already had. Confirmed fixed via a targeted `link02` re-link (`Lowest address: 4600`, matching `PROG_BASE`, instead of the broken build's `0189`) before doing anything else, then a full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` rebuild (0 errors/warnings) and a direct magic-byte/size sanity check across all 6 affected binaries (`bin/dir`/`bin/ls`/`bin/copy`/`bin/move`/`bin/del`/`bin/attrib` all now start with `4544 4601` = `'EDF'`+version 1, and are back to plausible, proportional sizes). **Awaiting a fresh hardware round to confirm the fix** — the original functional verification list below still applies once loading itself is confirmed working.
  - **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round covering, per program: confirm each of the 6 programs now LOADS AND RUNS at all (the immediate regression this round's fix addresses); zero matches (nullglob fallback to the literal text), exactly one match, several matches (<16, should be behaviorally unchanged from before), and more than 16 matches (the actual point of this redesign — the exact scenario that broke `COPY` earlier the same session); a glob against a bad/nonexistent directory prefix (the new, distinct `glob_init` DF=1 path, separate from zero-matches); for `COPY`/`MOVE` specifically, a glob source copied/moved to a non-directory destination (confirms the "requires an existing directory" policy); for `DIR` specifically, confirm a glob matching exactly one file now behaves identically to one matching several (closing the old match-count-dependent quirk).

20. **Plain top-level data (a bare `label: db/dw/ds ...` with no enclosing `proc`) sitting between two `proc`/`endp` blocks, but referenced FROM inside one of those procs, doesn't just risk a dropped fixup (gotcha #1/#6) — it can anchor the WHOLE LINKED BINARY to the wrong base address, corrupting every program that links against that object file.** Found 2026-07-27, root cause of "Invalid program file." on every one of `COPY`/`DIR`/`LS` (every program linking against the new `lib/file_glob.asm`) immediately after that library's first hardware round. `lib/file_glob.asm`'s `glob_next` proc referenced `gn_dot`/`gn_dotdot` (two tiny `db ".",0` / `db "..",0` labels) via plain `mov rd, gn_dot` — but those two labels were declared as bare top-level data, OUTSIDE any `proc`, sitting in the gap between `glob_next`'s `endp` and the next proc's `proc` line. Confirmed via direct `.prg` inspection: the two labels' bytes were emitted as a stray, non-proc-wrapped `:0189 2e 00 2e 2e 00` content line — Asm/02 assigns non-proc content a literal address from its own top-level counter (here landing at a small offset near where the file's very first non-`org`'d bytes happened to fall), and Link/02 then treats that as a real absolute placement requirement, dragging the entire linked image's own "Lowest address" down from the intended `PROG_BASE` ($4600) to that stray low address ($0189) instead of $4600 — every subsequent byte in the binary, including the `'EDF'` magic header itself, ends up at the wrong offset relative to where the kernel's loader expects it, and `prog_run`'s own EDF-magic check correctly rejects the result as "Invalid program file." Confirmed by direct binary inspection: the broken `bin/dir`/`bin/ls`/`bin/copy` didn't even start with `'EDF'` — they started with raw FAT-entry-looking bytes (`2e 00 2e 2e 00...`, the very `gn_dot`/`gn_dotdot` content itself), and were 4-8x larger than expected. **Fix**: move any data referenced across proc boundaries into the file's own shared data proc (here, `_file_glob_data`, which already correctly held `gi_ctx`/`gn_ctx`/`gn_examined`/`gn_dirent` the same way) with matching `extrn`/`public` declarations — never leave a bare label sitting in the gap between two `proc`/`endp` blocks in a file that's otherwise fully proc-based. **Practical rule: in any `proc`-based file (kernel modules, `lib/*.asm`), EVERY label is either (a) fully local to one proc (declared and used only inside its own `proc`/`endp`), or (b) declared inside a dedicated data proc with `public`, and `extrn`'d by every proc that uses it — there is no safe third option of "just a bare top-level label," even for something as small as a 2-byte string constant.** This bug class is NOT caught by any of this project's existing static sweeps (D-clobber, self-overwrite, gotcha-18) — the only way it was caught was because the build's own `Lowest address` in the link log didn't match the expected `PROG_BASE`, and because the resulting binary was visibly, grossly oversized. **Worth checking `Lowest address` in every `link02` invocation's output against the expected base (`PROG_BASE` for a program, or the kernel's own base for `kernel.bin`) as a matter of course** — a mismatch there is a fast, cheap tell for exactly this bug class, cheaper than waiting for a hardware "Invalid program file." report.

21. **✅ FIXED UPSTREAM in Asm/02 v1.7 (2026-07-27, `~/projects/Elf/Asm-02/asm.c`, commit `afe7011`, "Fix bug that recognized strings like `re_end` as registers" — pushed and installed to `/opt/elfc/asm02` the same day; confirmed still live as of 2026-08-01, `asm02` reports `"Asm/02 v1.7"`).** A forward `lbz` reference to a label could resolve to an address a constant number of bytes BEFORE that label's own true position, with no error or warning — and this turned out to be SYSTEMIC across an entire file region, not a one-off affecting a single label. Found while chasing the command-line-editing feature's multi-round "Enter hangs" bug — `progs/shell.asm`'s `rlwh_finish:` label was correctly positioned (confirmed via its own `.lst` entry) but every `lbz rlwh_finish` forward reference encoded a target 6 bytes earlier, landing inside the label immediately preceding it in the source. **Ruled out along the way, each via a direct, reproducible test against the real `.lst` output**: name collision, which specific label precedes it (offset stayed exactly 6 bytes short regardless of which label it was), comment placement, forward-reference count, a "single global drift" theory (disproven — each affected label independently carries its own -6 error). **Scope, once checked systematically**: a full-file scan (every `lb*` branch instruction's encoded target vs. every label's real `.lst`-confirmed address) found **61 mismatches across the whole file** — not just the 8 command-line-editing labels first found broken, but also `hist_copy_entry`, `hist_redraw_linebuf`, `hist_recall_up`, `hist_recall_down`, `hist_load`, `hist_split`, `hist_append`, and `hist_compact` — functions previously hardware-confirmed working in earlier sessions. A hand-verified instance (`hist_copy_entry`'s `hce_loop`/`hce_done`) confirmed even a **backward** branch reference was wrong by 6 bytes, ruling out "forward reference" as the defining characteristic.
    - **Root cause, found via a debug-instrumented build of Asm/02 itself** (a scratch copy with `fprintf` tracing added to `addLabel()`, comparing each label's pass-1-recorded value against its real pass-2/`.lst` position): the divergence traced to `isRReg()` — the function deciding whether an operand token (e.g. the second argument of `mov rf, re_pos`) is a CPU register name. Its "is there more identifier text after this register-looking prefix" check only tested for digits and letters (`0-9`, `a-z`, `A-Z`) — it was missing `_`, `!`, and `$`, which this same assembler's own label-scanning code elsewhere treats as valid identifier-continuation characters. So a label like `re_pos` (`r` + `e`, a hex-range letter that looks like a valid 2-char register suffix, immediately followed by `_`) was misidentified as the register "re" — confirmed directly with a standalone test harness (`isRReg("re_pos")` returned "IS A REGISTER"; `rd_pos`, `rc_something`, `re_`, `ra_foo` all reproduced the same false positive; genuine register names and unrelated labels like `rlwh_escape`/`hist_cur_len` were unaffected).
    - **The mechanism that turns this into a byte-miscounting bug**: `MOV` is defined via two `.op` macro forms in `include/opcodes.def` — `"NR"` (register-to-register, 4 bytes) and `"NW"` (register-to-word/address, 6 bytes). The match loop (`asm.c` ~line 2516) tries `NR` first; its `'R'`-slot check requires `isreg[j]` true AND the operand's *value* ≤ 15. For a normal, already-resolved label this value check alone would reject `NR` regardless of the `isRReg` bug — but for a **forward-referenced** label (not yet defined at the point this instruction is sized during pass 1), `getLabel()`'s own documented "not found" fallback returns `0` — which IS ≤ 15. With `isRReg` wrongly reporting true and the temporary value wrongly reporting ≤15, the match loop selects the WRONG, SHORTER 4-byte `NR` encoding during pass 1's own address-counting pass. Pass 2 (reached after every label is fully resolved to its real value) correctly re-evaluates and selects the right 6-byte `NW` form — but by then it's too late: `addLabel()` only ever runs during pass 1, so every subsequent label in the file inherits pass 1's now-permanently-wrong, 2-bytes-short running address, compounding further with each additional affected instruction (explaining why the real file showed a mix of exactly `-6`, `-2`, and other offsets rather than one uniform constant).
    - **Fix**: added `if (*line == '_' || *line == '!' || *line == '$') return 0;` to both of `isRReg`'s exclusion-check blocks, immediately after the existing digit/letter checks — the minimal change matching the assembler's own established identifier-character set used elsewhere in the same file. Verified via a standalone before/after test harness across 20+ cases (all known-buggy labels now correctly return "not a register"; all genuine register names, including edge cases like `r1`/`r15`/punctuation-terminated tokens, are unaffected).
    - **Verification, held to a high bar given the fix touches the shared toolchain, not just this one project's own source**: (1) built a debug copy of Asm/02 with the fix and re-ran the full 61-mismatch scan against `progs/shell.asm` — **0 mismatches**. (2) Applied the identical fix to the real `~/projects/Elf/Asm-02/asm.c`, bumped `NAME_AND_VERSION` to `"Asm/02 v1.7"`, built clean. (3) **Full zero-regression check**: rebuilt the ENTIRE ELF-DOS project (kernel + all 31 `progs/*` + all 9 `test/*`) in an isolated scratch copy using this fixed `asm02` binary, then compared every single output file byte-for-byte against the current production build (made with the unpatched, installed `/opt/elfc/asm02`) — **kernel.bin, kernel-full.bin, krnboot.bin, and every program except `bin/shell` are byte-IDENTICAL**; `bin/shell` is the same size but has different operand bytes (branches now resolve directly to their real targets instead of relying on the padding-workaround fallthrough), confirming the fix has zero side effects on any code that wasn't already broken. (4) Re-ran the 61-mismatch scan against this real (non-debug) build's own `shell.lst` — **0 mismatches**, confirming the production-grade build (not just the instrumented debug one) is clean.
    - **Installed and confirmed live (2026-07-27)**: the user pushed the fix in the Asm-02 repo and installed it to `/opt/elfc/asm02` themselves (`asm02` now reports `"Asm/02 v1.7"`). Before their install, the user independently double-checked the fix's precision — asking whether it correctly rejects near-miss cases like `rg`/`R$` — re-verified with the actual test harness: both were ALREADY correctly rejected even before the fix (their second character never enters the ambiguous `2-9`/`a-f`/`A-F` branch at all), confirming the fix is narrowly targeted at exactly the real bug shape (a valid register-letter immediately followed by `_`/`!`/`$`) and doesn't overreach. One unrelated, pre-existing observation surfaced during that check and left alone (not part of this bug, not touched by the fix): `isRReg("r0")` returns "not a register" — `'0'` was never in the checked `2`-`9` range — apparently never triggered as a problem anywhere in this codebase.
    - **`progs/shell.asm`'s 8 padding workarounds removed (2026-07-27) now that the real fix is live** — `rlwh_finish`/`rlwh_backspace`/`rlwh_left`/`rlwh_right`/`rlwh_delete_at`/`rlwh_escape`/`rlwh_up`/`rlwh_down` all had their `GHI R0` ×6 blocks and explanatory comments deleted, restoring each label to sit directly after the code preceding it with no padding. Re-ran the full 349-branch-instruction mismatch scan against the freshly assembled `shell.lst` (using the newly-installed `/opt/elfc/asm02` v1.7, no debug copy) — **0 mismatches**, confirming every one of these labels now resolves correctly natively, with no workaround needed. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`, `kernel.bin`/`kernel-full.bin`/`krnboot.bin` checksums unchanged from the whole rest of this session, confirming the newly-installed compiler is a safe, zero-regression swap now in actual production use for this project); `bin/shell` shrank to 11018 bytes (down from 11070 with padding).
  - **Ninth hardware round (2026-07-27), on the padding-free build: real progress.** Right/Left arrows, mid-line insert/delete, and Ctrl-E (end) all confirmed working. Cut-buffer keys (Ctrl-K/U/W/Y) confirmed silently ignored as designed. Two remaining items: (1) **Ctrl-A (home) needs two presses** — the first press does nothing, the second correctly moves to the start of the line; Ctrl-E never exhibits this asymmetry. Extensive static re-reading of `rlwh_home`'s code (unchanged by the padding-removal edits, confirmed via fresh read) found no logic bug — the backspace-count loop, the `edit_cursor=0` write, and the "already at 0" guard all trace correctly on paper for a single press. Since the toolchain bug is now fixed and no longer a suspect, this points at either a genuine remaining logic bug not yet spotted, or a byte-stream/timing quirk specific to byte value 1. (2) **DEL key request** (`ESC[3~`, should delete the character under the cursor like Ctrl-D, distinct from Backspace/Ctrl-H which deletes the character before it) — not yet implemented at the time of the report.
  - **Round-10 diagnostic added for the Ctrl-A mystery**: a per-keystroke raw-byte-value echo (same buffered `f_uintout`+`K_MSG` pattern used successfully in the earlier "Enter hangs" investigation) at the very top of `rlwh_loop`, plus two targeted markers inside `rlwh_home` itself — `[HOME cur=N]` printed at entry, before the "already at 0" check (reveals whether the ineffective first press is genuinely taking the no-op path with an unexpectedly-already-zero `edit_cursor`, or falling through into the real backspace loop), and `[HOMEDONE cur=N]` printed right before `rh_done`'s own return (confirms whether the routine's logic actually completes and correctly zeroes `edit_cursor`). New `diag_buf`/`diag_saved_char` scratch re-added for this round.
  - **DEL key support added in the same round** (`progs/shell.asm`'s `rlwh_escape`): after the existing `'D'` (left arrow) check, a new `'3'` check recognizes the start of the 4-byte `ESC [ 3 ~` sequence (longer than the 3-byte `ESC [ A`-style arrow sequences, since `'3'` doesn't terminate the sequence by itself) — on match, reads one more byte expecting `'~'` (malformed/unrecognized sequences are discarded, never guessed at, matching the same philosophy already established for the Up/Down byte-drop fix), then `lbr rlwh_delete_at` — confirmed safe to jump to directly, since `rlwh_delete_at` reads everything it needs fresh from `edit_cursor`/`hist_cur_len` memory with no dependency on any register the caller might be holding.
  - **Verified**: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings, 2422 lines), D-clobber sweep (same 21 pre-existing false positives, none in new code), self-overwrite sweep and duplicate-label scan clean, full branch-mismatch scan **0 mismatches across 351 branch instructions** (up from 349, matching the 2 new branches added for DEL key handling) — confirming the now-fixed, now-production `/opt/elfc/asm02` v1.7 continues to resolve every branch correctly even as new code is added, no padding needed anywhere.
