Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`; 31/9 binaries present), `bin/shell` now 11204 bytes.
  - **Tenth hardware round (2026-07-27): mystery solved, and it was never a shell bug.** The diagnostic transcript was decisive: for the "ineffective" first Ctrl-A press, **nothing printed at all** — not even the per-keystroke byte-value echo, which fires unconditionally the instant `f_uread` returns. That means the shell was never actually receiving a byte on the first press; it was still blocked in the read call. Only the second press's byte (value `1`) ever arrived and was processed correctly (`[HOME cur=3]`/`[HOMEDONE cur=0]`, both exactly as expected for a 3-character line). Asked the user whether they connect via a terminal multiplexer — **confirmed: minicom, whose own default command-prefix key is ALSO Ctrl-A**, exactly like GNU `screen`'s well-known Ctrl-A prefix behavior. A single Ctrl-A gets intercepted by minicom itself; pressing it again immediately is minicom's own passthrough convention for sending a literal Ctrl-A through to the remote program — this fully and exactly explains the "always needs two presses, second one behaves correctly" symptom with zero code involved. **No fix needed or possible on the ELF-DOS side** — this is client-side terminal-software behavior, not a bug in `rlwh_home` or anywhere else. The user's own explicit note for later: real Home/End keys (`ESC[H`/`ESC[F` or `ESC[1~`/`ESC[4~`, terminal-dependent) could be added as an alternative to Ctrl-A/Ctrl-E specifically to sidestep this class of terminal-prefix collision — deferred, not started.
  - **All temporary diagnostics removed (2026-07-27)** per the user's explicit request once the mystery was resolved: the per-keystroke byte-value echo (top of `rlwh_loop`) and the `[HOME cur=N]`/`[HOMEDONE cur=N]` markers inside `rlwh_home` (both added this same round to chase what turned out to be a non-bug) fully stripped, along with their `diag_buf`/`diag_saved_char` scratch fields — `grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" progs/shell.asm` confirmed clean. `rlwh_home`/`rh_done` restored to their bare, pre-diagnostic form. **DEL key support (added the same round) was KEPT** — it's a real, working feature addition, not a diagnostic. Re-verified: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings, 2371 lines), same 21 pre-existing D-clobber false positives, self-overwrite/duplicate-label scans clean, full branch-mismatch scan **0 mismatches across 351 instructions** (DEL key's own branches still correctly accounted for). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`; 31/9 binaries present), `bin/shell` now 11041 bytes.
  - **✅ ELEVENTH AND FINAL HARDWARE ROUND (2026-07-27): CONFIRMED WORKING, FEATURE COMPLETE.** The user reconfigured minicom's own command-prefix key (away from its default Ctrl-A) and confirmed Ctrl-A now works correctly on the first press, closing out the false alarm from the previous round. DEL key and Up/Down history recall both confirmed working. Hitting the 127-character line cap doesn't break anything. **This closes out command-line editing as a hardware-confirmed, shipped feature** — the full arc: two rounds of real bugs in this feature's own code (the `ert_start` tail-position conflation, the missing physical-backspace-before-redraw step), then the multi-round "Enter hangs" investigation that turned out to be a genuine, previously-unknown Asm/02 toolchain bug (gotcha #21, now fixed upstream and installed in production), then a final round that surfaced no remaining code bugs at all — only a terminal-client configuration collision (minicom's own Ctrl-A prefix).
  - **New, deliberately out-of-scope finding for future work**: when a single input line grows long enough to wrap across multiple terminal rows (relevant once a line approaches the 127-character cap on a narrower terminal), the cursor-editing math — which only ever reasons in terms of a flat character offset within `LINE_BUF`, not physical terminal rows/columns — visibly breaks down, since backspace/redraw counts assume a single row. Not investigated or fixed this session; the user's own framing is that a real fix likely needs the terminal's actual column width (a `COLUMNS`-style environment variable, per the same "future options" note already on file for `LS`'s own column-layout code) to translate a flat character offset into the right number of row-wraps for cursor movement. **Filed as a future work item, not started.**
    - **Scope beyond this one file, worth remembering**: this bug can affect ANY Asm/02-assembled source, in ANY project, wherever a label matches `r[2-9a-fA-F]` or `r1[0-5]` followed immediately by `_`, `!`, or `$` AND that label is used in a position needing this register-vs-value distinction (chiefly: as an operand to a multi-form `.op` macro like `MOV`) AND is a forward reference at first use. ELFC (the C compiler, which also uses Asm/02) is a plausible future victim if its own code generator ever emits similarly-shaped temporary/label names — worth a proactive check there once the fix is installed.
    - **Practical rule, still true regardless of this fix**: if a forward `lbz`/`lbr`/branch reference to a same-file label produces inexplicable runtime behavior, don't trust the assembler — decode the actual `.lst` file, find the real address of both the branch instruction's encoded target AND the label's own true position, and compare them directly. And when multiple same-shaped labels sit in one region (a dispatch chain, a jump table), check EVERY one of them this way, not just the first one found broken. This is the same "verify the actual claim, decode real bytes" discipline this project has needed before (gotcha #17's `mov reg,symbol+CONST` bug, gotcha #19's `equ`-in-proc bug, gotcha #20's proc-boundary bug above) — a clean assemble with 0 errors/warnings is not evidence that branch targets are correct.
    - **`progs/shell.asm`'s own 8 padding workarounds were removed the same day** (see the "8 padding workarounds removed" bullet above) once `/opt/elfc/asm02` v1.7 was actually installed — confirmed still absent as of the 2026-08-01 catch-up pass (`grep -c "GHI R0" progs/shell.asm` is 0; the string has never appeared in any committed revision of the file, since by the time command-line editing was committed the real fix was already installed and the workaround had already been stripped).

- **`glob_next` made resumable via two new kernel primitives (`K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE`), replacing the original re-scan-and-skip design — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-27, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** User's own direct question after confirming the file_glob redesign worked ("It takes noticeably longer to print each successive entry for something like `dir many*.txt`... Can you not keep enough context to 'pick up where you left off'?"), followed by "Yes, go ahead and do it" once the tradeoff (a real kernel change, this project's highest-risk category) was laid out.
  - **Root cause of why the original design needed a full re-scan**: `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` share ONE kernel-resident scan position (`dir_clust`/`dir_sect`/`dir_eptr`/`dir_eleft`/`dir_cur_lba`/`dir_buf`, `kernel/dir.asm`'s own `_dir_data`) — there was no way for a userland library to "pause" that state across whatever the calling program does with a match in between `glob_next` calls (`K_STAT`, `COPY`, `DEL`, etc. all do their own `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_READ` internally, silently clobbering it).
  - **New kernel routines** (`kernel/dir.asm`): `dir_save_state(RF=caller's DIR_STATE_LEN=9-byte buffer)` snapshots `dir_clust`(2)+`dir_sect`(1)+`dir_eptr`(2)+`dir_eleft`(1)+`dir_cur_lba`(3) = 9 bytes; `dir_restore_state(RF=that buffer)->DF` restores them and re-derives `dir_buf`'s own content via a **direct disk read** at the restored `dir_cur_lba` (same "restore via a fresh read, not a literal byte copy" pattern already established by `dir_create`/`dir_remove`'s own `dir_buf`-borrowing code) rather than snapshotting `dir_buf`'s 512 bytes too. **Deliberately excludes `dir_lfn`/`dir_lfn_chk`/`dir_lfn_ok`/`dir_last_off` from the snapshot** — verified by reading `dir_read`'s own body: `dir_lfn_ok` is unconditionally cleared to 0 immediately before every successful return (both the real-match path and `drd_skip_no_lfn`), meaning an LFN sequence is always fully consumed within the single `dir_read` call that returns the entry it describes and never spans a call boundary — so there is nothing meaningful to preserve there, cutting the snapshot from a would-be 141 bytes (including the 128-byte `dir_lfn` buffer) down to 9. New jump-table slots `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`($0188)/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE`($018B), next free $018E; `DIR_STATE_LEN=9` restated in both `kernel.inc` and `kernel_api.inc` (same "publish a size, not the struct" precedent as `FCB_LEN`/`DIRENT_LEN`). Also fixed a pre-existing, unrelated staleness bug found while adding these: `kernel_api.inc`'s own trailing "next free jump-table slot address" comment had been stuck at `$0179` since before `K_BATCH_ARGS_RESERVE`/`GETARG`/`K_GET_ERRORLEVEL` were added — corrected to `$018E` with a note that `kernel/kernel.asm`'s own trailing comment, right after the real jump table, is the actual source of truth.
  - **`lib/file_glob.asm`'s `glob_next` redesigned around this**: context block grew from `GLOB_CTX_LEN=133` to `141` bytes — `scanned_count`(2 bytes) replaced by `has_state`(1 byte) + `dir_state`(9-byte opaque snapshot). First call for a pattern (`has_state==0`) does a fresh `K_DIR_OPEN`; every call that finds a match calls `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE` right before returning (the kernel's own scan position, thanks to `dir_read`'s own auto-advance, already correctly points at the start of the next unexamined entry) and sets `has_state=1`; the next call resumes via `K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` instead of re-opening. This eliminates the entire `gn_examined`/"skip already-consumed entries" bookkeeping — no directory entry is ever examined twice across a whole `glob_next` sequence, turning it from O(matches × directory size) into a single forward pass. `gn_examined`'s own now-dead declaration/`public` removed from `_file_glob_data`.
  - **Verification**: `kernel/dir.asm` assembled clean standalone before wiring anything else up (0 errors/warnings); full manual register-liveness re-trace of both new routines against a fresh read (confirmed the exact byte-for-byte reverse relationship between `dir_save_state`'s write order and `dir_restore_state`'s read order, and that `dir_restore_state`'s own R7/R8 LBA setup for `f_ideread` exactly mirrors `_dir_next_sector`'s `dns_read`'s own byte layout — R8.lo/R7.hi/R7.lo — the same layout already hand-verified against real hardware behavior for over a year of this project's history). D-clobber sweep clean on both `kernel/dir.asm` and `kernel/kernel.asm`. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout). All 44 `K_*` jump-table slots decoded directly from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr`; `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE`'s own targets specifically cross-checked against `dir_save_state`/`dir_restore_state`'s real linked addresses via `link02 -s` on this exact relaxed build (not a separate, differently-relaxed throwaway link — this project's own standing lesson about `-r` renumbering everything per-build). `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (1536 bytes, zero boot-code impact — this is kernel-resident code, not boot code). `lib/file_glob.asm` assembled clean, D-clobber sweep clean, no duplicate labels, proc/endp balanced (5/5). All 6 glob-consuming binaries (`bin/dir`/`bin/ls`/`bin/copy`/`bin/move`/`bin/del`/`bin/attrib`) re-confirmed to link at `Lowest address: 4600` and start with the correct `'EDF'` magic (re-checking specifically for a repeat of gotcha #20, since this touches the same file that bug lived in — none found). **Kernel margin**: `Highest address` `$4377`, **649 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** (down from 775 before this change — the two new routines plus their jump-table slots cost ~126 bytes) — still healthy, but worth re-checking before the next kernel addition. **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming: `dir many*.txt`-style commands against a directory with many matches are now visibly fast (no longer re-scanning), and a full regression of every glob-consuming program (zero/one/several/many matches, bad path, nullglob fallback) to confirm the resumable design produces identical results to the original re-scan design, not just faster ones — the "no entry examined twice" property is easy to get subtly wrong (an off-by-one in exactly which entry the snapshot resumes AFTER, for instance) and this is genuinely new algorithmic logic, not a mechanical refactor.

- **`DIR`'s `<DIR>` indicator split into its own dedicated column, separate from the size column — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-27, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland (`progs/dir.asm`), zero kernel changes.** User's own explicit request, moving away from the 2026-07-26 design (where `<DIR>` and the byte count shared ONE right-justified 13-column field, matching real Windows `dir`'s own convention) to two always-present columns instead — a dedicated 7-character `<DIR>`-or-blank column (reusing the existing `dir_tag`/`spaces13` constants unchanged: `" <DIR> "` for a directory, the last 7 characters of `spaces13` for a file) printed immediately after date/time, followed by the existing 13-column size-or-blank field (unchanged size-printing logic for a file; a new 13-space-blank branch, `pde_size_blank`, replacing the old shared-field `<DIR>` printing for a directory). Every entry now always shows the same two columns, one of them blank, rather than one column whose content depends on entry type. Verified: clean assemble (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber sweep clean, full clean `make progs` (kernel untouched, 31/9 `bin`/`test/bin` present, correct EDF magic). **Not yet hardware-tested** — needs a round confirming column alignment looks right for a mixed file/directory listing.

- **`fopen_check_append`/`fsk_general`'s duplicate positioning algorithm consolidated into one shared kernel routine, `_fcb_seek_to` — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-27, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED.** User's own direct request, immediately after the `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` addition spent 126 bytes of kernel margin: "Could you compensate for some of the loss of kernel headroom by combining the parallel functions we talked about earlier?" — referring to the long-flagged, never-attempted follow-up (first noted during the 2026-07-26 >64K widening work: "each already complex enough on their own... factoring it out remains a viable future simplification if kernel margin gets tight again") to merge `file_open`'s own append-mode repositioning and `file_seek`'s general-case repositioning, which had independently computed byte-for-byte identical `last_byte_index`/`boff`/`sector_index`/`sector_in_clust`/`cluster_index`/`fat_get`-walk/wrap logic against a target byte position, differing only in what "target" itself was (`FSIZE` for append, an arbitrary validated seek target for `file_seek`) and in what happened immediately after (`file_seek` also clears `FCB_F_IOVALID` and returns the new position; `file_open`'s append case does neither).
  - **Judged safe to consolidate now specifically because both copies had already been independently hardware-bug-hunted and fixed IDENTICALLY twice over** during the >64K bug hunt earlier the same session (the `>>8` `ghi`-vs-`glo` bug, and the `sector_in_clust`-computed-too-late-and-clobbered-by-the-cluster_index-shift-loop bug) — exactly the kind of "nothing has diverged between the two copies" signal that makes a consolidation pass low-risk rather than a way to introduce a new, subtly-different third bug. Re-derived both routines fresh from the current file rather than trusting memory of what they "should" contain, confirming byte-for-byte identical algorithm structure before writing the shared version.
  - **New routine `_fcb_seek_to(RD:R8=target (32-bit, target>0), RB=FCB base) -> DF=0/1`** (`kernel/file.asm`, inserted between `file_init` and `file_open`): sets `FCB_CCLUST`/`CSECT`/`BOFF` only — deliberately does NOT touch `FCB_FPOS` or `FCB_FLAGS`, since `file_open`'s append case and `file_seek`'s own tail (`fsk_set_fpos`, shared with `fsk_rewind`, left completely UNCHANGED) do those differently enough (a plain 4-byte copy vs. also clearing `IOVALID` and returning the new position) that folding them in would have complicated the shared routine's own contract for a small saving. **Deliberately requires `target > 0`** (`last_byte_index = target-1` underflows at 0) — both callers keep their own pre-existing, UNTOUCHED `target==0` shortcuts (`file_open`'s own `FSIZE==0` early-exit, which does nothing at all since a fresh FCB's fields are already correctly zero; `file_seek`'s own `fsk_rewind`, which explicitly zeroes `CCLUST`/`CSECT`/`BOFF`) rather than folding that logic in too, since the two callers' own "what does zero mean here" semantics genuinely differ.
  - **Both call sites thinned to a handful of lines**: `fopen_check_append` now just loads `RD:R8=FSIZE`/`RB=fcb base` (unchanged setup) and calls `_fcb_seek_to`, on success reloading `RB` fresh (`_fcb_seek_to`'s own "Modifies: everything" contract) before its own unchanged `FCB_FPOS=FSIZE` 4-byte copy; a walk failure still unconditionally clears `DF` before returning (matching the pre-consolidation behavior exactly — an append-reposition failure has never failed the open itself). `fsk_general` now just loads `RD:R8=fsk_target`/`RB=FCB base` and calls `_fcb_seek_to`, then falls through into the pre-existing, untouched `fsk_set_fpos` tail on success or branches to the pre-existing, untouched `fseek_io_err` on failure.
  - **Scratch consolidation, done in the same pass since it was already in scope**: `_fcb_seek_to`'s own scratch (`fst_fcb`/`fst_boff`/`fst_cluster_idx`/`fst_sector_in_clust`, 7 bytes) folded into the already-existing `_shared_scratch` 9-byte block (the same mutually-exclusive-scratch-groups mechanism this project already uses for `fdel_*`/`dcr_*`/`drm_*`/`ren_*` — confirmed the mutual-exclusion argument extends cleanly: `_fcb_seek_to` is called only from `file_open`'s append path and `file_seek`, neither of which ever runs while a delete/dir_create/dir_remove/rename operation is in progress on this single-threaded/non-reentrant kernel), replacing the old `fa_boff`/`fa_cluster_idx`/`fa_sector_in_clust` `#define`s that used to point there. `file_seek`'s own separate, permanently-allocated `fsk_boff`/`fsk_cluster_idx`/`fsk_sector_in_clust` (5 bytes, never shared with anything) removed outright now that they're fully dead — this closes out a smaller, previously-flagged-but-unfinished opportunity from the same >64K-era scratch-coalescing work ("file_seek's own `fsk_*` scratch... was never folded into `_shared_scratch`... folding it in would grow the shared block to 16 bytes... for a net saving of roughly 9 bytes" — superseded here: the block didn't need to grow at all, since `_fcb_seek_to`'s 7-byte need already fits within the existing 9-byte size, and the real saving came from deleting the old `fsk_*` fields entirely rather than sharing them).
  - **Verification**: `kernel/file.asm` assembled clean standalone (0 errors/warnings, 3867 lines). A structural mistake was caught and fixed during the edit itself, before assembling: inserting `_fcb_seek_to` as a new `proc`/`endp` block between `file_init` and `file_open` left an ORPHANED extra `endp` behind (the one that used to close `file_init`, positioned after its own header-comment block, no longer matched by any open `proc` once `_fcb_seek_to`'s own explicit `endp` already closed things) — caught by directly re-reading the file's actual resulting structure line-by-line (not assumed), fixed by removing the stray `endp`. D-clobber sweep flagged 2 hits, both confirmed false positives on inspection: `sub16 r8, 1` (and, unrelated, a pre-existing `sub16 rd, r8` elsewhere in the file) followed by `lbdf`/`lbnf` — the sweep script incorrectly treats `lbdf`/`lbnf` as `D`-consumers, but they actually test `DF`, which `sub16`/`add16` correctly set as their own borrow/carry output; this is the same pattern the original, already-hardware-proven code used identically. Self-overwrite sweep clean, proc/endp balanced (26/26), no dangling references to any of the removed labels (`fa_lbi_no_borrow`/`fa_cidx_shr`/`fa_walk_loop`/`fa_no_sector_wrap`/`fsk_lbi_no_borrow`/`fsk_cidx_shr`/`fsk_walk_loop`/`fsk_no_wrap`/`fa_boff`/`fa_cluster_idx`/`fa_sector_in_clust`/`fsk_boff`/`fsk_cluster_idx`/`fsk_sector_in_clust` — grep-confirmed zero remaining code references, only explanatory history comments). Full manual register-liveness re-trace of both thinned call sites against a fresh read, confirming `RD:R8`/`RB` are freshly set immediately before each `call _fcb_seek_to` exactly matching its documented argument convention, and that both callers correctly treat `_fcb_seek_to` as clobbering everything (reloading `RB` fresh afterward rather than trusting it to survive). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings throughout, 31/9 `bin`/`test/bin` present). All 44 `K_*` jump-table slots re-decoded from the built `kernel.bin` and confirmed `0xC0`/`lbr` (unchanged count — this pass adds no new jump-table slots); `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE`'s own targets re-cross-checked against `dir_save_state`/`dir_restore_state`'s real linked addresses on this new build (confirming the unrelated `kernel/dir.asm` work from earlier the same session is undisturbed). `krnboot.bin` byte-identical (1536 bytes). **Kernel margin: `Highest address` `$427d`, 899 bytes before `PROG_BASE`/`KERN_LOAD`** — up from 649 (net +250 bytes gained from this consolidation, fully offsetting and then some the 126-byte cost of the `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` addition earlier the same session; 899 is even better than the 775-byte margin from before either change). **CONFIRMED WORKING ON HARDWARE (2026-07-27)**: user ran `test/bin/big64test` on multiple partitions, all phases passed — this directly exercises both consolidated paths (`big64test`'s own phase C append-mode write goes through `fopen_check_append`→`_fcb_seek_to`; phase E's `SEEK_SET`/`CUR`/`END` checks go through `fsk_general`→`_fcb_seek_to`), across differing `bpb_spc` values the same way the original >64K bug hunt needed to surface its own bugs. This closes out the consolidation's own verification — no regressions from merging the two positioning-math copies.

- **Real cross-drive bug found and fixed the same hardware round: `COPY <glob> <dest>` across drives silently stopped after only a few matches — CONFIRMED FIXED ON HARDWARE 2026-07-27** (user re-ran the exact failing scenario: "That fixed it. Did testing of the globbing on various drives without issue."). User's own report: `copy c:/cfg/*.txt .` while active on drive E:, with `/cfg` holding ~20 matching files, copied only the first 3 and stopped with no error — confirmed via `dir c:/cfg/*.txt` (run from the same E: session) correctly listing all 20, proving the glob itself wasn't the problem, only `COPY`'s own consumption of it.
  - **Root cause**: `K_DIR_OPEN`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` (both new/touched this same session) operate against whichever drive's BPB/FAT-cache happens to be ACTIVE — neither one switches to a specific drive itself. `glob_next`'s resumable design (see the `K_DIR_SAVE_STATE`/`K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` entry above) is vulnerable to this in a way the original re-scan design also would have been, but never surfaced: `COPY`'s own per-match sequence opens the source (on the glob's own drive, C:) then writes the destination (on the CURRENT drive, E:) — the LAST drive-touching operation before control returns to the glob loop is the destination write, leaving E: active. The next `glob_next` call's `K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` still correctly re-reads its saved sector (the LBA is absolute, drive-agnostic), but the FIRST time the resumed scan needs to cross a sector boundary — via `_dir_next_sector`/`fat_get`, both BPB/FAT-cache-dependent — it consults E:'s own (wrong) BPB fields and lands on garbage that reads as end-of-directory, silently ending the scan. `DIR`'s own glob consumer never showed this: its per-match action (`K_STAT` on the match's own `c:/`-prefixed full path) incidentally reactivates C: as its own side effect every single time, right before the next `glob_next` call — `COPY` has no equivalent last step. Same class of bug as the earlier XCOPY cross-drive fix (2026-07-25), and structurally pre-existing in `glob_next`'s whole design (not introduced by the resumable rewrite specifically) — it just needed a cross-drive `COPY` test to surface, which hadn't been run before this round.
  - **Fix**: `glob_next` (`lib/file_glob.asm`) now reactivates the source drive at the very top of every call, before either the fresh-`K_DIR_OPEN` or `K_DIR_RESTORE_STATE` path — a `K_PATH_RESOLVE` call on `context.name_copy` (the original, unexpanded pattern text, which always retains any `X:` prefix verbatim from what `glob_init` copied in), purely for its `_switch_drive` side effect, exactly mirroring XCOPY's own established fix shape ("call something that goes through `path_resolve`, discard the result"). **`K_PATH_RESOLVE`, not `K_STAT`, deliberately** — `K_PATH_RESOLVE` never looks up the final path component, so it's safe to call on text whose final component is a glob pattern (`*.txt`), not a real filename, which `K_STAT` would simply fail to find. A failure here (e.g. the drive was removed mid-scan) is treated as exhausted, matching every other failure mode in this routine. Every register is re-derived fresh from `gn_ctx` (memory) immediately after this call, matching the file's own established discipline — nothing is trusted to survive it.
  - **Verification**: `lib/file_glob.asm` assembles clean (0 errors/warnings), D-clobber/self-overwrite sweeps clean, proc/endp balanced (5/5). Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, kernel byte-unchanged — pure userland/library fix, confirmed via unchanged `Highest address` `$427d`). All 6 glob-consuming binaries re-confirmed to link at `Lowest address: 4600` with correct `'EDF'` magic (re-checked specifically given this file's own gotcha #20 history — none found). **CONFIRMED FIXED ON HARDWARE (2026-07-27)** — user re-ran the exact failing cross-drive scenario plus a broader round across multiple drives, all clean; same-drive globs unaffected by the added `K_PATH_RESOLVE` call.
  - **This closes out the file_glob branch in full.** Final hardware coverage across the whole branch: `DIR`/`LS`/`COPY`/`ATTRIB` glob paths (same/cross-drive, multiple partitions), `MOVE`/`DEL` glob paths (confirmed 2026-07-27, same call shape as the already-tested programs), `big64test` on multiple partitions (confirms the `_fcb_seek_to` positioning-algorithm consolidation), and the resumable `glob_next` design generally (no more re-scan slowness, no duplicate/missing matches observed across any of the above). Branch merged into `main` (`236cb08`, fast-forward) and pushed to `origin/main` by the user.

- **Command-line editing (arrow keys + Emacs/readline Ctrl shortcuts) — IMPLEMENTED AND BUILD-VERIFIED 2026-07-27, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED. Pure userland (`progs/shell.asm`), zero kernel changes.** `read_line_with_history` (2026-07-22/23) already replaced `K_INPUTL` with a raw `f_uread`-based character loop handling Up/Down history recall via real ANSI/VT100 CSI (`ESC [ A`/`ESC [ B`) — but had NO cursor concept at all: typing always appended at the end, backspace always removed from the end. This adds real mid-line editing on top, confirmed via a full Plan Mode design pass. User-supplied specifics: Left/Right = `ESC [ D`/`ESC [ C` (same CSI family as the already-proven Up/Down); Ctrl-key mapping from a standard readline/bash reference (Ctrl-A=home, Ctrl-E=end, Ctrl-B=left, Ctrl-F=right, Ctrl-D=delete-under-cursor, Ctrl-H=backspace, same byte the routine already dispatched on); Ctrl-K/U/W/Y (kill/yank, need a cut buffer) explicitly deferred.
  - **New state**: `edit_cursor` (byte offset within `LINE_BUF`, 0..`hist_cur_len`). Reset to 0 at `read_line_with_history`'s own entry (alongside the existing `hist_cur_len=0` reset); set to `hist_cur_len` (end) inside `hist_redraw_linebuf` after any full-line replace (Up/Down recall, or Down's restore-original-line path) — matches standard bash/DOS behavior of landing the cursor at the end of a recalled line.
  - **Terminal primitives, both already hardware-proven, no new terminal-capability assumptions**: cursor-left is a bare backspace byte (0x08, non-destructive) — already relied on today as the first step of the old erase-sequence; cursor-right is reprinting the actual character via `K_TYPE` (the same mechanism as ordinary echo) — deliberately NOT using `ESC [ C` for *output*, since the terminal is only confirmed to *send* that sequence, not to correctly *interpret* one written back to it.
  - **Shared redraw helper `edit_redraw_tail(D=blank_count)`**: reprints `LINE_BUF[edit_cursor..hist_cur_len-1]`, prints `blank_count` trailing spaces (0 for insert, 1 for a single-character delete — every operation in this pass changes exactly one character), then backspaces `(tail_len+blank_count)` times back to `edit_cursor`. Covers insert, backspace, and Ctrl-D uniformly, including the degenerate at-end-of-line case (zero shift, zero blanks — reduces to the original single-character echo with no special-casing needed).
  - **Two shift-loop primitives** (`edit_insert_char`/`edit_delete_at`), each doing a byte-shuffle within `LINE_BUF`: insert shifts right starting from the end working backward (to avoid clobbering not-yet-moved bytes); delete shifts left starting from the hole working forward. **A real design bug was caught before ever writing assembly**, specifically because the first-pass Python simulation used a high-level `range()`-based model that doesn't reflect actual unsigned 8-bit register arithmetic: a naive pre-test-then-decrement insert loop breaks at `cursor==0`/`length==0` (the loop counter needs to signal "done" by going to -1, but as an unsigned byte it wraps to 255, and an unsigned comparison then wrongly treats 255 as "still ≥ 0", spinning up to 300 further iterations before a runaway-loop guard in the *simulation itself* caught it — not something the original abstract model could ever have revealed). Re-verified with a byte-accurate mechanical simulation (explicit 8-bit wraparound, executing the literal planned instruction sequence) across 900+ boundary/random cases before trusting the fix: a POST-test loop for insert (copy first, then check "was that the last needed copy," decrementing only if not) sidesteps the underflow entirely, since the counter never needs to go below the stop point; the delete loop's own pre-test shape was already safe as designed, since its counter only ever increases.
  - **A second real bug class, independent of the algorithm itself, caught by this project's own D-clobber sweep**: five separate instances of gotcha #4 (`mov` clobbers `D`) — in each case, the value to store was loaded into `D` (via `ldn`) *before* setting up the destination pointer (via `mov`), instead of after, so the eventual `str` silently wrote the destination-variable's own address low byte instead of the intended value. All five followed the identical wrong shape (`mov rf,SRC / ldn rf / mov rb,DST / str rb`) and were fixed identically (`mov rb,DST / mov rf,SRC / ldn rf / str rb`) — a reminder that even with the underlying algorithm independently verified correct, the mechanical D-clobber discipline still needs its own separate, careful pass; the automated sweep is what actually caught all five here, not manual re-reading.
  - **New byte dispatch** added to `rlwh_loop`'s existing `xri`/`lbz` chain (ESC/CR/LF/BS unchanged): Ctrl-A(1)/E(5)/B(2)/F(6)/D(4) routed to new handlers (`rlwh_home`/`rlwh_end`/`rlwh_left`/`rlwh_right`/`rlwh_delete_at`); Ctrl-K(11)/U(21)/W(23)/Y(25) explicitly matched and silently discarded (deferred, not inserted as literal control bytes); any other byte `< 32` also discarded — closes a small pre-existing gap where an unhandled control byte used to fall through and get inserted as invisible literal text. `rlwh_escape` extended with `'C'`→`rlwh_right`/`'D'`→`rlwh_left` alongside the existing `'A'`/`'B'`→history recall, sharing the exact same handler code as the Ctrl-B/F dispatch (reached via `lbz` from two different places, not duplicated). `rlwh_backspace` generalized from "always removes from the end" to `edit_delete_at(hole=edit_cursor-1)` + `edit_cursor--`, replacing its own old fixed 3-`K_TYPE`-call erase sequence (now handled by `edit_redraw_tail` inside `edit_delete_at`) — the leftover 2 of 3 old `K_TYPE` calls had to be explicitly removed after the initial edit, caught by rereading the file's actual resulting content rather than assuming the replacement was complete.
  - **Verification**: two-stage Python verification before writing assembly (see above — a naive high-level model, then a corrected byte-accurate mechanical one, 900+ cases, 0 content failures). `progs/shell.asm` assembles clean (0 errors/warnings, 2347 lines); D-clobber sweep caught and all 5 hits were fixed (see above), re-run clean afterward; self-overwrite sweep clean; no duplicate labels.
  - **A THIRD, more severe bug — a genuine design flaw, not a mechanical mistake — found on the very first hardware round: 100% of keystrokes produced no visible input at all ("I am unable to enter a single character"), even though the OS booted and prompted normally.** Root cause: `edit_redraw_tail` conflated two DIFFERENT positions into a single variable (`edit_cursor`) — where the tail print-loop should START (the point where visible content actually changed), and where the terminal cursor should END UP (the backspace target). These two positions only happen to coincide for a delete (the resting position after a delete IS where content changed). For an INSERT, they're different: `edit_insert_char` incremented `edit_cursor` to its final (post-insert) value *before* calling `edit_redraw_tail`, so the "tail" being printed started AT the already-advanced cursor — for the common case of typing at the end of a line, that tail is zero-length, so **every ordinary keystroke silently stored the character into `LINE_BUF` but echoed nothing at all**, exactly matching the reported symptom. A SECOND, related gap existed in the delete path too: `edit_redraw_tail`'s print loop assumes the terminal's own PHYSICAL cursor is already sitting at the print-start position when it begins — true for Ctrl-D (the cursor never moves) but NOT true for backspace, where the physical cursor starts one column to the right of the deletion point and needs an explicit backspace byte printed first to get there (exactly what the OLD, pre-redesign backspace code used to do as its own first step, which had been dropped during the redesign without realizing it served this real, necessary purpose beyond just being part of the old erase sequence).
  - **Fix**: added a new `ert_start` variable, set explicitly by each caller BEFORE calling `edit_redraw_tail`, distinct from `edit_cursor` (which now serves purely as the backspace target, always guaranteed to already hold its FINAL value by the time `edit_redraw_tail` runs). `edit_insert_char` captures `ert_start = edit_cursor` (the OLD, pre-increment value) before advancing the cursor. `rlwh_delete_at` (Ctrl-D) sets `ert_start = edit_cursor` unchanged (cursor never moves). `rlwh_backspace` computes `hole`, sets both `ert_start = hole` and `edit_cursor = hole` (its FINAL value) BEFORE calling `edit_delete_at`, and prints one explicit backspace byte first to physically reposition the terminal cursor — with the reload of `hole` for `edit_delete_at`'s own argument done fresh from memory afterward (`edit_cursor`, which already holds it), never trusted in a register across the intervening `K_TYPE` call.
  - **Re-verified via a full manual walkthrough of three concrete scenarios with real example data** before rebuilding (not just re-reading the code in the abstract): typing a character on an empty line (the exact reported failure — confirmed the character now echoes and the cursor lands correctly), backspace on a 3-character line with the cursor mid-line (confirmed the physical-cursor-repositioning backspace, the tail reprint, the blanking of the stale trailing character, and the final backspace-count all combine to leave the terminal showing the correct new content with the cursor in the correct column), and Ctrl-D under the same conditions (confirmed the no-extra-backspace case works since the cursor doesn't move). D-clobber/self-overwrite sweeps re-run clean after the fix, no duplicate labels. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings, kernel byte-unchanged — confirmed via unchanged `Highest address` `$427d`), `bin/shell` confirmed with correct `'EDF'` magic.

The command does not execute."** Extensive static re-reading found no definitive cause: `rlwh_loop`'s CR/LF dispatch is byte-for-byte unchanged from the original, already-hardware-confirmed `read_line_with_history` code and sits early in the dispatch chain (right after ESC, before any of this pass's new Ctrl-key checks), so it isn't being shadowed by anything new; `hist_append` doesn't depend on `hist_cur_len` at all (recomputes its own length via `shell_strlen`), ruling out a `hist_cur_len`/`edit_cursor` desync as a direct cause of a hang specifically in the history-write path; no data-declaration overlap was found between the new `edit_cursor`/`ert_*`/`eic_i`/`eda_i`/`rh_count`/`re_pos` fields and `hist_fcb`/`hist_iobuf`/`hist_tmp_fcb`'s own scratch; a full manual trace of `edit_insert_char` typing "dir" (3 sequential calls) confirmed `LINE_BUF`/`hist_cur_len`/`edit_cursor` end up correctly synchonized (`"dir\0"`, `len=3`, `cursor=3`). This forced abandoning "reason about the shell's own logic" and directly decoding the actual assembled/linked addresses instead of trusting the assembler.**
  - **⭐ ROOT CAUSE FOUND: a genuine, confirmed Asm/02 toolchain bug — a forward `lbz` reference to a label resolves to an address a constant, reproducible number of bytes BEFORE that label's own true position.** Decoded `progs/shell.lst` directly: both `lbz rlwh_finish` instructions (from the CR and LF checks, far earlier in the file) encoded target `5be7` — but `rlwh_finish`'s own real address (confirmed by the `ldi 'X'` instruction actually placed under that label) was `5bed`, six bytes later. `5be7` turned out to be exactly the address of `rlwh_down` — the label immediately preceding `rlwh_finish` in the source. **Renaming `rlwh_done`→`rlwh_finish` did NOT fix it** (ruling out any theory about the two names being confused with each other, e.g. a truncated/case-insensitive symbol comparison). **Reordering `rlwh_up`/`rlwh_down` so a DIFFERENT label became the immediate predecessor shifted the wrong resolution to THAT label's address instead** — ruling out anything specific to `rlwh_down`'s own name/content and confirming the bug tracks "whichever label happens to sit immediately before" in the source. **Adding a trivial, never-referenced dummy label between them broke the "exact predecessor address" pattern** (the wrong value became a mid-instruction, non-label address) but, when the actual byte math was worked out, **every single variant showed the wrong resolved value was consistently, exactly 6 bytes less than the label's own true address** — reproduced across four structurally different tests (original ordering, swapped ordering, an inert dummy label inserted, and even reducing `rlwh_finish` from two forward references down to just one, which ruled out "reference count" as the mechanism too). The true underlying mechanism inside Asm/02's C source was investigated at length (its `-r`-relaxation-aware branch-fixup machinery in `asm.c`'s `OT_LBR` case, the `usedLocal`/`usedReference` expression-evaluator tracking already implicated in gotcha #19, the `evaluate()`/`getLabel()`/`addLabel()` pass-1-vs-pass-2 label-value bookkeeping, and the `fixups[30000]` array's own size) but was **not conclusively root-caused** in the C source itself before this had to ship — the empirical "-6, always" pattern was reliable enough across enough independent structural variations to trust as a real, reproducible property rather than coincidence, even without a confirmed line-level explanation.
  - **Workaround shipped**: 6 bytes of genuinely harmless padding (`GHI R0` ×6 — a pure register-to-`D` read with no side effects on any register the real code depends on, since `rlwh_finish`'s own first real instruction, `LDI 'X'`, overwrites `D` immediately anyway) inserted immediately before the real `rlwh_finish:` label. The buggy jump target lands on the padding's own start and falls straight through into the real code. Verified directly against the built `.lst` (both from a standalone `asm02` invocation and from the real `make progs` build, which uses this project's actual `ASMFLAGS`/`LFLAGS` including `-r`): `lbz rlwh_finish` now resolves to `5bed`, exactly the padding's own start address, with the real label (`ldi 'X'`) sitting exactly 6 bytes later at `5bf3`.
  - **Explicitly documented as FRAGILE** (both inline in `shell.asm` and here): this offset was never proven to be a structural invariant independent of everything else in the file — it might be tied to something positional (total bytes/lines processed so far in this specific file) that could shift if enough OTHER code changes before this point in the file. **Any future edit anywhere before `rlwh_finish` in `shell.asm` needs to re-verify this specific resolution via the `.lst` file** (`grep -n "lbz     rlwh_finish" progs/shell.lst`, cross-checked against `rlwh_finish`'s own real address) before trusting a clean assemble — this is now a standing, permanent verification step for this specific file, not a one-time fix.
  - **Verified**: D-clobber sweep (same 21 pre-existing false positives, none in the new padding code), self-overwrite sweep and duplicate-label scan clean. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`; 31/9 binaries present), `bin/shell` grew 11272→11279 bytes (the 6-byte padding plus its own comment overhead).
  - **✅ SEVENTH HARDWARE ROUND (2026-07-27): CONFIRMED WORKING — the padding workaround fixes the bug.** `[S]`/`[BR df=1]`/per-keystroke bytes/`[CRFT]` all fired as expected, and — for the first time across this whole multi-round investigation — `[E]` and every downstream checkpoint (`[A] len=3`, `[B]`, `[C]`, `[D]`) all printed correctly, followed by a REAL, correct `dir` listing of the current directory. The user then typed `cd cfg`, which also worked cleanly (prompt changed to `C:/cfg>`, confirming the shell reload/command-execution path is fully intact, not just the read loop). This closes out the multi-round "Enter hangs" investigation — the root cause really was the Asm/02 forward-branch bug (gotcha #21), not any logic in this feature's own code.
  - **All temporary diagnostics removed (2026-07-27)**: `[S]`/`[BR ...]` (restored the original single `lbdf start_interactive`), `[A]`/`[B]`/`[C]`/`[D]`, the per-keystroke byte-value print, `[CRFT]`, the `'X'`/`K_TYPE` test, and `[E]` all stripped, along with their `diag_buf`/`diag_saved_rf`/`diag_saved_char`/`diag_batch_df` scratch fields — `grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC" progs/shell.asm` confirmed clean. `rlwh_finish:`'s body is back to a bare `rtn`, matching its original pre-diagnostic form. **The 6-byte `GHI R0` padding workaround was deliberately KEPT.** Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`; 31/9 binaries present), `bin/shell` shrank to 11025 bytes.

The earlier round-7 success only worked because it exercised plain typing + Enter, which only touches `rlwh_finish`'s own already-patched forward reference — none of the actual editing keys had been hardware-tested yet.
  - **A costly false lead investigated first**: tested whether a SINGLE padding block, inserted once at the point the drift begins (between `rlwh_end` and `rlwh_left`), would fix every subsequent label at once (a "one global miscount" theory) — decisively disproven: after adding one test pad, every downstream label was STILL exactly 6 bytes short of its own real address, individually. This ruled out a single cumulative drift and confirmed each affected label carries its own independent -6 error.
  - **A self-inflicted analysis error caught and corrected before shipping**: a first automated verification pass checked `real_address == referenced_address` and reported every padded label as "MISMATCH" — panic-inducing, but wrong test. The fix does NOT make the reference equal the real address; it makes the wrong jump target land exactly on the padding's own start (6 bytes before the real label), which then falls through correctly. Re-verified properly: for every affected label, confirmed the referenced (wrong) address exactly equals the padding block's own start, and every byte in the 6-byte gap up to the real label is genuinely `0x90` (`GHI R0`) — not just that the numbers happened to differ by 6.
  - **Fix**: 6 bytes of `GHI R0` padding added immediately before each of the 7 remaining affected labels (`rlwh_backspace`/`rlwh_left`/`rlwh_right`/`rlwh_delete_at`/`rlwh_escape`/`rlwh_up`/`rlwh_down`), matching the mechanism already proven for `rlwh_finish`. `rlwh_home`/`rlwh_end` deliberately left untouched (already correct; adding padding there would have broken them). Verified via a Python script parsing the real `.lst` directly: for all 8 padded labels, the wrong-jump target exactly matches the padding's own start address and every intervening byte is confirmed `0x90`; for `rlwh_home`/`rlwh_end`, the reference exactly matches the real address with zero gap. Re-confirmed against the actual `make progs`-built `shell.lst` (not just a standalone `asm02` test) — all 10 labels report correct.
  - **Verified**: D-clobber sweep (same 21 pre-existing false positives), self-overwrite sweep and duplicate-label scan clean, zero `TEMPORARY`/`DIAGNOSTIC` markers remaining. Full clean `make`+`make progs`+`make test` (0 errors/warnings; kernel `Highest address` unchanged at `$427d`; 31/9 binaries present), `bin/shell` now 11070 bytes (up from 11025 — the 7 new padding blocks plus their comments). **NOT yet hardware-tested** — this is the first build with every editing key's own forward reference independently verified correct, not just `rlwh_finish`'s. Needs a full round: Left/Right arrows, Ctrl-B/F, Home/End (Ctrl-A/E), mid-line insert, backspace/Ctrl-D at various positions (including at start/end where each should cleanly no-op), Ctrl-K/U/W/Y confirmed to do nothing, Up/Down history recall, long-line edge cases near the 127-char cap. **Any future edit anywhere in this dispatch chain (`rlwh_loop` through `rlwh_finish`) must re-run this same `.lst`-decode verification** — the bug's trigger condition was never root-caused, so there's no guarantee a new label added to this region won't need the identical treatment, or that an edit elsewhere won't shift which labels are affected.
  - **User's own explicit follow-up priority for next session: properly track down the Asm/02 root cause** ("If this is a real Asm/02 bug, we need to track it down. Who knows where it might bite us again.") — the 6-byte-padding workaround unblocks THIS file but does nothing to protect any other file in this project (or the ELFC compiler, which also uses Asm/02) from hitting the same silent miscompilation elsewhere. See gotcha #21 for everything already ruled out (name collision, label ordering, comment placement, reference count) — next step is either a more deliberate, larger synthetic repro (my own minimal hand-built test file did NOT reproduce it, so the trigger needs more scale/realism) or direct instrumentation of Asm/02 itself (temporary printf-style tracing in `addLabel`/`getLabel`/the `OT_LBR` case in `asm.c`) run against the real `shell.asm` to catch the exact moment the wrong value gets recorded.

## Working with the user

No hardware access from this environment — every real test is the user reflashing and pasting console output back. When diagnosing a hardware bug, prefer targeted temporary instrumentation (clearly marked `; TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC` / `; END TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC`) over guessing, and always remove it once the root cause is confirmed (verify with `grep -rn "TEMPORARY\|DIAGNOSTIC"` before calling a fix done). Ask the user for BIOS routine source when its exact register/side-effect contract matters and isn't already documented here — they have it and have provided it before (`f_strcpy`, `f_uintout`).
